# Foreword

I am sure all of us have read many medical textbooks. Most medical textbooks are organized by areas of specialty; for example, infectious diseases, cardiology, pulmonary medicine, and so on. This type of book organization allows the reader access to information on diseases and is most useful when we know the diagnosis and want to learn more. In many such books, the relevant differential diagnoses are often scattered throughout the text and difficult to find, which makes such textbooks less useful to the practicing physician who has yet to make the diagnosis on the basis of the symptoms the patient is presenting with.

I have yet to encounter a parent or a child who comes for the first time and states that "My son has nephrotic syndrome". Rather, what we hear is a complaint of a problem, such as "My son's feet and face are swollen".

The purpose of this book is to provide medical students, postgraduates in pediatrics, general practitioners and practicing pediatricians with a quick reference to the differential diagnoses for 50 of the most common problems that a child may present with to a pediatric or family medicine practice. The information is organized to reflect the way practice really happens. Each problem is defined clinically, followed by a brief discussion of pathophysiology and a guide to the history and physical examination that allows the generation of an appropriate and prioritized list of differential diagnoses. Also provided are the common questions that we, as clinicians, need to ask ourselves, with answers to these questions.

The authors are experts in their respective field, and each chapter has been critically reviewed and edited to make this book evidence-based, reliable and useful to clinical practice. I congratulate all the authors, reviewers and editors for their substantial efforts and contribution to the successful completion of this book.

Dr. Naveen Thacker

President

Asia Pacific Pediatric Association (APPA)

Gandhidham

2017

# Preface

The ability to link basic science with clinical decision making does not come naturally to most medical students and junior doctors. As educators, we saw the need to help medical students and doctors develop good clinical reasoning skills. Accordingly, we embarked on this book. Along the way, we were inspired by the book "Tutorials in Paediatric Differential Diagnosis" by Field et al, and guided by the contents of the article "How to improve the teaching of clinical reasoning: a narrative review and a proposal" by Henk G Schmidt & Silvia Mamede (Medical Education 2015: 49: 961–973. doi: 10.1111/medu.12775).

With this book, we hope to encourage medical students and doctors to realize that clear and analytical thinking is vital to good decision making, which ultimately benefits our patients—whether it is to identify a sick child or make accurate diagnoses and therapeutic decisions with as few extraneous investigations as possible.

We are deeply grateful to our teachers, colleagues, patients and students for helping us become better doctors, teachers and persons.

We thank the authors and reviewers for their effort and contribution to this book.

We give thanks to the God who gives us life, light and reason; and to our families.

This book is dedicated with much love and appreciation to Anthony T, Joseph, Marianne, Ho Wee, Susie, Diana and Anthony A.

Denise LM Goh
Elizabeth Y Ang

# Determining Health and Disease

Denise L.M. Goh

# HEALTH

Health, as defined by the WHO, is a state of complete physical, mental, and social well-being; it is not merely the absence of disease or infirmity. Hence, to achieve good health, one needs to promote health rather than just treat the existing disease. In order to promote health and reduce/ ameliorate disease, one needs to understand the factors determining health and disease and their interplay.

# HEALTH DETERMINANT FACTORS

The factors that affect a person's health status include the following.

- Physical environment

- Air quality (humidity, air pollution, oxygen content/pressure)
- Access to water, water quality (pollutants, infectious agents, chemicals, etc.)
- Access to food, quality of food, food safety (infectious agents, contaminants, etc.)
- Waste and sewerage management systems
- Natural disasters (typhoon, earthquake, fires, floods, etc.)
- Exposure to other sources of physical injuries (war, crime, violence, etc.)
- Exposure to other environmental toxins, e.g., mercury, lead
- Exposure to infectious agents (virus, bacterial, fungi, parasites)
- Housing
- Transport
- Work place (dangers, safety measures, etc.)

- Social economic environment

- Education level
- Job status
- Income level
- Quality of family/social support

- Governance policies

- All polices that affect physical environment, social economic environment, health care services

- Health care services and health care treatment

- Availability and quality of health care services
- Accessibility, affordability
- Usage of health care services, compliance to treatment and health promotion
- Benefits, limitations, side effects of treatment

1

• Aging

• A person's genetics

  o Gender
  o Effect of gene(s)
  o Gene–gene interaction(s)
  o Gene–environmental interaction(s)

• A person's life style choices

  o Diet
  o Exercise
  o Weight control
  o Alcohol, drugs
  o Hand hygiene
  o Stress control

# EFFECTS OF HEALTH DETERMINANT FACTORS

These factors can affect an individual at any stage of life:

• Embryo
• Fetus
• Neonate
• Child
• Adolescent
• Adult
• Elderly

These factors can result in these cellular effects:

• Growth, regeneration
• Repair
• Defense mechanism immunological reaction, inflammation
• Infection
• Neoplasm
• Metabolic/nutritional consequences
• Hypoxia-ischemia insult
• Trauma
• Toxicity or adverse effects of drugs, toxin, chemicals
• Degeneration

These cellular effects can affect the normal structure and function at any of these levels:

• The cell's ubiquitous structure and/or function
• The cell's specialized/differentiated structure and/or function
• The organ and/or organ system structure and function
• The whole body structure and/or function

These effects will have a net effect on the individual's

• Lifespan/mortality
• Quality of life (physical well-being, mental well-being, social well-being)

Which will manifest as an individual having

• Good health or

- Disease (which will lead the patient to present with symptoms or a problem) or
- Death

### INDIVIDUAL, FAMILY, SOCIETY, POPULATION

An individual is part of a

- Social unit (family unit, community, society)
- Population

Hence, the sum of each individual's healthy and diseased status is the state of the population's health status.

There are many markers of population health. Some of the commonly used markers are

- Lifespan
- Mortality rates
- Disease burden
- Quality of life (physical well-being, mental well-being, social well-being)

### PROMOTING HEALTH AND REDUCING/AMELIORATING DISEASE

- Interventions can be done at any or every of the above-mentioned points (and at the individual, social unit, and/or population level) (see diagram)

![img-2.jpeg](img-2.jpeg)

The sum of each individual's healthy and diseased status = state of the population's health status

- Lifespan
Mortality rates
Disease burden
- Quality of life (physical well-being, mental well-being, social well-being)

• The intervention can be

○ Primary prevention: Methods to prevent the occurrence of the disease. These are the health promotion activities, e.g., immunization, healthy eating, no smoking.
○ Secondary prevention/treatment: Methods to detect presymptomatic disease and to treat it so as to prevent or slow its progression to disability/disease state
○ Tertiary prevention: Methods to detect symptomatic disease and to treat it so as to maximize remaining functions and capabilities
○ Palliative care: A specialized health care that ministers to individuals with life-limiting conditions so as to provide relief from the symptoms and stress of a serious illness as well as improve quality of life for both the patient and the family.

# Abdominal Pain

Andrea W.C. Yeo • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF ABDOMINAL PAIN

- Abdominal pain refers to pain that is felt in the abdominal area (from below the ribs to above the pelvis; in the front of the body, not at the back).
- Abdominal pain can be acute or chronic.
- Chronic abdominal pain is defined as intermittent or constant abdominal pain lasting more than 2 months.

# SYMPTOMS OF ABDOMINAL PAIN

- Pain located in the abdominal region
- Pain can be generalized or localized
- Pain can be constant or colicky
- In younger children who are not able to vocalize or describe adequately, abdominal pain can present as fretfulness, crying, and/or curling up of the legs into the abdomen

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

Abdominal pain can be acute or chronic. The causes of acute and chronic abdominal pain can be different. The causes may be intra-abdominal or lie outside of the abdomen. It is also important to remember that the prevalence of the causes may also be age dependent.

# 1. Causes of Acute Abdominal Pain

- Surgical causes

- Gastrointestinal tract (GIT): appendicitis, Meckel diverticulitis, perforated viscus, malrotation with midgut volvulus, incarcerated inguinal hernia, adhesions with intestinal obstruction, intussusception, bowel ischemia/gangrene
- Intra-abdominal solid organ: laceration of liver, spleen, etc.
- Gynecological: ovarian torsion, ruptured ovarian cyst, ruptured ectopic pregnancy
- Others: testicular torsion

- Nonsurgical causes

- GIT: constipation, gastroenteritis (GE), gastritis, peptic ulcer disease, gastroesophageal reflux, lactose (or other food) intolerance, malabsorption, bowel ischemia, acute mesenteric ischemia, mesenteric adenitis, and primary bacterial peritonitis
- Hepatobiliary: hepatitis, acute cholecystitis, cholelithiasis, cholangitis, and liver abscess

7

- Pancreas: pancreatitis
- Urinary tract: urinary tract infection, urinary calculi, distended bladder, obstructive hydronephrosis
- Gynecological: dysmenorrhea, mittelschmerz, pelvic inflammatory disease, threatened abortion, ectopic pregnancy, labor, endometriosis
- Poisoning: lead, iron
- Others: diabetic ketoacidosis, Henoch–Schönlein purpura, pneumonia, sickle cell crisis, myocarditis, musculoskeletal pain, hypovolemic shock
- First episode of chronic abdominal pain

## 2. Causes of Chronic Abdominal Pain

- Organic causes

- GIT: peptic ulcer disease, gastritis, gastroesophageal reflux, inflammatory bowel disease, lactose (or other food) intolerance, malabsorption, celiac disease, secondary causes of constipation
- Pancreas: chronic pancreatitis
- Urinary tract: urinary tract infection, hydronephrosis, urinary calculi
- Hepatobiliary: chronic hepatitis, cholelithiasis, chronic cholecystitis
- Gynecological: dysmenorrhea, mittelschmerz, imperforate hymen with hematocolpos, pelvic inflammatory disease, endometriosis
- Others: lead poisoning, porphyria, tumors, malignancy

- Functional causes

- Functional abdominal pain (functional dyspepsia, abdominal migraine, irritable bowel syndrome, functional abdominal pain syndrome)
- Primary constipation and its related colic

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

### 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

- Check pulse

- Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
- Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

- When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

• If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

## 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

## 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

- History taking: Is the pain really in the abdomen? Is the abdominal pain acute or chronic?

  - Location of the pain
  - Duration of the pain: Abdominal pain may present acutely, or may present as an acute exacerbation of a more long-standing chronic pain.

- History taking: What is the nature of the pain?

  - Onset of pain
    - sudden onset
    - slow onset
  - Nature of the pain
    - constant, colicky, intermittent
    - severity
  - Radiation of pain

- History taking: What are the associated symptoms?

  - Always do a systems review.
  - Potential associated symptoms can include

    - Fever
    - Vomiting (bilious, blood)
    - Change in stools
      - Diarrhea
      - Constipation
      - No bowel output
      - Other stool abnormalities, e.g., bleeding from the lower GIT

    - Rash

    - Dysuria

    - Frequent urination

    - Loss of weight

    - Loss of appetite

    - Polydipsia

    - Polyphagia

    - Joint pain

    - Menstrual history

- Testicular swelling
- Trauma
- Jaundice
- Pallor

- Medication history
- Past medical history, chronic illness
- Travel history
- Contact history

- History taking: Assess for complications.

- Dehydration (thirst, decreased urine output, dizziness, lethargy, altered conscious state)
- Shock, low blood pressure (lethargy, altered conscious state, altered skin color)
- GIT bleeding (bloody vomitus, coffee grounds vomitus, black smelly stools, dark red stools, fresh blood per rectum)

- Do a thorough head to toe physical examination including growth and development.

![img-3.jpeg](img-3.jpeg)

4. Algorithm for Acute Abdominal Pain

• Is there any sign or symptom to suggest a surgical abdomen?

○ Severe abdominal pain may be a sign of a surgical abdomen.

○ Signs and symptoms of peritoneal irritation

- Severe abdominal pain, which may be localized or generalized
- Constant abdominal pain
- Associated with pain on movement or walking
- May be associated with referred pain to the neck or shoulder

○ Signs and symptoms of peritonitis

- Severe abdominal pain
- Generalized pain
- Constant pain
- Associated with pain on movement or walking
- Involuntary guarding, tenderness, rebound tenderness, decreased bowel sounds

○ Signs and symptoms of intestinal obstruction

- Cramping abdominal pain, vomiting that may be bilious, abdominal distension, increased bowel sounds, may have no bowel output.

- If it is likely a surgical abdomen, determine the likely cause.

○ Associated with peritoneal irritation and/or peritonitis

- Perforated viscus
- Ischemic bowel, with subsequent infarction/gangrene and perforation
- Blood in the peritoneum (ruptured liver, ruptured spleen, ruptured ovarian cyst, ruptured ectopic pregnancy)
- Inflamed bowel (acute appendicitis, Meckel diverticulitis)
- Pancreatitis

○ Associated with abdominal mass

- A sausage-shaped mass in the epigastrium and intermittent cramping abdominal pain with vomiting may suggest intussusception
- Enlarged liver: hemorrhage, hematoma, laceration, cholangitis, abscess
- Enlarged spleen: laceration, hemorrhage, hematoma
- Groin mass: hernia (which may be obstructed/strangulated)
- Testicular swelling: torsion
- Any other mass: torsion of ovarian cyst/mass, tumor, malignancy, gravid uterus, abscess

○ Associated with intestinal obstruction

- Intussusception
- Volvulus
- Incarcerated/obstructed hernia
- Adhesion causing intestinal obstruction

○ In a patient with previous abdominal surgery

- Intestinal obstruction in this case could be due to adhesions

○ Associated with trauma

- Consider laceration of the liver or spleen, bowel/viscus perforation, traumatic pancreatitis

• In a sexually active female

- Is her menstrual cycle delayed? Has she tested positive on a urine pregnancy test?
- Is there any vaginal bleeding? Is she hemodynamically stable? Consider ruptured ectopic pregnancy, especially if hemodynamically unstable.
- Note that it can be any of the above causes too (i.e., not just related to the female anatomy).

• If it is not a surgical cause and the acute abdominal pain is in the upper abdomen, determine the likely cause.

• Where is the exact location of the pain? Is there any radiation of the pain? Is there any change with posture?

- An intense epigastric or periumbilical pain radiating to the back or scapula may suggest pancreatitis.
- Epigastric pain can also be caused by gastritis or peptic ulcer disease, and may be associated with nausea, vomiting, hematemesis, or melena.
- Pain in the right upper quadrant may suggest hepatitis, cholangitis, cholelithiasis, acute cholecystitis, or liver abscess. It may be accompanied by jaundice.
- Pain in the left upper quadrant may suggest gastritis or a splenic cause.

• Associated with jaundice

- This would suggest a hepatobiliary cause for the abdominal pain, e.g., hepatitis, cholangitis, cholelithiasis, acute cholecystitis, liver abscess, or pancreatitis.

• Associated with fever and cough, or any abnormal lung findings

- This is suggestive of pneumonia.

• Associated with signs of heart failure (respiratory distress, tachycardia/bradycardia, weak pulses, hypotension, hepatomegaly)

- This suggests myocarditis. Patient may also present with other nonspecific symptoms such as vomiting or loss of appetite. There are usually some prodromal symptoms like fever, malaise, and/or myalgia.

• Is there any history of liver disease or other systemic disorders such as hemoglobinopathies, hemolytic disease, diabetes mellitus, or cystic fibrosis?

- These may predispose to pancreatitis or cholelithiasis/cholecystitis.

• Was there any recent intake of drugs or use of total parenteral nutrition?

- Drugs can cause abdominal pain, either as a side effect (e.g., antibiotics) or as a result of complications (e.g., Non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs [NSAIDs] causing gastritis).
- Overdose of iron can cause abdominal pain associated with vomiting, diarrhea, bleeding GIT, hypotension, altered mental status, and seizures.
- Total parenteral nutrition can cause cholecystitis or cholelithiasis.

○ Remember to re-evaluate periodically, as this type of abdominal pain may be a surgical abdomen in evolution.

• If it is not a surgical cause and the acute abdominal pain is periumbilical, determine the likely cause.

○ Vomiting with fever may suggest a gastroenteritis or food poisoning.

○ Diarrhea may suggest gastroenteritis, food poisoning, or inflammatory bowel disease.

○ History of travel to another country may suggest a gastroenteritis or food poisoning.

○ Contact history with anyone with similar symptoms may suggest a gastroenteritis or food poisoning.

○ Blood in the stools is seen in bleeding upper GIT, gastroenteritis, Meckel diverticulitis, gut ischemia, intussusception.

○ Hard, infrequent stooling may suggest constipation.

○ Abdominal pain that is associated with constipation and relieved by passing motion is suggestive of constipation colic.

○ Symptoms of diarrhea, flatulence, bloating that are related to the ingestion of a particular food are suggestive of a food intolerance, e.g., lactose intolerance, malabsorption, irritable bowel syndrome.

○ Any migration of the pain from its initial periumbilical site to the right iliac fossa is suggestive of acute appendicitis.

○ Abdominal pain associated with signs and symptoms of an upper respiratory tract infection suggests mesenteric adenitis.

○ Purpuric rashes over the lower limbs and any joint pain or swelling is suggestive of Henoch–Schönlein purpura.

○ If there is a history of nephrotic syndrome or chronic liver disease with ascites, the presence of abdominal pain may suggest primary bacterial peritonitis or hypovolemic shock.

○ Associated with trauma: Consider laceration of the liver or spleen, bowel/viscus perforation, or traumatic pancreatitis.

○ Associated with polydipsia, polyuria, polyphagia, loss of weight: This is suggestive of diabetic ketoacidosis.

○ Associated with severe dehydration, blood loss, or hypovolemia: This may cause acute mesenteric ischemia.

○ Remember to re-evaluate periodically, as this type of abdominal pain may be a surgical abdomen in evolution.

• If it is not a surgical cause and the acute abdominal pain is in the lower abdomen, determine the likely cause.

○ Pain localized to the right lower quadrant may point to an appendicitis or mittelschmerz. Rarely, Meckel diverticulitis or ovarian torsion can present with similar symptoms.

○ Pain in the left lower quadrant may be related to the ovary (e.g., torsion and mittelschmerz) or the colon (e.g., constipation).

o Pain localized to the suprapubic area is related to the bladder or the uterus.

- Symptoms of dysuria, frequency, and tenderness over the pubic area suggest cystitis.
- Symptoms of inability to urinate or incomplete emptying of bladder suggest bladder distension.
- In an adolescent female who is sexually active, consider pregnancy or threatened abortion.
- In a sexually active female with vaginal discharge, consider pelvic inflammatory disease.

o Pain is localized in the flank: This is pain related to the kidneys.

- Fever, urinary symptoms, and flank pain suggest a pyelonephritis.
- Pain with radiation from the flank to the groin or pain with hematuria suggests urinary stones.
- Persistent flank pain may suggest obstruction causing hydronephrosis.

o Remember to re-evaluate periodically, as this type of abdominal pain may be a surgical abdomen in evolution.

![img-4.jpeg](img-4.jpeg)

5. Algorithm for Chronic Abdominal Pain

- If the abdominal pain is chronic, is it related to the menstrual cycle?

○ Has the female patient started menstruating?

■ If not, and patient has other signs of puberty, examine the perineum and consider imperforate hymen with hematocolpos.

○ Which part of the menstrual cycle is the pain related to?

■ Pain in the first 2 days of the cycle is likely physiological menstrual cramps.
■ Severe progressive dysmenorrhea that lasts more than 2 days may suggest endometriosis.
■ Midcycle pain = mittelschmerz.

- If the chronic abdominal pain is not related to the menstrual cycle, determine the likely cause

○ Where is the location of the pain?

■ Epigastric pain may suggest peptic ulcer disease or gastritis.
■ Epigastric pain with radiation of the pain to the retrosternal region may suggest gastroesophageal reflux.
■ Epigastric pain that radiates to the back and associated with nausea and vomiting may suggest chronic pancreatitis. This pain usually improves on leaning forward.
■ Right upper quadrant pain suggests a hepatobiliary cause.
■ Left upper quadrant pain suggests gastric pain. A large spleen can sometimes also cause discomfort there.
■ Left lower quadrant pain and a history of infrequent defecation and passing of hard stool may suggest constipation. Other causes of left lower quadrant pain include ulcerative colitis or an ovarian mass or cyst.
■ Right lower quadrant pain may suggest an ovarian cyst or mass or a chronic appendicitis.
■ Suprapubic pain suggests a bladder- or uterus-related cause.
■ Periumbilical pain is usually colicky pain. The possible causes are lactose or other food intolerances, malabsorption, inflammatory bowel disease, celiac disease, porphyria, lead poisoning, tumors, and malignancy.
■ Flank pain may suggest hydronephrosis, renal tumour, urinary calculi.

○ Is there any alarming symptom or sign?

■ Weight loss or poor growth: These may suggest malignancy, inflammatory bowel disease, or some form of malabsorption (e.g., from celiac disease or chronic pancreatitis).
■ Chronic diarrhea with blood or mucus in the stool and peri-anal abnormalities may suggest inflammatory bowel disease. Other causes of chronic diarrhea and abdominal pain include irritable bowel syndrome, celiac disease, lactose (or other food) intolerance, and food protein-induced enteropathy.
■ Significant vomiting: This is significant but does not help to narrow down the differential diagnosis as it is associated with many organic causes.

- Unexplained fevers: This may suggest an ongoing infectious or inflammatory or malignant process.
- Dermatitis herpetiformis-like rash or recurrent aphthous ulcers. This may suggest celiac disease.
- Urinary symptoms like hematuria, dysuria, urgency, or flank pain suggest recurrent urinary infections or nephrolithiasis.
- The presence of an abdominal mass: The most common cause is a fecal mass. Other major causes include solid tumors such as Wilms tumor, neuroblastoma, and ovarian tumor.
- Severe episodic abdominal pain that is associated with recurrent acute pain and swelling in the hands or feet: Consider sickle cell crisis.
- Severe episodic abdominal pain that is associated with nausea, vomiting, pallor, or headaches: Consider abdominal migraine.
- Severe abdominal pain that is episodic, associated with constipation, vomiting or signs of ileus, and peripheral neuropathy: Consider acute porphyria.
- Associated with developmental delay, learning problems, or behavioral issues: Consider chronic lead poisoning.
- Positive family history of inflammatory bowel disease or celiac disease: If present, these diseases need to be excluded.

- A normal physical examination and the absence of alarming signs and symptoms make the diagnosis of functional abdominal pain most likely. Investigations may be needed to rule out other causes before diagnosing functional abdominal pain.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Acute abdominal pain

- Severe abdominal pain
- Signs and symptoms of sepsis
- Signs and symptoms of shock
- Signs and symptoms of peritoneal irritation or peritonitis
- Signs and symptoms of intestinal obstruction
- Bilious vomiting
- Bloody vomitus

- Chronic abdominal pain

- Significant weight loss or poor growth (may indicate a malignancy, a chronic inflammatory condition, or malabsorption)
- Abdominal mass
- Pain that wakes a child up at night
- Pain that is not central/periumbilical

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well, 9-year-old girl presents to you for the problem of abdominal pain. She has no significant medical history of note. She has been growing and developing appropriately.

Her abdominal pain started in the peri-umbilical region 2 days ago. This has worsened over the last 24 hours, and she now has difficulty walking due to the pain. This is associated with fever, and a few episodes of vomiting and diarrhea over the last 24 hours. The vomitus is not bilious or bloody, and there is no blood in her stool. She has some loss of appetite over the last 2 days.

She has not started menstruating yet. There is no skin rash or joint pains. There is no painful urination or foul-smelling urine. There is no history of trauma. The remaining review of systems is negative. There is no travel history or contact with persons with similar complaints.

On examination, her body temperature is 38.3°C, heart rate 130/min, respiratory rate 21/min, blood pressure 92/57 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 98%.

Her height is on the 75th percentile. Her weight is on the 50th percentile. She is not pale or jaundiced.

Heart rate is regular. On auscultation, S1 and S2 are heard. There is no additional heart sounds. On auscultation of the lungs, there is vesicular breath sounds. No crackles or wheezing are heard.

The abdomen is guarded, with maximal tenderness over the right lower quadrant. Rebound tenderness is present. There is no mass felt. No organomegaly is noted.

Her peripheries are warm. Capillary refill is less than 2 seconds. She is moderately dehydrated. Musculoskeletal examination is normal. Neurological exam is normal.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Acute abdominal pain

- What is your diagnosis?

- A surgical cause; most likely an acute appendicitis with perforation.

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Presence of abdominal pain, associated with fever, vomiting, and anorexia.
- Worsening of the abdominal pain with time
- Acute illness (good health previously, no significant medical history, good growth, and normal development)
- Right iliac fossa tenderness on palpation
- Symptoms and signs of peritonitis (guarding and rebound tenderness)

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Presence of diarrhea (though it can be seen in some cases of acute peritonitis)

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No history of migration of pain from the peri-umbilical region to the right iliac fossa or right lower quadrant
- No information about the bowel sounds

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Ruptured right ovarian cyst

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Acute pain
- Localized to the right
- Signs of peritoneal irritation

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Pain starting in the umbilicus
- Fever, vomiting, and diarrhea

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- None

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Acute abdominal pain due to peritonitis; most likely a perforated appendicitis

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Symptoms and signs of peritonitis
- Moderately dehydrated

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Keep nil by mouth.
- Conduct full blood count (FBC) and C-reactive protein (CRP) tests. The white cell count (WBC) and CRP are typically elevated in an acute appendicitis.
- Do urea, electrolytes, and creatinine to determine the renal function and look for complications of the poor oral intake such as dehydration, acute kidney impairment, and electrolyte abnormalities.
- Carry out blood cultures and start intravenous antibiotics.
- Conduct urine microscopy to exclude a urinary tract infection.
- Start intravenous fluid rehydration.
- Consider urgent referral to a pediatric surgeon.
- Monitor vital signs closely.

# Abnormal Stature (Short, Tall)

Loke Kah Yin

# DEFINITION OF SHORT STATURE

A short child is defined as any child whose height is less than the 3rd percentile for his/her community. This approximates 2 standard deviations (2 SD) below the mean for the respective age and sex of the normal population.

# SYMPTOMS OF SHORT STATURE

- Height is perceived to be shorter than peers and/or family members.
- Height is less than the 3rd percentile for his/her community.
- Height is less than the 3rd percentile on the growth chart.

# Significance

- Short stature and suboptimal growth may be the first sign of a chronic disease. Early detection and early treatment is important.
- Short stature may result in psychological issues, because short children may be reminded of their nonconformity and they may be bullied, teased, or excluded from activities.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Pathophysiology

- Normal bone growth is

  - Determined by familial genetics
  - Determined by the biological clock

- Abnormal bone growth can be due to

  - Primary bone pathology

    - Skeletal dysplasia

  - Chronic illness that affects the bones secondarily

    - Prenatal

      - Causes of intrauterine growth retardation
      - Chromosomal abnormalities
      - Other genetic disorders

    - Postnatal

      - Renal disorder
      - Endocrine disorder
      - Gastrointestinal disorder
      - Cardiopulmonary disorder
      - Hematological disorder
      - Nutritional deficiency (e.g., rickets, lack of calories)

21

## 2. The Causes for Short Stature Can Thus Be Divided into Two Categories:

- Normal variant short stature

- Familial short stature
- Constitutional growth delay

- Pathological short stature

- Disproportionate short stature

- Rickets
- Skeletal dysplasia

- Proportionate short stature

- Prenatal

- Causes of intrauterine growth retardation (inadequate fetal nutrition, intrauterine infections, drugs, toxins)
- Chromosomal abnormalities
- Other genetic disorders

- Postnatal

- Renal disorder (chronic kidney disease, renal tubular acidosis, etc.)
- Endocrine disorder (hypothyroidism, growth hormone deficiency, hypopituitarism, Cushing syndrome, etc.)
- Gastrointestinal disorder (maldigestion, malabsorption, inflammatory bowel disease, etc.)
- Cardiopulmonary disorder (congestive cardiac failure, chronic respiratory disease, bronchiectasis, etc.)
- Hematological disorder (different causes of chronic anemia, etc.)
- Nutritional deficiency (lack of calories, macronutrient deficiency, micronutrient deficiency, etc.)

## 3. Information about Conditions

- Familial short stature (genetic short stature)

- Family history of short stature
- Child's height falls within the target range based on the parental heights.
- Birth size (height and weight) is on the lower range of normal, and the child will grow near or below the 3rd percentile by 1 year of life.
- Puberty occurs at the normal time, and the child will achieve a final height that is short in relation to the population, but normal for the genetic potential of the family.
- Bone age (based on an X-ray of the left hand) will correspond to the chronological age and will not be significantly delayed.

- Constitutional growth delay (maturational delay)

- Family history of delayed puberty, e.g.:

- Mothers did not have any breast development by 13 years of age.
- Fathers recalling that their voice broke later than their peers.

- Mothers and fathers recalling that they were short when their friends were maturing.
- Mothers and fathers experienced their growth spurt only in their mid- to late-teenage years.

- Normal birth size, but height velocity will slow down until 1 year of age when the height becomes less than the 3rd percentile.
- After 1 year of age, the height velocity is normal (≥4.0 cm/year) and the patient's height growth will parallel the 3rd percentile.
- Puberty and bone age are delayed.
- Final adult height (at maturity) and sexual development are normal.

# • Pathological disproportionate short stature

- Any child with disproportionate short stature has rickets or skeletal dysplasia.
- Diseases that primarily affect the spine result in the short trunk dwarf with a typically reduced US/LS ratio. (US/LS is the upper segment-to-lower segment ratio.)
- Diseases that primarily affect the limb growth (including rickets) may result in an increased US/LS ratio.
- For example, a 10-year-old child with spondyloepiphyseal dysplasia (which primarily affects the spine) may have a reduced US/LS ratio of 0.7, whereas a child with achondroplasia (which primarily affects the limbs) may have an increased US/LS ratio of 1.3.

# • Pathological proportionate short stature

- Proportionate short stature can be due to any systemic disease of significance.
- The causes of proportionate short stature can generally be subdivided into prenatal and postnatal causes, based on the birth weight.
- Short child with low birth weight (<2.5 kg) could have a prenatal etiology.
- Short child with normal birth weight (>2.5 kg) but demonstrating suboptimal growth after birth is likely to suffer from a postnatal systemic disease. For example, malabsorption results in insufficient calories for normal growth; with cardiac failure, the extra workload on the heart and lungs means that there are less calories for growth.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. What Is the Child's Height Compared to the Normal Population?

- The assessment of a child's height is first based on comparison with norms for the age and sex of the specific population.
- Because height is a continuous variable that is normally distributed, height percentiles can be used to describe a child's height.
- The lowest and highest percentiles of a normal accepted height generally correspond to the 3rd and 97th percentiles, respectively.
- Plot all serial height data and look for the trend.

## 2. What Are the Child's Body Segment Ratios Like?

- The US/LS ratios of a child can provide a clue to the diagnosis.
- Upper segment: It is measured from the pubic symphysis to the vertex of the head; it indirectly assesses the trunk growth. It can be derived from subtracting the lower segment from the height measurement.
- Lower segment: It is measured from the pubic symphysis to the sole of the foot; it indirectly assesses the limb growth.
- Normal infants are born with relatively short legs, so the US/LS ratio is 1.7:1. As the child grows older, the legs lengthen more, relative to the spine and the ratio becomes 1:1 by 7 years of age.

## 3. Determine whether the Child Is "Short and Proportionate" or "Short and Disproportionate"

- Look at the US/LS ratio and compare it to the norms.
- If the US/LS ratio is normal, this child is proportionate and short. See point 4 below.
- If the US/LS ratio is not normal, this child is disproportionate and short. See point 5 below.

## 4. If the Child Is Short and Proportionate

- Is the child short for his/her genetics?

- The child's height must also be assessed in relation to the genetic potential (target height range) based on the parental heights.
- Target height range provides the 10th–90th percentiles of the genetic potential.
- Target height range formulas is based on mid-parental height (MPH)

  - For boys

    - The adjusted MPH = (father's height + [mother's height + 13])/2.
    - Target range = MPH ± 7.5 cm.

  - For girls

    - The adjusted MPH = (father's height + [mother's height - 13])/2.
    - Target range = MPH ± 5.5 cm.

- To use the target height:

  - Plot the adjusted MPH and target range at 18 years of age on the height for age curve.
  - Extrapolate the target range back to the child's age: if the child's height falls within the target range, the child is within his/her genetic potential. Conversely, if the child's height falls outside of the target range, the child is either shorter or taller than his/her genetic potential.

- Please note that the formula shown above for the target height range calculation is the generic formula. Some countries have their own specific formula for calculating the target height range. Please refer to your country's or population's formula if there is one.

- Is there any other explanation apart from his/her genetics?

- Ask for all serial weight data as well as head circumference data and plot them to look at the trend.
- Ask for associated symptoms.
- Ask about the antenatal history (maternal health, exposures, and fetal growth history).
- Take note of any newborn screening results.
- Take a history of when parents' puberty occurred.
- Do a systemic review including developmental history.
- Do a head-to-toe examination to look for any constitutional disease state.
- The child who is proportionately short and whose weight percentile is more than the height percentile may have an endocrinopathy such as growth hormone deficiency, hypothyroidism, or Cushing syndrome.
- Headache and vomiting after sleeping may suggest increased intracranial pressure from a suprasellar tumor, which causes short stature because of hypopituitarism.
- Abdominal pain or diarrhea may indicate inflammatory bowel disease.
- Lethargy and poor weight gain with no other overt clinical manifestation can occur in chronic kidney disease and renal tubular acidosis.

# 5. If the Child Is Short and Disproportionate

- Look at the family history.
- Ask for all serial weight data as well as head circumference data and plot them to look at the trend.
- Ask for associated symptoms.
- Ask about risk factors and symptoms of rickets.
- Ask about risk factors and symptoms of skeletal dysplasia.
- Do a systems review.
- Do a head to toe examination. Look for signs of rickets and skeletal dysplasia.

6. Algorithm

![img-5.jpeg](img-5.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- A child with suboptimal height velocity (<4.0 cm/year in mid-childhood).
- Headache and vomiting after sleeping: this is suggestive of increased intracranial pressure (e.g., a suprasellar tumor, which causes short stature because of hypopituitarism).

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO 1

A 7-year-old boy is brought to the outpatient clinic by his parents who are concerned that he is shorter than his friends at school. He has no other complaints and has not suffered from any serious illnesses. He has recently been teased at school because he is short. He was born full term by a normal delivery with a birth weight of 3.0 kg. On reviewing his previous height chart, his height has always been marginally below the 3rd percentile. His current height measures 108.0 cm (just below the 3rd percentile) and his weight is 15.0 kg (also just below the 3rd percentile). His father is 165.5 cm

and his mother is 158.2 cm tall. The lower limit of the target height range corresponds with the 3rd percentile on the height chart. His father does not recall that he was short when he was young. The mother experienced her menarche at the age of 17 years.

On clinical examination, he is not dysmorphic. He is proportionately short with an US/LS ratio of 1.0. His BP is 90/65 mmHg with a pulse rate of 80/min. He is euthyroid and the rest of the physical examination is normal.

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Short stature because his height is less than the 3rd percentile for his population

- What is your diagnosis?

- Constitutional growth delay

- List the findings that support this diagnosis.

- He is proportionately short.
- His height is only just below the 3rd percentile. The closer the child's height is to the 3rd percentile, the less likely it is to be pathological. The converse is also true that the further the child's height deviates from the 3rd percentile, the more likely it is to be pathological.
- The lower limit of the target range corresponds to the 3rd percentile, and he is at the lower limit of his genetic potential.
- His birth weight was normal.
- His mother had a history of delayed menarche.
- He was otherwise well and the physical examination was normal.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

○ A full systemic review to ensure that important symptoms that may not be volunteered are detected. These are the red flag symptoms, which include headache, vomiting, abdominal pain, intermittent diarrhea, lethargy, and poor weight gain.

○ His bone age would be delayed.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

○ Familial short stature

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

○ List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

■ He is at the lower limit of the target height range.

○ List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

■ His mother had delayed menarche.

○ If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

■ His bone age would be normal in familial short stature.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Constitutional growth delay

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ The boy is being teased in school for his short stature.

- What is your plan for this patient?

○ X-ray left hand for bone age: If this is constitutional growth delay, the bone age will be significantly delayed by more than 2 years.

○ If the systemic review shows that he is well, no other tests are necessary.

○ If the systemic review shows that he is unwell or if there is any red flag in the history or physical abnormalities detected, consider checking the serum urea, creatinine and electrolyte levels, free thyroxine with TSH, and basal IGF-1 level (an indirect assessment of the growth hormone status).

○ Assuming that his bone age is delayed, and he is otherwise well, the diagnosis would be constitutional growth delay. For constitutional growth delay, the treatment is to reassure and counsel both parents and child that the latter is normal. Puberty may occur late but when he does enter puberty, he will experience a growth spurt and reach an adult height that is normal for the family genetics. We will have to help him cope with the teasing at school.

○ However, even in constitutional growth delay, the child must initially be monitored for 6 months to 1 year. A normal boy should grow at least 2 cm in 6 months or 4 cm in 1 year. If this growth is not achieved, he needs to be re-evaluated for the cause of his short stature.

### DEFINITION OF TALL STATURE

A tall child is defined as any child whose height is more than the 97th percentile for his/her community. This approximates 2 standard deviations (2 SD) above the mean for the respective age and sex of the normal population. The assessment follows the same steps as outlined above for short stature.

# SYMPTOMS OF TALL STATURE

- Height is perceived to be taller than peers and/or family members.
- Height is more than 97th percentile for his/her community.
- Height is more than the 97th percentile on the growth chart.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Pathophysiology

- Normal bone growth

- Determined by familial genetics (familial tall stature)
- Determined by nutrition (preadolescent exogenous obesity)

- Abnormal bone growth

- Genetic diseases
- Endocrine diseases

# 2. Causes

- Normal variant tall stature

- Familial tall stature.
- Preadolescent exogenous obesity: Obese children tend to mature earlier and are tall compared to peers of the same age.

- Pathological tall stature

- Genetic diseases:

- Klinefelter syndrome (47, XYY), Marfan syndrome, Soto syndrome, Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome, etc.

- Endocrine diseases:

- Growth hormone excess (pituitary giant)
- Precocious puberty (early growth spurt)
- Delayed puberty (no sex hormones to fuse the bones)

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. What is the Child's Height Compared to the Normal Population?

- The assessment of a child's height is first based on comparison with norms for the age and sex of the specific population.
- Because height is a continuous variable that is normally distributed, height percentiles can be used to describe a child's height.
- The lowest and highest percentiles of normal accepted height generally correspond to the 3rd and 97th percentiles, respectively.
- Plot all serial height data and look for the trend.

# 2. Is the Child Tall for His Genetics?

- The child's height must also be assessed in relation to the genetic potential (target height range) based on the parental heights. Target height range provides the 10th-90th percentiles of the genetic potential.

• Target height range formulas is based on mid-parental height (MPH)

o For boys

▪ The adjusted MPH = (father's height + [mother's height + 13])/2.
▪ Target range = MPH ± 7.5 cm.

o For girls

▪ The adjusted MPH = (father's height + [mother's height - 13])/2.
▪ Target range = MPH ± 5.5 cm.

• To use the target height:

o Plot the adjusted MPH and target range at 18 years of age on the height for age curve.
o Extrapolate the target range back to the child's age: if the child's height falls within the target range, the child is within his/her genetic potential. Conversely, if the child's height falls outside of the target range, the child is either shorter or taller than his/her genetic potential.

• Please note that the formula shown above for the target height range calculation is the generic formula. Some countries have their own specific formula for calculating the target height range. Please refer to your country's or population's formula if there is one.

### 3. Is There Any Other Explanation Apart from His Genetics?

• Ask for associated symptoms.
• Ask about the antenatal growth history.
• Ask for all serial weight data as well as head circumference data and plot them and look for the trend.
• Take a history of when parents' puberty occurred.
• Take a diet history.
• Take a developmental history.
• Do as systemic review.
• Do a head-to-toe examination to look for the constitutional disease state.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Headache and vomiting after sleeping: this is suggestive of increased intracranial pressure (e.g., a suprasellar tumor, which causes tall stature because of growth hormone excess or precocious puberty).

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO 2

A 15-year-old boy is seen at the clinic for tall stature. He has been tall since he was in primary school. He is otherwise asymptomatic. He has been achieving only a borderline pass in his tests and is reportedly withdrawn at school, tending to keep to himself. His father is 165.0 cm and his mother is 163.0 cm. His height of 182.0 cm is well above the upper limit of his target range. On clinical examination, he was not found dysmorphic. His US/LS ratio is 1.0. There is no ligamentous laxity or scoliosis. He has acne, with facial hair, and Tanner stage 4 pubic hair. His testes measured 4 mL each and were soft in consistency. The rest of the physical examination was normal.
