# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Tall stature

- What is your diagnosis?

- Klinefelter syndrome

- List the findings that support this diagnosis.

- He is proportionately tall and taller than his genetic potential (height above the upper limit of his target range).
- He is not doing well academically in school.
- He is withdrawn at school and does not mix well with peers.
- His testes volume is smaller than expected for the degree of virilization. A testicular volume of 4 mL indicates early puberty only. In Klinefelter syndrome, there may be adequate testosterone production initially, but the germ cells that make up the majority of the testicular volume are deficient. Hence, the testes in Klinefelter syndrome appear smaller and softer than expected for the stage of puberty.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- A full systemic review is done to ensure that important symptoms (which may not be volunteered) are detected.
- There is no mention of whether there is gynecomastia or gynecoid habitus.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Another genetic condition might be present

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- He has many features that are not normal.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- His abnormal features and behavior might not be related to the condition that causes his tall height.

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

■ Not sure. This is way beyond my knowledge.

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Klinefelter syndrome

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ None

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Chromosomal karyotype to confirm the diagnosis of Klinefelter syndrome.

○ Basal serum gonadotropins (LH, FSH) and testosterone level. In Klinefelter syndrome, an elevated FSH would indicate germ cell dysfunction. An elevated LH and low testosterone level would indicate primary hypogonadism, which would require male hormone replacement therapy. At initial presentation, many teenagers with Klinefelter syndrome have adequate virilization in early puberty, although they may experience pubertal arrest later.

○ Referral to an endocrine and genetic specialist.

○ Family and patient will need the following:

■ Counseling regarding Klinefelter syndrome.
■ Evaluation to see if male hormone replacement therapy is needed.
■ Discussion on fertility issues and options.

# Abnormal Urine Dipstick Test

Perry Y.W. Lau

# DEFINITION OF ABNORMAL URINE DIPSTICK TEST

The urine dipstick test is a quick test that can alert us to the presence of systemic diseases or diseases affecting the kidneys and the urinary tract. This test can provide results in a few seconds, is simple to use, and has a low cost.

The urine dipstick test incorporates various chromogens that can cause chemical reaction with various chemicals in the urine to cause color change in the chromogens.

Components of urinalysis done by a urine dipstick usually include the following (normal values are shown in brackets):

- urine specific gravity (1.003–1.030 depending on the person's hydration status)
- urine pH (5–8 depending on the person's acid–base balance)
- blood (negative)
- protein (negative)
- leukocyte esterase (negative)
- nitrite (negative)
- glucose (negative)
- ketone (negative)
- bilirubin (negative)
- urobilinogen (negative)

An abnormal urine dipstick test result is one that is outside the normal value. An abnormal urine dipstick test needs be followed up by in-depth/confirmatory testing as false-positive and false-negative results are common in this test.

# SYMPTOMS ASSOCIATED WITH INCREASED RISK FOR AN ABNORMAL URINE DIPSTICK TEST

The urine dipstick test has been used as a screening test in the following symptoms and diseases:

- Fever, dysuria, or cloudy urine: to screen for urinary tract infection (UTI)
- Dark urine (red, pink, or brown): to screen for hemoglobinuria or hematuria
- Bubbly urine: to screen for proteinuria
- Swelling, edema, or other signs suggestive of fluid retention in the body: to screen for nephritic or nephrotic syndrome

34

- Hypertension: to screen for renal causes
- Frequency, polyuria: to screen for diabetes mellitus, chronic kidney disease, UTI
- Symptoms suspicious of autoimmune conditions: to screen for hematuria or proteinuria due to renal involvement
- Growth failure: to screen for renal tubular acidosis by looking for whether acidification of urine is intact

### PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON CAUSES OF ABNORMAL DIPSTICK TEST RESULTS

Table 3.1 summarizes the clinical significance of color changes of each of the chromogens in the urine dipstick.

It must be remembered that not all abnormal results are clinically significant. Causes of abnormal urine dipstick test results can be pathological or nonpathological (Tables 3.2 and 3.3). In addition, false-positive and false-negative results of the urine dipstick test are common.

TABLE 3-1

COMPONENTS IN URINE TEST

|  Component in Urine Test | Chromogen | Clinical Significance of Color Changes  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Specific gravity (SG) | Bromothymol blue | SG correlates with urine osmolality. This gives insight into the patient's hydration status or concentrating ability of the kidneys. Low SG (SG < 1.010) indicates relative hydration. High SG (SG > 1.020) indicates relative dehydration.  |
|  pH | Methyl red (color change in the range of pH 4–6), bromothymol blue (color change in the range of pH 6–9) | Normal urinary pH is slightly acidic (5.5–6.5). Urine pH generally reflects serum pH, except where there is renal tubular acidosis. Urine pH can also be affected by ingestion of protein, acidic fruits, and medications.  |
|  Blood/hemoglobin | Tetramethylbenzidine | Chromogen detects peroxidase activity of hemoglobin released by lysis of red blood cells in urine or myoglobin in urine. Hence, positive result may indicate hematuria, hemoglobinuria, or myoglobinuria.  |
|  Protein | Tetrachlorophenol, tetrabromosulfonphthalein | Chromogen sensitive to albumin but may not detect low concentrations of albumin (e.g., microalbuminuria) or Bence-Jones proteins or γ-globulins. A result of 1+ corresponds to approximately 30 mg of protein per dL. Hence, a result of 1+ or more is considered positive for proteinuria. Proteinuria is often associated with early renal disease.  |

Continued

TABLE 3-1

COMPONENTS IN URINE TEST—cont'd

|  Component in Urine Test | Chromogen | Clinical Significance of Color Changes  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Leukocyte esterase | Indoxyl ester hydrolysis followed by reaction with diazonium salt | Leukocyte esterase is produced by neutrophils. Hence, positive reaction indicates pyuria. To detect significant pyuria accurately, 5 min should be allowed for the dipstick chromogen to change color.  |
|  Nitrite | Reaction with para-arsanilic acid or sulfanilamide followed by reaction with tetrahydrobenzoquinoline | Nitrites are not normally found in urine. Bacteria reduce urinary nitrates to nitrites. Positive dipstick nitrite indicates the presence of nitrate-reducing bacteria (many Gram-negative and some Gram-positive bacteria) in significant numbers (i.e., >10,000/mL). Test is specific but not highly sensitive for UTI. Hence, a positive result suggests UTI, but a negative result does not rule out UTI.  |
|  Glucose | Glucose oxidase (oxidation of glucose) reaction followed by tetramethylbenzidine detecting peroxidase activity | Positive test indicates glycosuria, which occurs when the filtered load of glucose exceeds the ability of the proximal convoluted tubule of the kidney to reabsorb glucose (i.e., exceeds the renal threshold for glucose).  |
|  Ketone | Sodium nitroprusside | Ketones are products of body fat metabolism and are normally not found in urine. Positive test suggests the presence of ketosis (not dehydration). Ketosis may be physiological, e.g., response to fasting/starvation or pathological, e.g., in diabetic ketoacidosis.  |
|  Bilirubin | Diazonium salt (dichloroaniline or dichlorobenzene-diazonium-tetrafluoroborate) | Unconjugated bilirubin is water insoluble, whereas conjugated bilirubin is water soluble. Urine does not contain detectable amounts of bilirubin normally. Positive test needs evaluation.  |
|  Urobilinogen | Methoxybenzene-diazonium-tetrafluoroborate | Urine contains only small amounts of urobilinogen normally because most urobilinogen is excreted by the liver. Positive test needs evaluation.  |

TABLE 3-2

PATHOLOGIC, NONPATHOLOGIC, FALSE-POSITIVE CAUSES OF ABNORMAL URINE DIPSTICK RESULTS

|  Component in Urine Test | Pathologic Causes of a Positive Result | Nonpathologic Causes of a Positive Result | Causes of a False-Positive Result  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Specific gravity (SG) | Low SG: diabetes insipidus, diuretic use, impaired renal function High SG: dehydration, SIADH, glycosuria | Low SG: polydipsia | Contamination during collection and storage, IV radiopaque dyes  |

TABLE 3-2

PATHOLOGIC, NONPATHOLOGIC, FALSE-POSITIVE CAUSES OF ABNORMAL URINE
DIPSTICK RESULTS—cont'd

|  Component in Urine Test | Pathologic Causes of a Positive Result | Nonpathologic Causes of a Positive Result | Causes of a False-Positive Result  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  pH | Low pH: acidosis High pH: distal renal tubular acidosis, UTI due to urease-producing bacteria (e.g., Proteus mirabilis) | Low pH: high-protein diet, acidic fruits (e.g., cranberry) High pH: low-protein diet, high citrate diet | High pH from urease-producing organisms  |
|  Blood/hemoglobin | Glomerulonephritis, UTI or trauma, hypercalciuria, stones, tumor, renovascular or tubulointerstitial disorders Hemoglobinuria, myoglobinuria | Exercise, traumatic catheterization | Oxidizing contaminants (e.g., hypochlorite), dehydration, menstrual blood  |
|  Protein | Glomerulonephritis, tubular disorders, UTI | Exercise, fever, orthostatic proteinuria | Concentrated urine (SG > 1.025), alkaline urine (pH > 8), presence of cells/bacteria in urine, antiseptics, detergents  |
|  Leukocyte esterase | UTI, glomerulonephritis, pelvic inflammation | Fever | Oxidizing contaminants, contamination with vaginal fluid  |
|  Nitrite | UTI | None | Urine contamination, prolonged exposure of dipstick to air, medications that turn urine color to red, gross hematuria  |
|  Glucose | Diabetes mellitus, Fanconi syndrome | Transitory renal glycosuria after a meal high in sugars | Strong oxidizing agents  |
|  Ketone | Diabetic ketoacidosis | Restricted carbohydrate intake, e.g., starvation, ketogenic diet | Medicines with sulfhydryl groups (captopril, methyldopa, mesna)  |
|  Bilirubin | Hepatitis, biliary obstruction | None | Other pigments in urine (rifampin, chlorpromazine, etc.)  |
|  Urobilinogen | Hepatitis, intravascular hemolysis | Systemic antibiotic therapy, bile duct obstruction | Alkaline urine  |

TABLE 3-3

CAUSES OF FALSE-NEGATIVE RESULTS OF URINE DIPSTICK

|  Component in Urine Test | Causes of False Negatives  |
| --- | --- |
|  Specific gravity (SG) | Alkaline urine  |
|  pH | Low pH from mixing of reagents from adjacent test pads  |
|  Blood/hemoglobin | Ascorbic acid, large nitrites, high SG, pH < 5.1  |
|  Protein | Dilute urine (SG < 1.002), acidic urine (pH < 4.5), low molecular weight proteins  |
|  Leukocyte esterase | Ascorbic acid, proteinuria, glycosuria, ketonuria, high SG, cephalosporins, nitrofurantoin, tetracycline, gentamicin  |
|  Nitrite | Inadequate dietary nitrate intake, non-nitrate-reducing bacteria, insufficient incubation time for conversion of nitrate to nitrite, ascorbic acid, high urobilinogen, high SG, pH < 6  |
|  Glucose | Ascorbic acid, high SG, exposure to humid environment  |
|  Ketone | Prolonged standing of urine, moisture on test pad  |
|  Bilirubin | Ascorbic acid, prolonged standing in light  |
|  Urobilinogen | Broad-spectrum antibiotics, discolored urine, prolonged standing in light  |

### REDUCING FALSE-POSITIVE AND FALSE-NEGATIVE RESULTS OF URINE DIPSTICK

The following precautions can be taken to reduce false-positive and false-negative results of the urine dipstick test:

- Use midstream urine specimen, if possible, to reduce contamination.
- Test urine within 2 hours of collection.
- Do not use expired or discolored dipsticks.
- Keep the lid of the dipstick container tightly closed.
- Do not dip the dipstick in urine for more than a second.

### CLINICAL APPROACH TO ABNORMAL URINE DIPSTICK RESULTS

In interpreting the abnormal urine dipstick result and deciding on the extent of further investigations, consider the clinical scenario in which the dipstick is done and potential false positives and false negatives. The clinical approaches to positive blood, protein, nitrite, leukocyte esterase, glucose, and ketone on dipstick are presented here.

### 1. Urine Dipstick Blood Positive 1+ or More

- Look at the urine dipstick protein result. Check for symptoms and signs of acute nephritic syndrome, e.g., edema, hypertension, and oliguria.

- If symptoms are present and/or if the dipstick protein result is 1+ or more, start evaluation for acute glomerulonephritis.

- Check for lower urinary tract symptoms, e.g., dysuria, frequency, and urgency.

  - If symptoms are present, evaluate for UTI, hypercalciuria, or renal calculi.

- For a female who has started menses, check whether the urine test is done during her menstrual period.

  - If the urine test is done during her menses and she has none of the above-mentioned symptoms and signs, repeat the urine dipstick test after her menstrual period ends.

- Confirm if there is microscopic hematuria by doing a urine microscopic examination.

  - If no red blood cells are seen,

    - suspect hemoglobinuria or myoglobinuria (if urine is dark brown), and carry out the test to confirm this.

    - false-positive result due to dehydration (if urine is yellow).

  - For confirmed microscopic hematuria, and if patient has no abnormal symptoms and no abnormal signs are absent, and if the urine dipstick protein is negative or trace, repeat urinalysis weekly for another two times.

    - If repeat tests are negative, this is transient hematuria and patient can be discharged from further tests.

    - If repeat tests are positive, investigate for causes of persistent hematuria.

## 2. Urine Dipstick Protein Positive 1+ or More

- Confirm proteinuria with quantitative urine protein measurement, e.g., spot urine protein/creatinine (UPCr) ratio.
- Look for microscopic hematuria with urine microscopic examination.
- Check BP, and look for symptoms and signs of fluid retention.
- If the patient has signs or symptoms of

  - fluid retention mainly in the intravascular space (e.g., hypertension, oliguria, raised jugular venous pressure, enlarged turgid liver, and pitting edema), start evaluation for acute nephritic syndrome.

  - fluid retention mainly in the third space (pitting edema, ascites, no raised jugular venous pressure) and the dipstick protein result is 2+ or more, start evaluation for nephrotic syndrome.

- If the patient is asymptomatic, has a normal UPCr, and has no microscopic hematuria,

  - suspect false-positive result, e.g., dehydration, especially if urine SG > 1.025. Repeat at a later date.

- If the patient is asymptomatic, has an elevated UPCr, and with or without microscopic hematuria, do 24-h urinary total protein (UTP).

  - If 24-h UTP > 0.3 g/1.73 m²/day, further evaluation for the cause of proteinuria is required.

  - If 24-h UTP < 0.3 g/1.73 m²/day, suspect transient proteinuria or orthostatic proteinuria. In orthostatic proteinuria, early morning UPCr is normal, whereas evening UPCr is elevated.

### 3. Urine Nitrite Positive

- Send the urine for microscopic examination and culture.
- Urine nitrite test is highly specific for UTI. If a child is febrile or has irritative urinary symptoms, consider starting antibiotics for UTI after collecting urine for urine culture.

### 4. Urine Leukocyte Esterase Positive

- Send the urine for microscopic examination and culture.
- If the dipstick nitrite is also positive and the child is febrile or has irritative urinary symptoms, consider starting antibiotics for UTI after collecting urine for urine culture.
- If there is pyuria but urine culture is negative, consider causes of sterile pyuria such as viral infections, tuberculosis, and glomerulonephritis (especially if there is concurrent hematuria and proteinuria).

### 5. Urine Glucose Positive

- Check the blood glucose level.
- If blood glucose is elevated,

  - check urine ketones (present in diabetic ketoacidosis; absent in a patient with Type 2 diabetes mellitus or Type 1 diabetes mellitus who is not in diabetic ketoacidosis).

- If the blood glucose is normal,

  - presence of glucose and protein in urine may suggest Fanconi syndrome.
  - presence of glucose and absence of protein in urine may suggest renal glycosuria.

### 6. Urine Ketone Positive

- Check the dipstick glucose.

  - If the dipstick glucose is positive, this suggests diabetic ketoacidosis.
  - If the dipstick glucose is negative, the most common cause is restricted carbohydrate intake or starvation ketosis. There are rarer causes, e.g., inborn errors of metabolism.

LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- If the urine dipstick blood and/or protein is positive and there are symptoms of acute glomerulonephritis

  - Symptoms and signs of hypertension (no symptoms, headaches, visual disturbances, papilledema, seizures, elevated BP, etc.)
  - Symptoms and signs of fluid overload (puffiness, gain of weight, swollen feet, breathlessness, raised jugular venous pressure, enlarged liver, pitting edema, etc.)
  - Symptoms and signs of uremia (lethargy, loss of appetite, elevated urea, elevated creatinine, etc.)

- If the urine dipstick nitrite and/or leukocyte esterase is positive and there are signs and symptoms of febrile urosepsis
  - Symptoms and signs of sepsis (fever, poor feeding, vomiting, lethargy, poor perfusion, low BP, etc.)
- If the urine dipstick glucose and ketone are positive and there are signs and symptoms of ketoacidosis
  - Symptoms and signs of dehydration (lethargy, pronounced thirst, decreased skin turgor, tachycardia, low BP, etc.)
  - Symptoms and signs of acidosis (nausea, vomiting, tachypnea, Kussmaul breathing, etc.)

### CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 15-year-old male has fever, generalized myalgia, and backache for 3 days. He was evaluated by a family practitioner 3 days ago and was given antipyretic and painkiller medications. He presents to you now with one episode of dark colored urine. He describes his urine as dark brown. He is passing less urine due to decreased oral intake since past 3 days. He has no breathlessness or orthopnea or leg swelling. There is no dysuria, or frequency or urgency in urination.

On examination, his temperature is 38.5°C, heart rate 90/min, respiratory rate 16/min, blood pressure 100/67 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air is 97%. His height is on the 50th percentile. His weight is on the 75th percentile. There is no periorbital or lower limb edema. His peripheries are warm. Capillary refill time is less than 2 seconds. His throat, nose, and ears are normal. His neck is supple. On auscultation of the heart, S1 and S2 are heard. There are no additional heart sounds. On auscultation of the lungs, there is vesicular breath sounds. The abdomen is soft and nontender. No organomegaly is found.

A urine dipstick is done. The results are as follows: hemoglobin 3+, leukocyte esterase trace, nitrite negative, protein trace, urobilinogen 2+, bilirubin 1+, ketone negative, and glucose negative.

Urine microscopic examination shows red blood cells of 2/high power field (0–2), white blood cells of <1/high power field (0–4).

Full blood count shows white cell count 10.1 × 10⁹/L (4–10.5), hemoglobin 10.0 g/dL (12.5–16.1), and platelet 372 × 10⁹/L (150–500). Renal function test shows urea 5.8 mmol/L (2.5–6.0), creatinine 76 μmol/L (46–94), sodium 137 mmol/L (135–150), and potassium 3.9 mmol/L (3.5–5.0).

Liver function test shows total bilirubin 156 μmol/L (5–30), conjugated bilirubin 28 μmol/L (0–5), unconjugated bilirubin 128 μmol/L (5–25), albumin 37 g/L (35–45), AST 56 U/L (10–50), ALT 25 U/L (10–70), LDH 4535 U/L (300–700).

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that supports this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that goes against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Dark colored urine, and presence of hemoglobin, bilirubin, and urobilinogen on urine dipstick

- What is your diagnosis?

- Hemolytic anemia

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Presence of hemoglobinuria: presence of hemoglobin on urine dipstick but no significant red blood cells found in urine microscopic examination.
- Presence of urobilinogen in urine supports intravascular hemolysis.
- Suggestive of ongoing hemolysis

- Anemia (low hemoglobin in blood)
- Elevated serum bilirubin (total, conjugated, and unconjugated), lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information about laboratory confirmation of hemoglobin and hemosiderin in urine.
- No information about jugular venous pressure or splenomegaly on physical examination.
- No information about the cause of hemolytic anemia.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Myoglobinuria secondary to rhabdomyolysis in viral myositis

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Myalgia.
- Myoglobinuria is dark in color, causes a false positive of hemoglobin in urine, and no significant red blood cells on urine microscopy.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Bilirubin and urobilinogen should not be present in the urine of myoglobinuria. Hence, the presence of these findings supports the diagnosis of intravascular hemolysis causing dark urine rather than myoglobinuria.

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

o Mild anemia; patient is not symptomatic of anemia.

- What is your plan for this patient?

o Get further information about whether the patient has G6PD deficiency, family history of hemolytic anemia (hereditary spherocytosis, alpha-thalassemia), and details of recent or concurrent drug history.

o Confirm the presence of hemoglobin, hemosiderin, or myoglobin in urine with a urine specimen to the laboratory.

o Carry out serum haptoglobin. Decreased serum haptoglobin further supports hemolytic anemia.

o Carry out the following tests to work out the cause of hemolytic anemia: peripheral blood film, direct Coombs test. There may be a need to do investigations to screen for autoimmune causes, if the direct Coombs test is positive.

o Hydrate the patient.

o Monitor full blood count over the next few days. Watch for severe and/or symptomatic anemia.

o Further history revealed that this patient had taken mefenamic acid, which is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID), for his fever and myalgia. The direct Coombs test was positive, suggesting autoimmune hemolytic anemia secondary to NSAID intake. Mefenamic acid was stopped and he recovered from hemolytic anemia.

# Active Child

Kang Ying Qi • Jennifer Kiing • Kalyani Mulay

• Denise L.M. Goh • Charmaine Teo

# DEFINITION OF AN ACTIVE CHILD

"Active child" is a term frequently used by a nonmedical person to describe a child who seems to have a lot of energy or one who keeps moving about. It is also sometimes used to describe a child who is perceived not to have normal behavior.

# SYMPTOMS OF AN ACTIVE CHILD

An active child should be checked to see if he/she has signs and symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, and/or impulsivity that are out of the range for his/her age.

# Inattention

Inattention refers to moving from one task/activity to another without adequately completing any one task/activity.

Symptoms include the following:

- Failing to give close attention to detail and making careless mistakes
- Difficulty in following instructions and failing to complete tasks
- Difficulty in sustaining attention during activities and getting easily distracted
- Often getting distracted by extraneous stimuli
- Avoiding activities that demand sustained mental effort
- Often not listening when spoken to directly
- Difficulty in organizing tasks and activities
- Forgetful in daily activities
- Often losing things necessary for daily activities

# Hyperactivity

Hyperactivity refers to excessive motor activity.

Symptoms can include the following:

- Often fidgeting with hands or feet or squirming while in a seat
- Often leaving the seat in classroom or in other situations in which remaining seated is expected
- Often running about or climbing excessively in situations in which it is inappropriate
- Often being "on the go"

44

- Often having difficulty in playing or engaging in leisure activities quietly
- Often talking excessively

### Impulsivity

Impulsivity refers to acting without adequate forethought or the tendency to act with less forethought than peers.

Symptoms can include the following:

- Often interrupting or intruding on others
- Often blurting out answers before questions have been completed
- Often having difficulty in awaiting for his/her turn

### PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

The differential diagnoses for an active child are as follows:

- Within the range of normal variation

  - Preschool-aged children are normally quite active and sometimes mistaken to be "hyperactive," especially by parents/other adults who are busy or have limited experience of handling younger children.

- Not within the range of normal variation

  - Psychiatric/CNS causes:

    - Attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD).
    - Other developmental disorders, e.g., autism, learning difficulty, intellectual disability, language disorder.
    - Other emotional/psychiatric disorders, e.g., anxiety, depression, oppositional defiant disorder, other conduct disorders.
    - Seizures, tics.
    - Hearing impairment, visual impairment.
    - Bright children who feel that they are not being sufficiently challenged in school, e.g., bright students may feel bored and not complete work because it is "too easy," whereas the teachers feel that if he/she is so bright, he/she should be completing things faster than his/her peers.
    - Psychosomatic manifestation of non-accidental injury (child abuse including sexual abuse) or family dysfunction.

  - Other medical conditions:

    - Thyroid disorders, obstructive sleep apnea or other sleep-related disorders.

  - Environmental causes:

    - Medications, e.g., decongestant, cough mixture.
    - Stimulants, e.g., coffee, tea, other caffeine-containing drinks (e.g., some sodas and soft drinks).
    - Unfavorable parenting style and expectations.
    - Inadequate sleep.
    - Excessive screen time.
    - Screen time before bed time.

![img-7.jpeg](img-7.jpeg)

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Take a Good History

- Note the age of the child and whether the child was premature.
- Ask the parents what they mean exactly when they say the child is active.
- Take a detailed history about the child's behavior to see if there are symptoms suggestive of inattention, hyperactivity, and/or impulsivity (see above for the symptoms).
- Ask for other associated symptoms.
- Do a systemic review, including gathering information about sleep, screen time use, growth, and development.
- Ask about drug history.
- Ask about diet history.
- Ask about family history.
- Ask about social history, including who the caregivers are, who are the disciplinarians, and get information about parenting style.
- See if there are elements of the history that are suggestive of non-accidental injuries (child abuse) (see Chapter 35 on injuries).
- See if there is any legal violation, e.g., stealing, involvement in gangs, drug abuse, and sexual assault.
- Assess for risk of or evidence of self-harm or suicidal intention.
- Gather information from other caregivers, school teachers, etc.
- Consider using tools such as the National Initiative for Children's Healthcare Quality (NICHQ), Vanderbilt Assessment Scales, or the Conners Rating Scale to assess the child.

# 2. Do a Thorough Physical Examination

- Do a good head-to-toe physical examination, including the child's emotional state, growth, and development.
- Observe the child's behavior and the child's interaction with you and the other people in the room.
- See if there is any behavior in the child and/or caregiver suggestive of non-accidental injury. Observe parent and caregiver's parenting style.

# 3. Algorithm

- See the flow chart.
- Does the child have signs and symptoms suggestive of inattention, hyperactivity, and/or impulsivity that are outside of the normal variation for the age of the child?
- If they are outside the normal variation

- Are there red flags?

- Non-accidental injuries (child abuse including sexual abuse) (see Chapter 35 on injuries).
- Over disciplining.
- Any legal violation, e.g., stealing, involvement in gangs, drug abuse, and sexual assault.
- Any risk of or evidence of self-harm or suicidal intention.

- If there are red flags, escalate immediately to senior and the appropriate authorities and manage accordingly.
- Does this child have symptoms/signs of conditions that contribute to or mimic ADHD?

- If the child has a condition(s) that can contribute to or mimic ADHD, treat this first and see if the symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity resolve.
- If they do resolve completely, the child does not have ADHD.
- If they do not resolve completely, see if the child meets all the DSM-V criteria for ADHD.

- If the child does not have symptoms/signs of conditions that contribute to or mimic ADHD, see if the child meets all the DSM-V criteria for ADHD which are as follows:

- Symptoms of inattention/hyperactivity/impulsivity present for at least 6 months and the onset is prior to 12 years old.
- Present in at least two settings (e.g., home and school).
- Affects functioning.
- Not better accounted for by another mental health disorder.

- If the child meets all the DSM-V ADHD criteria, the diagnosis of ADHD is made.

- Start ADHD management, which includes, behavioural therapy, medication and educational support.
- Review if there is a need to continue treating the conditions contributing/mimicking ADHD.

- If the child does not meet all the DSM-V ADHD criteria and does not have a condition mimicking ADHD, the child is likely to be within the normal variation. Monitor the child as clinically indicated.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Possible neglect/non-accidental injury (child abuse)/over disciplining at home
- Any legal violations, e.g., stealing, involvement in gangs, drug abuse, and sexual assault
- Any risk of or evidence of self-harm or suicidal intention

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

Natalie is a 6-year-9-month-old girl who presents with a short-attention span. Her teacher reports that Natalie does not concentrate in class and is often fiddling with stationery during lesson time. She is often seen disturbing her friends during lessons or chatting to the girl sitting beside her when the teacher is talking.

Her parents report that she has been inattentive since preschool. While in the kindergarten, teachers have often commented about her lack of focus and short-attention span. She would often stand up and walk around

the class during lesson time. During ballet class, Natalie would fidget and move about when her friends were standing in a line.

Natalie is now in Primary 1 and can remain seated in the class. However, she often touches or takes her friends' things without their permission. She sometimes also touches their faces to get their attention while the teacher is talking. Natalie is described by her mother to be a disorganized girl who often misplaces her things. She needs frequent reminders to complete her school work and pack the right things for school.

She makes friends easily but does not appear to keep them for very long. She likes to lead and have her friends follow her lead. When her friends want to do something else, disagreements would occur. Natalie has difficulties in resolving conflicts. Natalie is also a bit of a worrier. She worries about doing well in school and always wants to be first.

In terms of her learning and development, Natalie achieved normal milestones. She is articulate and expresses her ideas well. She has difficulties completing her work in class and needs to complete it at home. Even at home, she needs a lot of prompting to complete her work. Natalie is able to write fairly neatly, but her handwriting tends to be messy when she is rushing through pieces of work. Her school grades are below average and this is of concern to her parents and teachers.

Natalie was born full term with no postnatal complications. Except for mild eczema, Natalie has no other medical problems. Natalie passed her newborn hearing screen soon after birth. Her vision is normal. There is no history of developmental regression. Natalie gets 8 hours of sleep on weekdays and 13 hours of sleep on average on weekends. Her screen time is limited to 1-2 hours per day. Natalie has a younger sister, who is 3 years old. Natalie lives with her parents, who are her main caregivers. The family is bilingual in English and Chinese.

On examination, her blood pressure is 100/85 mmHg with a heart rate of 90/min. Her height and weight are on the 25th percentile. Natalie is not dysmorphic. Throughout the consult, Natalie sat at the desk drawing an elaborate picture. She remained seated for half an hour, drawing and listening to the consult. Natalie displayed affection and closeness to her parents and appropriate social understanding in the consult.

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

• Inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity.

• What is your diagnosis?

• The girl has sleep deprivation and possible anxiety issues that may be contributing to or mimicking ADHD.

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

• Symptoms of inattention

■ Does not concentrate in class.
■ Has difficulties completing her work in class and needs to complete it at home.
■ Needs a lot of prompting to complete her work
■ Needs frequent reminders to pack the right things for school.
■ Disorganized girl.
■ Often misplaces her things.

• Symptoms of hyperactivity

■ Often fiddling with stationery during lesson time.
■ Would often stand up and walk around the class during lesson time.
■ During ballet class, would fidget and move about when her friends were standing in a line.

• Symptoms of impulsivity

■ Often seen disturbing her friends during lessons or chatting to the girl sitting beside her when the teacher is talking.
■ Often touches or takes her friends' things without their permission.
■ Sometimes also touches their faces to get their attention while the teacher is talking.

• Symptoms of inattention present in preschool (before 12 years old).

• Symptoms of inattention present in more than one setting (school and home).

• Symptoms present for more than 6 months.

• Affects her social functioning and her academic performance.

• Possible contributing/mimicking conditions:

■ She is a bit of a worrier. This may be symptomatic of anxiety which can mimic inattentive ADHD.
■ She is not getting enough sleep (10 hours is recommended for her age).

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

• None.

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

• None.

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

• ADHD.

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

○ List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

■ The girl has symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity as listed above. She meets all DSM-V criteria.

○ List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

■ Natalie is able to sit quietly and occupy herself in clinic (but this may not be representative as some hyperactive kids can behave in clinic but not in classrooms or places with lots of distractions).

○ If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

■ Natalie would have difficulty following instructions and would not seem to hear when spoken directly: No information given on this.

■ Natalie would have task-avoidant behavior: avoiding or disliking tasks that require ongoing mental effort. No information given on this.

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ No.

- What is your plan for this patient?

○ Optimize sleep.

○ Explore if the child is anxious; if so, to start some nonmedication-based intervention to help reduce anxiety, e.g., counseling, cognitive, and behavioral therapy.

○ See if symptoms resolve with the above interventions. If not, refer to developmental pediatrician for possible ADHD.

# Altered Mental State

Koh Pei Lin • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF ALTERED MENTAL STATE

Altered mental state (AMS) is defined as failure to respond to verbal or physical stimulation in a manner that is deemed appropriate for that person's developmental level.

# SYMPTOMS OF ALTERED MENTAL STATE

The symptoms of the alteration of mental state can be due to altered perception and/or decreased level of awareness.

- Symptoms of altered perception

- Disoriented, and cognitive abilities are slowed/impaired (confusion)
- Disoriented, cognitive abilities are slowed/impaired plus evidence of disordered thinking, agitation or inability to focus (delirium)

- Symptoms of decreased level of awareness

- Awake but less alert than usual and disinterested in the surroundings (lethargy)
- Sleepy, depressed consciousness, disinterested in the surroundings, and slow psychomotor responses (obtunded)
- In a sleep-like state, has depressed consciousness, can respond to stimuli but the response is not full (e.g., only withdraws to pain, does not wake up fully, and no verbalization) (stupor)
- In a sleep-like state, unarousable, and unresponsive to stimuli (comatose)

- There may be symptoms of the underlying cause of the AMS (see the section "CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM").

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

An AMS indicates depression of the cerebral cortex or dysfunction of the ascending reticular activating system (RAS). There are many causes of AMS in a child. The causes can be divided into primary central nervous system (CNS) pathologies, systemic causes, or mental health causes.

# 1. Primary CNS Causes

- CNS infection (e.g., meningitis, encephalitis, brain abscess, cerebral malaria)
- Space-occupying lesion (e.g., brain tumor)
- Seizure/epilepsy and status epilepticus, including nonconvulsive status epilepticus

52

- Raised intracranial pressure (e.g., due to space-occupying lesion, bleed, stroke, obstructive hydrocephalus)
- Brain concussion/contusion
- Intracranial bleeding

  - Spontaneous, e.g., from arteriovenous malformation/cerebral aneurysm

  - Traumatic (accidental or non-accidental)

- Arterial ischemic stroke
- Cerebral venous sinus thrombosis
- Noninfectious causes of encephalitis, e.g., systemic lupus erythematosus with CNS involvement, acute disseminated encephalomyelitis (ADEM)

## 2. Systemic Causes

- Hypoxia/ischemia
- Hypercarbia
- Hypertension leading to hypertensive encephalopathy
- Hypotension/shock (hypovolemic, distributive, cardiogenic, etc.)
- Temperature derangements, e.g., hyperthermia (heat stroke), hypothermia
- Drugs/toxins/poisoning
- Hypothyroidism
- Metabolic derangements

  - Hypoglycemia
  - Hyperglycemia, e.g., diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA), hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state
  - Hyponatremia
  - Hypernatremia
  - Hypercalcemia
  - Metabolic acidosis, e.g., lactic acidosis
  - Uremia
  - Hyperammonemia
  - Hepatic encephalopathy

## 3. Mental Health Causes

Psychiatric catatonia is a rare cause but may be seen in adolescents. This is a diagnosis by exclusion.

### CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

As a child with an AMS may have a life-threatening condition, AMS is a medical emergency, and prompt evaluation and concurrent resuscitation (if necessary) is of utmost importance. In practice, assessment and management are performed jointly.

![img-8.jpeg](img-8.jpeg)

Continued on next page

Continued from previous page

![img-9.jpeg](img-9.jpeg)

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

- Check pulse

- Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
- Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

- When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

- If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates the need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate the need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.
- Airway: Is the airway compromised?

- Assess if the patient is able to maintain the airway open.
- If not, then take measures to keep the airway open, e.g., insert an oropharyngeal airway, if the child is obtunded, or do a head-tilt chin lift if there is no neck injury.
- Presence of stridor is an indication of partial obstruction of the upper airway. Exclude foreign body, croup, or extrinsic compression of the airway, e.g., mediastinal mass, neck mass.
- Presence of secretions warrants suctioning in most instances.
- Be cautious when suctioning in the presence of stridor. The suctioning can trigger laryngospasm.

- Breathing: Is the breathing compromised?

- Assess the respiratory rate and effort:

- Tachypnea, subcostal and intercostal retractions, and nasal flaring are evidence of respiratory distress in children.
- Grunting is a sign of respiratory distress in neonates and young babies. Grunting is an attempt to generate positive end expiratory pressure so as to help keep their airway/air sacs open so as to improve oxygenation.
- Kussmaul breathing suggests primary metabolic acidosis, e.g., diabetic ketoacidosis and certain poisonings such as salicylate.

■ Symptoms and signs of respiratory depression indicate that breathing is ineffective and respiratory support in the form of assisted ventilation should be instituted.

○ Regular reassessment of breathing effort is important.

○ Assess with pulse oximetry for hypoxia – normal SpO₂ should be more than 94% in room air.

○ Auscultate the lungs to detect adventitious sounds or unequal breath sounds.

• Circulation: Is the circulation compromised?

○ Assess the heart rate and rhythm:

■ Tachycardia is the first sign of circulatory compromise in infants and children.

■ Bradycardia may be a sinister sign suggestive of prolonged circulatory compromise or cardiac arrhythmia; bradycardia with a heart rate of less than 60/min indicates impending arrest.

○ Assess pulses and pulse volume. Pulse volume may be reduced in circulatory compromise. Pulse volume may be bounding in septic shock.

○ Assess the blood pressure for hypotension or hypertension.

○ Assess the capillary refill time – delayed capillary refill (>2 s) indicates poor peripheral perfusion.

○ Assess the peripheries for temperature and color.

■ Cool peripheries indicate poor peripheral perfusion.

■ Duskiness, being gray, or mottled indicates a state of impaired circulation and should be taken as a sinister sign.

■ Pallor indicates anemia, peripheral vasoconstriction or poor perfusion.

○ Look at the cardiac rhythm if the child is on an ECG monitor to determine if there is sinus rhythm or an arrhythmia.

• Disability: Is there any disability?

○ Assess the conscious level of the child using the Glasgow Coma Scale or the AVPU scale:

■ See Table 5-1 for the Glasgow Coma Scale.

■ AVPU pediatric response scale is

□ Alert – alert, awake, and appropriately responsive to parents and stimuli

□ Voice – responds only to voice

□ Painful – responds only to painful stimuli

□ Unresponsive – does not respond to any stimulus

○ Unresponsive or GCS of 8 or less is associated with an inability to protect the airway. The airway may need to be secured with endotracheal intubation.

○ Look at the pupils for size, symmetry, and response to light:

■ Bilaterally constricted pupils can be due to opiate poisoning.

■ Bilaterally dilated pupils can be due to sympathomimetic agent poisoning, e.g., amphetamine, or anticholinergic agent poisoning, e.g., tricyclic antidepressant.

TABLE 5-1

GLASGOW COMA SCALE AND PEDIATRIC GLASGOW COMA SCALE

|  Sign | Glasgow Coma Scale^{a} | Pediatric Glasgow Coma Scale^{b} | Score  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Eye opening | Spontaneous | Spontaneous | 4  |
|   |  To command | To sound | 3  |
|   |  To pain | To pain | 2  |
|   |  None | None | 1  |
|  Verbal response | Oriented | Age-appropriate vocalization, smile, or orientation to sound, interacts (coos, babbles), follows objects | 5  |
|   |  Confused, disoriented | Cries, irritable | 4  |
|   |  Inappropriate words | Cries to pain | 3  |
|   |  Incomprehensible sounds | Moans to pain | 2  |
|   |  None | None | 1  |
|  Motor response | Obeys commands | Spontaneous movements (obeys verbal command) | 6  |
|   |  Localizes pain | Withdraws to touch (localizes pain) | 5  |
|   |  Withdraws | Withdraws to pain | 4  |
|   |  Abnormal flexion to pain | Abnormal flexion to pain (decorticate posture) | 3  |
|   |  Abnormal extension to pain | Abnormal extension to pain (decerebrate posture) | 2  |
|   |  None | None | 1  |
|  **Best total score** |   |   | **15**  |

Note: A GCS score of 13 or higher correlates with mild brain injury; a score of 9–12 correlates with moderate injury; and a score of 8 or less represents severe brain injury. The Pediatric Glasgow Coma Scale (PGCS) was validated in children aged 2 years or less.

$^{a}$Data from: Teasdale G, Jennett B. Assessment of coma and impaired consciousness. A practical scale. Lancet 1974; 2:81.

$^{b}$Data from: Holmes JF, Palchak MJ, MacFarlane T, Kuppermann N. Performance of the Pediatric Glasgow Coma Scale in children with blunt head trauma. Acad Emerg Med 2005; 12:814.

■ Unilateral/bilateral dilated, unreactive, or sluggishly reactive pupil indicate raised intracranial pressure or the third cranial nerve palsy.

○ Identify any seizure.
○ Determine the blood glucose level.

• Exposure: Any abnormality on exposure?

○ Assess for hyper or hypothermia.
○ Look for rash, which may help identify the underlying diagnosis.
○ Look for evidence of trauma or bleeding.

• If primary assessment indicates a life-threatening condition, institute resuscitation measures immediately, e.g., intubation.

### 3. Secondary Assessment

• Take a focused history

○ SAMPLE mnemonic:

■ Signs and symptoms – decreased consciousness, fever, seizure, headache, shortness of breath, etc.

- Allergies – medications, food, etc.
- Medications – medications, last dose and time taken, any possible drug overdose, accidental poisoning
- Past medical history, e.g., diabetes mellitus, asthma, congenital heart disease, seizures, brain tumor
- Last meal – time and nature of the last meal
- Events – events leading to current illness, e.g., sudden or gradual onset, injury

- Do a complete top to toe physical examination

- Full neurological examination, including looking for neck stiffness and signs of meningism; do fundoscopy (look for papilledema in raised intracranial pressure, retinal hemorrhages in shaken baby syndrome)
- Cardiovascular, respiratory, and abdomen examination
- Examination of head and neck and peripheries for signs of injury
- Examination of ear canals/nose for blood/cerebrospinal fluid (CSF)

# 4. What Is the Likely Cause of the Altered Mental State (AMS)?

- See the flowchart for the likely differential diagnosis based on primary and secondary assessment.
- In general, the differential diagnosis can be narrowed if the AMS is grouped based on the associated symptoms:

- AMS + temperature abnormality
- AMS + low blood pressure
- AMS + high blood pressure
- AMS + low SpO₂
- AMS + seizure
- AMS + abnormal neurological examination
- AMS only; no other associated symptoms

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- GCS < 8
- Unresponsive
- Dilated, nonreactive, or sluggish pupil(s)
- Seizure
- Hypotension
- Hypertension
- Hypertension and bradycardia = Cushing reflex (seen in raised intracranial pressure)
- Central cyanosis, hypoxia
- Apnea/hypoventilation/low respiratory rate
- Respiratory distress (suggestive of primary respiratory acidosis or primary metabolic acidosis)
- Bradycardia

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 3-month-old baby girl was brought to the Children's Emergency by her mother for the chief complaint of drowsiness. According to her mother, the baby was previously well. She was born full-term with good birth weight but was described as a "colicky" baby who was always crying and needed to be carried most of the time. Yesterday, the baby had a low-grade fever, vomited once after feeding, and was noted to be more drowsy than usual. Today, the baby vomited once after feeding and suddenly turned pale, limp, and unresponsive. Her mother then rushed her to hospital.

On examination, there was no spontaneous eye opening. Her eyes did not open in response to pain, but she moaned to pain with extension of limbs (GCS 5 – E1M2V2). She had shallow respiratory effort with a respiratory rate of 10 breaths per minute. She was pale. Central and peripheral pulses were palpable. Heart rate was 65/min, and blood pressure was 120/80 mm Hg, and oxygen saturation was 90% on room air. Her pupils were dilated at 5 mm bilaterally and sluggishly reactive to light.

In view of her low GCS < 8, she was intubated immediately. Bedside capillary blood glucose was 6.8 mmol/L. Capillary blood gas showed pH 7.16, pCO₂ 61 mm Hg, pO₂ 58 mm Hg, HCO₃ 21.2 mmol/L, base excess – 4 mmol/L.

Further physical examination revealed bilateral retinal hemorrhages and papilledema. There were circular bruises on her back of varying ages. Heart sounds were normal with no murmur. Lungs were clear. Abdominal examination was normal. Her tone was increased in all four limbs, with bilateral ankle clonus and hyperreflexia in all four limbs. Babinski reflexes were upgoing bilaterally.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that supports this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that goes against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

  o Altered mental state

• What is your diagnosis?

  o AMS due to acute brain injury (concussion/contusion/intracranial bleed), most likely secondary to non-accidental injury.

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

  o Evidence for AMS

    ■ Symptoms – drowsiness, unresponsiveness
    ■ GCS = 5, AVPU scale = painful

  o Signs of acute brain injury

    ■ Signs of raised intracranial pressure: hypertension and bradycardia (Cushing reflex), dilated and sluggishly reactive pupils, papilledema, hypertonia, hyperreflexia
    ■ Retinal hemorrhages on fundoscopy

  o Signs of non-accidental injuries: bruises of varying ages, bruises in a nonmobile infant, retinal hemorrhages

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

  o Fever may suggest (intracranial) infection as a cause, but is more likely a red herring in this child. Rarely, a subarachnoid bleed can cause fever.

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

  o Detailed history to look for risk factors for non-accidental injury (see Chapter 35 on injuries)

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

  o GCS < 8
  o Dilated, nonreactive, or sluggish pupils
  o Hypertension and bradycardia = Cushing reflex (seen in raised intracranial pressure)
  o Hypoxia
  o Hypoventilation, low respiratory rate
  o Primary respiratory acidosis

• What is your plan for this patient?

  o Immediate ventilation to decrease pCO₂ to 35–40 mm Hg, which will help to decrease intracranial pressure.
  o Obtain venous access. Urgent CT brain scan.
  o Intravenous hyperosmolar therapy, e.g., intravenous mannitol or hypertonic saline.
  o Urgent neurosurgical referral.
  o Transfer to the pediatric intensive care unit or OT depending on the surgeon's opinion.
  o Refer to medical social worker and child protection services.
  o File a police report for non-accidental injury.

# Bleeding from GIT
(Upper and Lower)

Andrea W.C. Yeo • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF BLEEDING FROM GASTROINTESTINAL TRACT (GIT)

Gastrointestinal (GI) bleeding is defined as bleeding occurring from anywhere along the GI tract, from the esophagus all the way down to the anus.

GI bleeding is usually divided into

- Upper GI bleeding, which involves the GI tract proximal to the ligament of Treitz (which is found in the distal duodenum)
- Lower GI bleeding, which involves the GI tract distal to the ligament of Treitz

# SYMPTOMS OF BLEEDING FROM GIT

- Hematemesis: the vomiting of bright red blood or coffee-grounds.
- Melena: the passage of black tarry stool, which is usually very smelly.
- Hematochezia: the passing of bright red or maroon-colored blood mixed with stool.
- Fresh bleeding per rectal.
- Abnormal fecal occult blood (FOB) test: small amounts of blood in the stool that are not visible to the naked eye but may be detected on laboratory testing:
  - In a guaiac-based FOB test, a positive test result indicates that bleeding is occurring somewhere in the GI tract (upper and/or lower GI tract).
  - In a fecal immunochemical test (FIT) based FOB test, a positive test result indicates that there is bleeding in the lower GI tract. A FIT based FOB test is specific for human hemoglobin and hence will detect only human blood. A FIT based FOB test will only be positive when the human hemoglobin is relatively intact. In an upper GI bleed, the hemoglobin is usually broken down by the time it reaches the lower GI tract and hence will not be detected by a FIT based FOB test.
- Anemia may be the first presentation of occult GI bleeding.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Upper GI Bleeding

- Causes

- Esophageal: Mallory–Weiss tear, reflux esophagitis, eosinophilic esophagitis, varices.
- Gastric: peptic ulcer disease, gastritis, Helicobacter pylori infection, varices.

62

○ Duodenal: peptic ulcer disease, duodenal web, duplication cyst.
○ Vascular: vascular malformations, vasculitis, e.g., Henoch–Schonlein purpura (HSP), hereditary hemorrhagic telangiectasia (HHT).

○ Hematological

■ Coagulopathy

□ Congenital, e.g., von-Willebrand disease, hemophilia.
□ Acquired: infections, chronic liver disease, vitamin K deficiency, drugs.

■ Platelet disorders

□ Isolated thrombocytopenia; no other cell-line involvement.
□ Thrombocytopenia with other cell-line involvement (pancytopenia).
□ Platelet function disorders.
□ See Chapter 14 on bruising and bleeding.

○ Others: poisoning with caustic substances, foreign body, drugs (e.g., nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs [NSAIDs]), tumors (e.g., leiomyoma).

○ Trauma

• Manifestations

○ Signs and symptoms of upper GI bleeding:

■ Usually presents as hematemesis or melena.
■ Occasionally can present as hematochezia, if the blood moves rapidly down the GI tract.
■ Can also cause occult blood in the stool.
■ Anemia may be the presentation of chronic, slow blood loss.

○ Gastritis and peptic ulcers usually cause epigastric pain, and can be caused by stress, critical illness, or drugs, the most common of which is NSAIDs.
○ Bleeding from esophageal varices can be massive; varices are usually caused by portal hypertension from chronic liver disease/liver cirrhosis, portal vein thrombosis or hepatic vein obstruction; if chronic liver disease is present, there may be jaundice or stigmata of chronic liver disease.

# 2. Lower GI Bleeding

• Causes

○ Small intestine: intussusception, Meckel diverticulum, necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC), Crohn's disease
○ Large intestine: allergic colitis (e.g., milk protein allergy/intolerance), volvulus, Hirschsprung disease, infectious enterocolitis, intestinal polyps, inflammatory bowel disease (Crohn's disease or ulcerative colitis)
○ Anus: perianal fissure, hemorrhoids, anal trauma
○ Hematological

■ Coagulopathy

□ Congenital, e.g., von-Willebrand disease, hemophilia
□ Acquired: infections, chronic liver disease, vitamin K deficiency, drugs

■ Platelet disorders

☐ Isolated thrombocytopenia; no other cell-line involvement.
☐ Thrombocytopenia with other cell-line involvement (pancytopenia).
☐ Platelet function disorders.
☐ See Chapter 14 on bruising and bleeding.

○ Vascular: vascular malformations, vasculitis (e.g., HSP), HHT

○ Others: foreign body, drugs (e.g., NSAIDs), tumors (e.g., juvenile polyp)

○ Trauma

• Manifestations

○ Signs and symptoms of lower GI bleed:

■ Usually presents with hematochezia or fresh blood per rectum.
■ Occasionally can present as melena (which implies blood has had time to be altered in the GI tract).
■ Can also present as occult blood in the stool.
■ Anemia may be the presentation of chronic, slow blood loss; may be associated with abdominal pain, diarrhea, and/or vomiting, depending on the etiology.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Initial Impression

• Initial impression

○ Assess appearance
○ Assess work of breathing
○ Assess circulation to skin (color)

• The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

○ If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

■ Check pulse

☐ Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
☐ Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

■ When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

○ If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

• Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
• The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
• If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
• If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

![img-12.jpeg](img-12.jpeg)

![img-13.jpeg](img-13.jpeg)

### 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

- History taking: Determine whether there is really blood in the GI tract

  - Ask for the color, nature of the bleeding.

    - Determine if it is hematemesis, melena, hematochezia, fresh bleeding per rectal and/or a positive FOB test by history.
    - Inspect the specimen if available.
    - Exclude the ingestion of substances that may look like blood, e.g., beet, red food colorings.
    - Exclude the ingestion of substances that may look like melena, e.g., activated charcoal, iron, bismuth, or blueberry ingestion.
    - Exclude bleeding from adjacent areas, e.g., menses may be mistaken as bleeding per rectal.

  - Ask for the associated symptoms; there are many possible associated symptoms; if the patient is not a good historian, then you will need to ask more focused questions.

  - Ascertain the possible source of the blood:

    - Hematemesis: swallowed blood, bleeding from upper GI tract.
    - Melena: swallowed blood, bleeding from upper GI tract, rarely from lower GI bleeding.
    - Hematochezia: swallowed blood, rapid bleeding from the upper GI tract, bleeding from the lower GI tract.
    - Fresh bleeding per rectal: rapid bleeding from the upper GI tract, bleeding from the lower GI tract.
    - Fecal occult blood:

      - Swallowed blood (may not be detected if the assay used was a FIT based FOB test).
      - Bleeding from the upper GI tract (may not be detected if FIT based FOB test was the assay used).
      - Bleeding from the lower GI tract.

- History taking: Determine whether it is swallowed blood

  - If a baby is breastfed, ask if there is any sore or bleeding nipple. If there is, a breastfed baby may ingest maternal blood during breastfeeding, and this may mimic an upper GI bleed. An Apt test may be required to differentiate maternal from baby's blood.
  - Ask if there is any bleeding from the nose or mouth. Epistaxis and/or bleeding from the upper respiratory tract or mouth can mimic an upper GI bleed as blood from these sites might be swallowed, and subsequently vomited out as hematemesis or coffee-grounds vomitus, passed out as melena, or give a positive result on a guaiac-based FOB test.

- History taking: Determine whether there is a systemic cause for the bleeding

  - Ask if there is any bleeding elsewhere.

  - History suggestive of a systemic cause of bleeding includes:

    - Skin changes such as petechiae, bruises, ecchymoses, hemangiomas, telangiectasias, vasculitic rash.
    - Persistent bleeding after minor skin abrasion.

- Subcutaneous hematoma.
- Muscle hematoma.
- Joint bleed.
- Menorrhagia.
- Hematuria.
- Cerebrovascular bleed.
- Pulmonary hemorrhage/hemothorax.
- Delayed bleeding.

- History taking: If the history is suggestive of a GI tract bleed, look for the cause

○ Is there any vomiting? Was this forceful? If yes, this may suggest a Mallory–Weiss tear.

○ Is there any abdominal pain?

- A history of epigastric pain that is relieved by meals is suggestive of gastritis or gastric ulcers.
- A history of intermittent abdominal pain may suggest a GI infection, inflammatory colitis, or an obstructive cause.
- When there is GI bleeding, abdominal pain, and vomiting, consider causes of GI infection, intestinal obstruction (e.g., intussusception or volvulus).
- Absence of abdominal pain may suggest a Meckel diverticulum, vascular malformations, coagulopathy, platelet disorder.

○ What are the stools like?

- If the blood is coating the stool, this localizes the bleeding usually to the anorectal region.
- If the blood is well mixed with the stools, the site of bleeding is usually more proximal than the rectum.
- Loose stools may be a sign of colitis (infective, inflammatory, drug induced).
- Association with constipation or difficulty passing stool may suggest anal fissures as the cause of the bleeding.

○ Is there fever?

- Fever, diarrhea, and abdominal pain may indicate an infectious enterocolitis or inflammatory bowel disease.

○ Is there any use of medication? A history of NSAID use may suggest NSAID-induced gastritis/ulcers.

○ Is the infant formula fed? Consider a cow's milk protein–induced colitis.

○ If the baby is breastfed, ask about the mother's diet. Rarely, a milk protein–induced colitis can occur when the mother is taking cow's milk.

○ If this is a premature baby, symptoms of vomiting, diarrhea, poor feeding, abdominal distension, or temperature instability may suggest NEC.

○ Is there a history of weight loss or poor weight gain? This may point to a chronic inflammatory condition such as inflammatory bowel disease.

○ Is there any associated rash over the limbs or joint pain? This may suggest HSP.

- Was there any trauma?
- Has the patient been critically ill? Consider stress ulcers/gastritis.
- Is there any contact history with anyone with similar symptoms?
- Is there any personal or family history of liver disease? This may suggest varices as the source of bleeding.
- Was there any other complication during the neonatal period and/or was an umbilical venous catheter inserted? These may predispose to portal vein thrombosis, subsequent portal hypertension, and esophageal varices.

- History taking: Look for complications

- Hypovolemia and anemia are the main complications.
- Symptoms of anemia include lethargy, decreased effort tolerance, postural dizziness, syncope, pallor, and palpitations.
- Symptoms of hypovolemia include postural dizziness, feeling cold and clammy, decreased urine output, syncope, and altered mental state.

- Do a physical examination

- Look for pallor; the presence suggests significant bleeding.
- Look for jaundice; the presence suggests the presence of liver disease.
- Examine the nose and the upper respiratory tract for any site of bleeding.
- Examine the mouth for any site of bleeding.

- Abdominal examination

- Scars
- Tenderness (infection, inflammation, intestinal obstruction)
- Distension (infection, inflammation, intestinal obstruction)
- Hepatosplenomegaly (consider liver disease, portal hypertension)
- Mass (consider intussusception, volvulus, tumor)
- Bowel sounds

  - Hyperactive (infection, inflammation, intestinal obstruction)

  - Absent (bowel ischemia, bowel perforation)

- Perianal examination

- Inspection of the perianal area may reveal fissures, fistulas, skin tags, skin breakdown, or evidence of trauma.
- Signs suggestive of child abuse include perianal tearing, irregularities in anal tone, and contour.
- Consider doing a digital per-rectal examination.

- Skin examination

- Petechiae and bruises suggest that there may be a coagulopathy or platelet disorder.
- Cutaneous hemangiomas may suggest gastrointestinal hemangiomatosis as a possible source of bleeding.
- Mucocutaneous telangiectasia may suggest HHT.
- A vasculitic rash may suggest HSP.

- Musculoskeletal examination

- Clubbing can be seen in inflammatory bowel disease.

■ Joint involvement can be seen in HSP, inflammatory bowel disease, and hemophilia.

○ Others: stigmata of chronic liver disease suggest varices or coagulopathy or thrombocytopenia.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- If the bleeding is acute and severe, there may be symptoms and signs of hypovolemic (hemorrhagic) shock, e.g., tachycardia, hypotension, poor perfusion, decreased urine output, altered mental status.
- If the bleeding is chronic, there may be symptoms and signs of anemia, e.g., lethargy, decreased effort tolerance, syncope, pallor, tachycardia.

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 14-year-old girl presents to you with three episodes of vomiting out a large amount of coffee ground-like material.

She has been having epigastric pain almost daily over the last 1 week, and the pain has been worsening. This is associated with nausea and a loss of appetite. Her pain is not improved or worsened by eating. There is no associated fever or diarrhea. She has been vomiting about once a day since the abdominal pain started, and this has been rather forceful in nature.

She does not have any significant past medical history of note. She has been growing and developing appropriately. She was last seen in the emergency department 2 weeks ago after a fall, where X-rays revealed a fractured right forearm. She was placed in a cast and discharged with ibuprofen for pain relief. She has been taking the ibuprofen regularly for management of her pain.

The remaining review of systems is negative. There is no travel history or contact with persons with similar complaints. There is also no history of abdominal trauma.

On examination, her temperature is 37.1°C, heart rate 110/min, respiratory rate 18/min, blood pressure 98/57 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air is 98%.

Her height is on the 75th percentile. Her weight is on the 25th percentile. She is pale but not jaundiced.

Heart rate is regular. On auscultation, S1 and S2 are heard. There are no additional heart sounds. On auscultation of the lungs, there are vesicular breath sounds. No crackles or wheezing are heard.

The abdomen is soft, with tenderness over the epigastrium. Rebound tenderness is negative. There is no mass felt. No organomegaly is noted. Bowel sounds are active. There are no stigmata of chronic liver disease.

Her peripheries are warm. Capillary refill is less than 2 seconds. Musculoskeletal examination is normal. Neurological examination is normal.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Acute upper GI bleed.

- What is your diagnosis?

- Gastritis/gastric ulcer, likely secondary to NSAID use.

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Coffee-ground vomitus
- Presence of epigastric pain
- Worsening of the abdominal pain with time
- Acute illness (good health previously, no significant past medical history, good growth, normal development)
- Recent history of NSAID use
- No symptoms or clinical signs to suggest chronic liver disease or portal hypertension

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- No improvement or worsening of pain with eating
- Forceful vomiting

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- Dose of ibuprofen she is taking
- Any use of antacids, proton-pump inhibitors or H2 antagonists
- Any melena present

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Mallory-Weiss tear

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:
- List the findings in this case that support this alternative diagnosis

- hematemesis following forceful vomiting

- List the findings in this case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Preceding 1 week of epigastric pain suggests that the primary pathology was in the gastric mucosa.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.
- None.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Acute upper GI bleed from gastritis secondary to NSAID use.

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Pallor and tachycardia on examination are suggestive of hypovolemia and anemia.

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Carry out a full blood count and a coagulation profile (prothrombin time, activated partial thromboplastin time, and INR) to assess the extent of bleeding, and any possible concomitant coagulopathy.
- Cross-match blood (depending on amount and rate of bleeding)
- Correct volume loss, first with intravenous crystalloids, and then with blood products when they become available
- Arrange for upper GI endoscopy.
- Escalate to senior.
