Chapter 6

# Blood in the Urine

Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF HEMATURIA

Hematuria is defined as

- >5 RBC/mm³ in fresh uncentrifuged midstream urine or >3 RBC/HPF (red blood cells per high power field) in fresh centrifuged midstream urine.
- Tested positive on at least two out of three occasions.

# SYMPTOMS OF HEMATURIA

- Macroscopic hematuria: blood in urine that can be seen by the naked eye. Urine is usually dark brown, coca-cola colored, smoky, or red in color.
- Microscopic hematuria: blood in urine that cannot be seen by the naked eye (i.e., urine looks normal color). Hematuria is usually detected when urine dipstick and/or urine microscopy is done.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

Blood is either from the glomerulus or from the non-glomerular structures

(e.g., renal parenchyma, urinary collecting tubules, collecting ducts, ureters, bladder, and urethra)

# 1. Glomerular Causes

# Causes

- Genetic: familial benign hematuria/thin basement membrane nephropathy, nonfamilial benign hematuria, Alport syndrome
- Immunological: Hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS) C3 glomerulopathy, poststreptococcal acute glomerulonephritis, IgA nephropathy
- Injury: interstitial nephritis
- Malformation: cystic renal disease
- Vascular: renal vein thrombosis

# Manifestations

- No voiding symptoms, e.g., dysuria, pain, urgency, frequency.
- May have evidence of glomerular dysfunction (i.e., fluid retention, hypertension, anemia, renal bone dystrophy, etc.).
- Red blood cells are dysmorphic.
- May have RBC casts, proteinuria.

74

## 2. Non-glomerular Causes

- Causes

- Factitious: blood added to urine
- Hematological: coagulopathy related
- Infection: urinary tract infection
- Injury: trauma, renal calculi
- Malignancy: Wilms tumor
- Metabolic: hypercalciuria
- Physiological: exercise induced, blood from menstruation

- Manifestations

- Some conditions may have voiding symptoms, e.g., dysuria, pain, urgency, frequency.
- No evidence of glomerular dysfunction.
- Red blood cells are isomorphic.
- There is no RBC cast, no proteinuria.

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

![img-14.jpeg](img-14.jpeg)

# 1. Is There Really Blood in the Urine?

- Review the color of the urine by history taking and do a visual inspection of the urine.
- Look at the results of urine dipstick and urine microscopy.
- If there is no hematuria: consider hemoglobinuria, myoglobinuria, beet root causing red urine.

# 2. Hematuria is Present. Determine whether It Is Glomerular or Non-glomerular

|  Parameter | Glomerular | Non-glomerular  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Voiding symptoms | Absent | May be present  |
|  Nature of urine | May be normal to the eye; if abnormal, the color is usually dark brown or coca-cola colored; the color abnormality is usually the same throughout the whole act of voiding | Urine may be bright red in color Color of urine may vary during the act of voiding  |
|  Evidence of fluid overload | May be present | Usually absent  |
|  Hypertension | May be present | Usually absent  |
|  Urine phase contrast | ≥20% of RBCs are dysmorphic | RBCs are isomorphic  |
|  Urine microscopy | RBC casts and/or proteinuria may be present | No RBC casts or proteinuria  |

# 3. If It Is Glomerular, Determine the Likely Cause

- Ask for the associated features.
- Is there a family history of hematuria or chronic kidney disease or kidney failure?
- Are there other features suggestive of an immune cause?

- Rash? Joint involvement? Hematological involvement?
- Preceding history of sore throat or skin infection?
- What are the complement levels and ASOT results?
- Is the blood IgA level elevated?

- Was there any recent intake of drugs that are known to cause interstitial nephritis, e.g., antibiotics?
- Is the patient known to have any renal malformation? Was there any renal imaging done?
- Is there a history of flank pain or back pain that is suggestive of renal vein thrombosis?
- Was there any renal biopsy done?

# 4. If It Is Non-glomerular, Determine the Likely Cause

- Ask for the associated features.
- If the patient is a female, is she having her menstruation?

- Is the blood in the urine related to exercise/strenuous physical activity?
- Is the patient on any drug that predisposes to bleeding? Anticoagulant? Antiplatelet?
- Does the patient have signs or symptoms suggestive of a bleeding disorder?
  - Easy bruising, prolonged bleeding, bleeds into joint, etc.
  - Little red dots suggestive of petechiae, etc.
- Is there any sign and symptom suggestive of a urinary tract infection, e.g., fever, flank pain, dysuria, frequency, foul-smelling urine?
- Is there a history of trauma to the back or flank?
- Is there a history of renal stones? Is there a family history of renal stones?
- Are there signs and symptoms of malignancy, e.g., loss of weight, loss of appetite, large mass?
- Did anyone add blood to the urine after sample collection?

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Glomerular

- Symptoms and signs of hypertension (headaches, visual disturbances, papilledema, seizures, elevated BP readings, etc., though most hypertension is asymptomatic)
- Symptoms and signs of fluid overload (puffiness, gain of weight, swollen feet, breathlessness, raised jugular venous pressure, enlarged liver, pitting edema, etc.)
- Symptoms and signs of uremia (lethargy, loss of appetite, elevated urea, elevated creatinine, etc.)
- Symptoms and signs of anemia

- Non-glomerular

- If the bleeding is acute and severe, there may be symptoms and signs of hypovolemia/shock
- If the bleeding is chronic, there may be symptoms and signs of anemia

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 8-year-old boy presents to you for the problem of dark-colored urine. He has no major medical history of note. He has been growing and developing appropriately. He was sick about 2 weeks ago with fever and sore throat. He did not see any doctor and only took paracetamol. The fever and sore throat resolved after 5 days.

Over the last 2 days, his urine has become dark in color. He says it looks like the color of coca-cola. He has not been passing as much urine as previously and reports that he has passed urine only one time in the last 12 hours. Over the past 2 days, his mother noticed that his face looked puffier. His shoes feel tighter than usual. There is no leg swelling or breathlessness or orthopnea.

He has a dull headache over the last 24 hours. He has had some central abdominal pain that comes and goes, with no obvious aggravating or relieving

factors. There is no skin rash or joint pains or mouth ulcers. He has some loss of appetite over the last 2 days. There is no frequency or urgency in urination. There is no painful urination or foul-smelling urine. There is no fever. He is not taking any medication. There is no history of trauma. The remaining review of systems is negative. There is no family history of similar complaints.

On examination, his temperature is 37.2°C, heart rate 102/min, respiratory rate 21/min, blood pressure 132/85 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air is 98%.

His height is on the 25th percentile. His weight is on the 50th percentile. He is not pale or jaundiced. Pupils are equal and reactive. Fundoscopy is normal. There is mild periorbital edema. His throat, nose, and ears are normal. His neck is supple. His jugular venous pressure was seen in the middle of the neck.

Heart rate is regular. On auscultation, S1 and S2 are heard. There are no additional heart sounds.

On auscultation of the lungs, there are vesicular breath sounds. The abdomen is soft and nontender. There is no mass felt. The liver is felt 2 cm below the right costal margin. Bowel sounds are normal. No organomegaly is noted. His peripheries are warm. Capillary refill is less than 2 seconds. No edema is noted in his legs, feet, or hands. No skin rashes or impetigo scars are seen. His genitalia are normal. No scrotal edema is noted. Musculoskeletal examination is normal. Neurological examination is normal.

A urine dipstick was done. The results are as follows: leukocytes negative, nitrite negative, urobilinogen negative, protein 3+, hemoglobin 4+, ketone positive, bilirubin negative, and glucose negative.

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

○ Macroscopic hematuria (dark/coca-cola-colored urine, presence of hemoglobin on urine dipstick).

• What is your diagnosis?

○ Glomerular cause; most likely poststreptococcal acute glomerulonephritis.

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

○ Presence of hematuria: dark/coca-cola-colored urine, presence of hemoglobin on urine dipstick

○ Suggestive of glomerular source

■ Dark/coca-cola-colored urine

■ Absence of voiding symptoms

■ Presence of proteinuria

■ Symptoms and signs of fluid retention (decrease in urine output, puffy face, tight shoes, periorbital edema, elevated jugular venous pressure hepatomegaly)

■ Symptoms and signs of hypertension (elevated BP, headache)

○ Acute illness (good health previously, good growth, normal development)

○ Predisposing factor for poststreptococcal acute glomerulonephritis: sore throat and fever 2 weeks ago, suggestive of strep throat, not treated by antibiotics

○ Absence of frequency, dysuria, current fever, trauma, coagulation medication, absence of abdominal mass

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

○ Central abdominal pain.

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

○ No information provided about whether RBCs in urine were dysmorphic or isomorphic

○ No information about pitting edema in the sacral region

○ No information about gain in weight

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ Symptoms and signs of mild fluid retention but not in overt pulmonary edema (decrease in urine output, puffy face, tight shoes, periorbital edema, elevated jugular venous pressure, hepatomegaly, not breathless, no orthopnea)

○ Symptoms and signs of mild hypertension but no hypertension emergency (elevated BP, headache, fundoscopy normal, no seizure)

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Order urine microscopy and phase contrast to confirm the presence of hematuria and to determine if the RBCs are isomorphic or dysmorphic. It will also help determine if there is the presence of casts.

○ Order urine protein/creatinine ratio to confirm proteinuria and to quantify the proteinuria.

○ Order urea, creatinine, and electrolytes to determine the renal function and to look for complications such as uremia, electrolyte abnormalities. If present, these will need to be managed.

○ Order the following tests to determine if it is truly poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis: serum complement levels, serum ASOT.

• Get the patient into negative fluid balance:

■ Restrict salt and water intake.
■ Give IV frusemide to improve urine output.
■ Put on strict input/output charting.
■ Record daily weight.

• Monitor blood pressure regularly and control blood pressure as needed.

• Urgent referral to nephrologist.

# Blood Pressure (High)

Perry Y.W. Lau

# DEFINITION OF HIGH BLOOD PRESSURE

Normal blood pressure (BP) in children and adolescents is

- Defined as systolic blood pressure (SBP) and diastolic blood pressure (DBP) that is less than 90th percentile for sex, age, and height.
- 90th percentile is defined by referring to the normative distribution of BP in healthy children (blood pressure tables found in National High Blood Pressure Education Program Working Group on High Blood Pressure in Children and Adolescents report¹).

Prehypertension in children and adolescents is

- Defined as an average SBP and/or DBP that is greater than or equal to the 90th percentile, but less than the 95th percentile for sex, age, and height.
- An indication of heightened risk for developing hypertension.
- An indication to recheck BP in 6 months.

Hypertension or high BP in children and adolescents is

- Defined as an average SBP and/or DBP that is greater than or equal to the 95th percentile for sex, age, and height on at least three separate occasions.

White coat hypertension is

- Defined as a condition in which the patient has BP readings that are above the 95th percentile when measured in a physician's office or clinic, whereas the average BP readings measured outside of a clinical setting are below the 90th percentile.

Hypertensive urgency is

- Defined as a significant elevation of BP without acute end-organ injury.
- SBP or DBP > (99th percentile + 5 mmHg) in patients 0–17 years old.
- SBP ≥ 160 mmHg or DBP ≥ 100 mmHg in patients ≥18 years old.

Hypertensive emergency is

- Defined as a significant elevation of both SBP and DBP with acute end-organ injury such as
  - Cerebral infarction (focal neurological deficit, altered mental state, seizure)
  - Cerebral hemorrhage (focal neurological deficit, altered mental state, seizure)
  - Encephalopathy (altered mental state, seizures)
  - Grade III–IV retinopathy (exudates, hemorrhage, papilledema)

82

- Myocardial ischemia (chest pain, dyspnea)
- Left heart failure and pulmonary edema (dyspnea, lung crepitations)
- Acute kidney injury (elevated serum creatinine, hematuria, proteinuria)

### SYMPTOMS OF HYPERTENSION

- Patients with hypertension are usually asymptomatic. Hence, surveillance should be put in place to detect asymptomatic hypertension.
- Surveillance

- Children ≥ 3 years old should have their BP measured at least once during every health care episode.

- Children < 3 years old with the following conditions should have their BP measured during every clinic review:

- History of prematurity, very low birth weight, or other neonatal complications requiring intensive care
- Congenital heart disease (surgically corrected or not)
- Recurrent urinary tract infection, hematuria, or proteinuria
- Known renal or urologic disorders
- Family history of renal disease, especially congenital renal disease
- Bone marrow or solid organ transplant
- Malignancy
- Treatment with drugs known to raise BP
- Systemic diseases associated with hypertension (neurofibromatosis, etc.)
- Evidence of raised intracranial pressure

- Less frequently, patients will have symptoms

- In infants, symptoms of hypertension can be very nonspecific such as growth failure, irritability, and feeding problems.
- Symptoms in older children: headache, blurred vision, epistaxis, nausea, vomiting, altered mental state, seizure, chest pain, dyspnea.

### PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

#### 1. Primary or Essential Hypertension

- Pathophysiology:

- Multifactorial in origin.
- Obesity, insulin resistance, activation of the renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system, renal sodium retention, impaired pressure natriuresis, activation of sympathetic nervous system, high serum uric acid levels, changes in vascular smooth muscle structure and reactivity, genetic factors and fetal programming have been implicated.

- Prevalence increases in parallel with increase in childhood obesity.

- Prevalence increases with age.

#### 2. Secondary Hypertension

- Defined as hypertension due to an organic cause.

- The age of the patient can give a clue. Infants and preschool-aged children are most likely to suffer from secondary hypertension, whereas older children and adolescents are more likely to be diagnosed with essential hypertension.
- BP is often markedly elevated at first presentation and is associated with the presence of other symptoms according to the underlying etiology.
- See the following table for the underlying etiologies of secondary hypertension.

|  Etiology of Secondary Hypertension | Suspect with the Following Clinical Features | Investigations Needed  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Renal Conditions:  |   |   |
|  Acute glomerulonephritis (AGN) | Hematuria, oliguria, fluid overload | Urine microscopy Serum creatinine  |
|  Hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS) | Renal failure, oliguria, hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, schistocytes on blood film | Serum creatinine, full blood count, peripheral blood film  |
|  Other causes of acute kidney injury (AKI) | Elevated serum creatinine level | Serum creatinine  |
|  Chronic kidney disease (CKD), e.g., polycystic kidney disease, chronic glomerulonephritis, and obstructive uropathy. | Proteinuria and/or hematuria Failure to thrive Ballotable kidneys in polycystic kidney disease or hydronephrosis secondary to obstructive uropathy Distended bladder may be seen in bladder outlet obstruction | Urine dipstick Serum creatinine Renal ultrasound (if indicated)  |
|  Renal artery stenosis | Renal bruit | Doppler renal ultrasound Captopril MAG3 or DMSA scan MR angiogram Renal angiogram  |
|  Renal artery thrombosis | Neonatal history of umbilical artery line placement | Doppler renal ultrasound  |
|  Renal vein thrombosis | Gross hematuria, oliguria, flank pain, thrombocytopenia, enlarged kidney Neonatal history of umbilical vein line placement Hypercoagulable conditions | Doppler renal ultrasound  |
|  Malignancy, e.g., mesoblastic nephroma, Wilms tumor, juxtaglomerular cell tumors | Fever, hematuria, or asymptomatic abdominal mass Increased risk of Wilms tumor in WAGR syndrome | Abdominal ultrasound CT or MRI abdomen  |

|  Etiology of Secondary Hypertension | Suspect with the Following Clinical Features | Investigations Needed  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  **Cardiac or Vascular Conditions:**  |   |   |
|  • Aortic coarctation | Decreased femoral pulses or radiofemoral delay | Echocardiogram  |
|  • Mid-aortic syndrome (defined as long segment narrowing of thoracoabdominal aorta with variable stenosis of primary aortic branches) | Decreased femoral pulses or radiofemoral delay Abdominal pain (mesenteric ischemia) Leg claudication Can be idiopathic or associated with neurocutaneous syndromes, Williams syndrome, Alagille syndrome Need to exclude inflammatory aortitis/vasculitis | Echocardiogram MR angiogram Angiography  |
|  • Vasculitis, e.g., Takayasu arteritis | Fever, malaise, weight loss in early stage Cold extremities or leg claudication in later stage Absent or diminished pulses | ESR MR angiogram  |
|  **Endocrine Conditions:**  |   |   |
|  • Pheochromocytoma or paraganglioma | Paroxysmal headache, sweating, tachycardia Weight loss | 24-h urine catecholamines Plasma catecholamines MIBG scan PET-CT scan  |
|  • Neuroblastoma | Fever, weight loss, irritability Abdominal mass | Urinary vanillylmandelic acid and homovanillic acid Abdominal ultrasound CT or MRI abdomen  |
|  • Hyperthyroidism | Goiter | Thyroid function  |
|  • Cushing syndrome | Truncal obesity with characteristics features such as moon face, buffalo hump | 24-h urine free cortisol Serum cortisol  |
|  • Mineralocorticoid excess, e.g., Conn syndrome | Hypokalemia, metabolic alkalosis Low renin, high aldosterone | Plasma renin and aldosterone  |
|  • Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH): 11-β-hydroxylase deficiency | Hypokalemia Signs of androgen overproduction (virilization of external genital in female, early puberty in male, acne) Low renin, low aldosterone | Plasma renin and aldosterone Urinary steroid profile analysis  |
|  • Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH): 17-α-hydroxylase deficiency | Hypokalemia Inadequate virilization (ambiguous genital in male, delayed puberty in female) Low renin, low aldosterone | Plasma renin and aldosterone  |

Continued

|  Etiology of Secondary Hypertension | Suspect with the Following Clinical Features | Investigations Needed  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  **Neurologic Conditions:**  |   |   |
|  • Raised intracranial pressure (ICP) | Headache, vomiting, altered mental state, ocular palsies, papilledema, widened pulse pressure, bradycardia | CT or MRI brain  |

|  **Genetic Conditions (Monogenic Hypertension):**  |   |   |
|  • Apparent mineralocorticoid excess | Hypokalemia, low birth weight, failure to thrive, nephrocalcinosis Low renin, low aldosterone | Plasma renin and aldosterone Urinary steroid profile analysis Genetic test  |
|  • Glucocorticoid remediable aldosteronism | Hypokalemia, family history of early-onset hypertension Low renin, high aldosterone | Plasma renin and aldosterone Urinary steroid profile analysis Genetic test  |

### CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

#### 1. Is there really Hypertension?

- What was the BP reading?
- How was it taken? Was the appropriate arm and an appropriate BP cuff size used?

- Right arm is preferred for comparison to standard BP tables.
- Appropriate BP cuff size should have

  - Inflatable bladder width that is at least 40% of arm circumference at a point midway between olecranon and acromion.

  - Cuff bladder length should cover 80%–100% of circumference of arm.

- Was the patient agitated during BP measurement?

  - Ideally the child should be sitting quietly for 5 min before BP measurement, seated with his or her back supported, feet on the floor, and arm supported with cubital fossa at the heart level.

- Repeat BP measurement if the prior BP reading was taken when the child was agitated.
- Note that a child can be agitated because the high BP is causing the agitation; do not always assume that agitation caused the high BP.

# • Was the patient anxious?

- If anxiety is the cause of the high BP, subsequent measurements tend to show a declining trend toward normal as a result of reduction of anxiety.
- If a home BP machine is available, get parents to measure BP at home (but need to check if the cuff size is appropriate and if the machine is correctly calibrated).
- 24-h ambulatory BP monitoring can be used to evaluate if it is persistent elevation of blood pressure or transient elevation of blood pressure.

# • Was the patient in pain?

- If so, repeat BP measurement when the patient is not in pain.

# • Was the patient fitting?

- If so, repeat BP measurement when the patient is not fitting.

# • Was the BP ≥ 95th percentile for sex, age, and height on repeated measurements?

- If the initial BP was high and the subsequent BP is normal, this is likely secondary to anxiety or pain. If the child has no other symptoms, monitor over weeks and months. If a home BP machine is available, get parents to measure BP at home. 24-h ambulatory BP monitoring can be used.
- If the repeated BP measurements are high, assess if the patient has hypertensive emergency or hypertensive urgency.

# 2. History Taking: Is This a Hypertensive Emergency or Hypertensive Urgency?

# • Is there a significant elevation of BP?

- Significant elevation is defined as

- SBP or DBP > (99th percentile + 5 mmHg) in patients 0–17 years old.
- SBP ≥ 160 mmHg or DBP ≥ 100 mmHg in patients ≥18 years old.

# • Is there any symptom suggestive of possible acute end-organ injury?

- Altered mental state
- Seizures
- Headache
- Blurring of vision
- Chest pain
- Breathlessness
- Leg swelling
- Blood in urine, change in urine color
- Protein in urine, frothy urine

# 3. History Taking: What Is the Cause of Hypertension? Is it Primary/Essential Hypertension or Secondary Hypertension?

- Ask for associated symptoms.
- Do a good systems review.

• The following list shows some of the more likely symptoms and their possible associations.

○ Weight

■ Overweight/obese (essential hypertension, Cushing syndrome)
■ Recent gain in weight: suspect fluid overload (CKD, AGN, other causes of AKI)
■ Weight loss (hyperthyroidism, autoimmune condition, pheochromocytoma, malignancy)

○ Height

■ Poor height velocity (CKD, Cushing syndrome)

○ Fever

■ Recurrent fevers suggest an inflammatory cause, e.g., SLE, Takayasu.
■ Recent fever suggests possible infection-related conditions such as AGN, HUS.

○ Headache (raised ICP, pheochromocytoma)

○ Headache and vomiting (raised ICP)

○ Facial puffiness due to fluid overload (CKD, AGN, other causes of AKI)

○ Pallor (autoimmune conditions, HUS, CKD)

○ Neck swelling: goiter (hyperthyroidism)

○ Palpitations (hyperthyroidism, pheochromocytoma, pain, anxious)

○ Abdominal pain (mid-aortic syndrome)

○ Abdominal mass (hydronephrosis, polycystic kidney, distended bladder, pheochromocytoma, neuroblastoma)

○ Abdominal swelling (fluid overload, fat, abdominal mass)

○ Ambiguous genitalia (CAH)

○ Precocious puberty (CAH)

○ Menses irregular (anovulatory cycle, CAH, polycystic ovarian syndrome)

○ Urine output

■ Decreased (CKD, AGN, HUS, other causes of AKI)
■ Frequent urination (CKD)
■ Nocturia (CKD)

○ Red urine or blood detected in urine (AGN, HUS, renal vein thrombosis)

○ Protein detected in urine (AGN, HUS, other causes of AKI, CKD)

○ Neurological deficits (space-occupying lesion in the brain causing raised ICP, vasculitis related, e.g., SLE, Takayasu)

○ Skin

■ Petechiae (SLE, HUS)
■ Purpuric rashes (vasculitis)
■ Café au lait macules (NF-1)
■ Hirsutism (CAH, Cushing, polycystic ovarian syndrome)
■ Sweating (hyperthyroidism, pheochromocytoma)
■ Sweating and flushing (pheochromocytoma)

- Joint pain and swelling (autoimmune conditions)
- Leg swelling due to fluid overload (AGN, HUS, CKD, other causes of AKI)
- Leg pain or claudication (mid-aortic syndrome)
- Muscle cramps or weakness (hypokalemia due to hyperaldosteronism)
- Recent illness

- Sore throat (AGN)
- Skin infection (AGN)
- Diarrhea (HUS)

- Past medical history

- Congenital heart disease
- UTI, renal malformation, renal disorders
- Neonatal history of umbilical artery or umbilical vein catheterization (renal artery or renal vein thrombosis)
- Known hypercoagulable condition (e.g., nephrotic syndrome) that puts the patient at risk of renal vein thrombosis

- Medication use:

- Steroids, cyclosporine, caffeine overdose, decongestant (ephedrine, pseudoephedrine, amphetamine, etc.)
- Liquorice
- Illicit drug, e.g., ecstasy, cocaine
- Is the hypertension easy to control? If it is not controlled with two or more antihypertensive drugs, this is likely a secondary cause of hypertension.

- Other cardiovascular risk factors and comorbidities:

- Unhealthy diet
- Sedentary lifestyle
- Smoking
- Alcohol
- Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA):

- Sleep history, habitual snoring, observed apnea, daytime somnolence
- Sleep study results if previously done

- Family history

- Hypertension
- Heart disease
- Hyperlipidemia
- CKD, renal issues
- Autoimmune conditions

# 4. Physical Examination

- Carry out a complete head-to-toe examination.
- There are many possible findings. The following list shows some of the more likely findings and their possible associations.

- Growth parameters

- Overweight/obese (essential hypertension, Cushing syndrome)
- Truncal obesity (Cushing syndrome)
- Buffalo hump (Cushing syndrome)
- Thin (hyperthyroidism, autoimmune conditions, pheochromocytoma, malignancy)
- Poor height velocity (CKD, Cushing syndrome)

- Altered conscious state (raised ICP, hypertensive emergency)
- Cushing triad consisting of bradycardia, hypertension, and decreased respiratory rate (severe raised ICP with impending coning)
- Dysmorphism (syndromes such as William syndrome)
- Pallor (autoimmune conditions, HUS, CKD)
- Periorbital edema (AGN, HUS, other causes of AKI, CKD, heart failure in hypertensive emergency)
- Thyroid eye signs (hyperthyroidism)
- Mouth breathing (OSA)
- Enlarged tonsils (OSA)
- Goiter (hyperthyroidism)
- Elevated jugular venous pressure (AGN, HUS, other causes of AKI, CKD, heart failure in hypertensive emergency)
- Cardiovascular system

- Pulses

  - Decreased peripheral pulses (Takayasu).

  - Radiofemoral delay (aortic coarctation).

- Carry out a four-limb BP measurement. Look for SBP difference (>10 mmHg) between arms; look to see if upper limb BP is >10 mmHg higher than lower limb BP (aortic coarctation).
- Displaced apex beat, 3rd/4th heart sounds (heart failure in hypertensive emergency).

- Respiratory system

  - Crepitations (pulmonary edema in hypertensive emergency)

- Abdominal exam

- Enlarged liver (AGN, HUS, other causes of AKI, CKD, heart failure in hypertensive emergency)
- Ballotable kidney (polycystic kidney, hydronephrosis, renal vein thrombosis)
- Other masses (pheochromocytoma, neuroblastoma)
- Palpable bladder in obstructive uropathy
- Renal bruit (renal artery stenosis)

- Genitalia

  - Ambiguous genitalia or precocious puberty (CAH)

- Skin

- Petechiae (SLE, HUS)
- Purpuric rashes (vasculitis)
- Café au lait macules, axillary and groin freckling, neurofibromas (NF-1)

- Hirsutism (CAH, Cushing syndrome, polycystic ovarian syndrome)
- Sweating (hyperthyroidism, pheochromocytoma)
- Sweating and flushing (pheochromocytoma)
- Xanthelasma (hyperlipidemia)
- Acanthosis nigricans (diabetes mellitus)
- Striae (obesity, Cushing syndrome)
- Hirsutism (CAH, Cushing, polycystic ovarian syndrome)

# • Musculoskeletal

- Joint pain and swelling (autoimmune conditions)
- Lower limb edema (AGN, HUS, other causes of AKI, CKD, heart failure in hypertensive emergency)

# • Neurological

- Blurred vision (hypertensive emergency)
- Neurological deficit (space-occupying lesion in the brain causing raised ICP, vasculitis related, e.g., SLE, Takayasu)
- Muscle cramps or weakness (hypokalemia due to hyperaldosteronism)
- Fundoscopy

  - Chronic changes of hypertension

  - Papilledema (hypertensive emergency, raised ICP)

![img-15.jpeg](img-15.jpeg)

5. Algorithm

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

• The following are situations where there is a need for immediate pharmacologic antihypertensive treatment and admission:

• Hypertensive urgency
• Hypertensive emergency
• Neurological symptoms and signs (altered mental state, seizures, acute neurological deficits)
• Visual disturbances (blurring of vision, papilledema)
• Symptoms of myocardial ischemia (chest pain, dyspnea)
• Symptoms and signs of fluid overload (puffiness, gain of weight, breathlessness, raised jugular venous pressure, lung crepitations, enlarged liver, pitting edema, etc.)
• Symptoms and signs of acute glomerulonephritis (gross hematuria, oliguria, fluid overload)

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 15-year-old girl presents to you for the problem of headache for 1 day. She describes the headache as a pressure all over the head, not relieved by paracetamol. This headache is associated with giddiness and facial flushing. There is no vomiting, drowsiness, or weakness of limbs. She had a fever and sore throat 2 days prior to this presentation of headache. There is no cough or running nose.

She has had intermittent headaches since past 1 year but never as severe as this episode. Her previous headaches were relieved by paracetamol and sleep. For the past few months, she can sometimes feel warm and has sweating even in an air-conditioned room. She has been growing and developing appropriately. Her height has been on the 75th percentile, and weight on the 50th percentile. She had a fainting spell at the age of 8 years, and electroencephalogram (EEG) and MRI of the brain done at that time were found normal. Her father has hypertension. Her mother was recently diagnosed with hyperthyroidism. Her maternal aunt has Graves' disease.

On examination, her temperature is 37.2°C, heart rate 140/min, respiratory rate 16/min, BP 220/130 mmHg, and Glasgow Coma Scale of 15. She is not pale or jaundiced. Pupils are equal and reactive. Fundoscopy shows papilledema and retinal hemorrhages. Her throat, nose, and ears are normal. Her jugular venous pressure is seen at the base of her neck. Heart rate is regular. On auscultation, S1 and S2 are heard. There are no additional heart sounds. On auscultation of the lungs, there are vesicular breath sounds. The abdomen is soft and nontender. There are no masses or renal bruits. No edema is noted in her legs, feet, or hands. Neurological examination is normal.

On admission, her blood tests show sodium 141 mmol/L, potassium 3.0 mmol/L, chloride 100 mmol/L, bicarbonate 28 mmol/L, urea 4.5 mmol/L, and creatinine 58 μmol/L.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Hypertensive emergency (severe elevation of BP, headache, papilledema, and retinopathy)

- What is your diagnosis?

- Hyperthyroidism

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Severe elevation of BP
- Tachycardia
- Heat intolerance
- Family history of hyperthyroidism or Graves' disease
- Hypokalemia

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Presence of flushing.
- Blood pressure is usually not so high in hyperthyroidism.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information about hand tremors and bowel habits, e.g., diarrhea
- No information about weight loss
- No information about goiter
- No information about eye signs that are seen in Graves' disease

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Pheochromocytoma

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Tachycardia, hypertension, facial flushing, heat intolerance (sympathetic overactivity); intermittent headache and heat intolerance

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- None

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Insufficient information to distinguish between hyperthyroidism and pheochromocytoma

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ She has hypertensive emergency; severe elevation of BP and evidence of end-organ damage in the eye.

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Admit the patient to an intensive care unit or high dependency ward.
○ Escalate to senior.
○ Lower her BP with intravenous antihypertensive medication.
○ Investigate for the secondary cause of hypertension:

■ Thyroid function test
■ 24-h urine catecholamines
■ Plasma catecholamines
■ Plasma renin and aldosterone

○ Investigate for other end-organ injury:

■ ECG and 2D echocardiogram
■ Urine dipstick for proteinuria

○ In this patient, thyroid function test was normal. Her 24-h urine catecholamines were elevated. PET scan suggested a paraganglioma at the hilum of her right kidney. The paraganglioma caused compression of the right renal artery, resulting in secondary hyperaldosteronism (elevated plasma renin and aldosterone) with hypokalemia. Her hypertension was controlled with phenoxybenzamine and propanolol before she underwent surgery to remove the paraganglioma tumor. After removal, her BP was normal even without antihypertensive treatment. This case shows the importance of a thorough search for the secondary cause of hypertension.

# Reference

1. National High Blood Pressure Education Program Working Group on High Blood Pressure in Children and Adolescents. The fourth report on the diagnosis, evaluation, and treatment of high blood pressure in children and adolescents. Pediatrics 2004 Aug; 114(2 Suppl 4th Report): 555–576.

# Blood Pressure (Low)

Denise L.M. Goh • Nicola S.P. Ngiam

# DEFINITION OF LOW BLOOD PRESSURE (HYPOTENSION)

Hypotension or low blood pressure in children is defined as

- Blood pressure (BP) that is less than the 5th percentile or below two standard deviations (SDs) of the mean for sex and age or
- Systolic BP < 70 + (age in years × 2) or
- Mean arterial pressure < 40 + (age in years × 1.5) (reference: Haque and Zaritsky¹).

All BP readings need to be interpreted with respect to adequacy of cardiac output to determine their significance.

# SYMPTOMS AND SIGNS OF LOW BLOOD PRESSURE (HYPOTENSION)

- Manifestations related to poor cardiac output causing poor peripheral perfusion
  - Skin: skin mottling, cold and clammy peripheries, reduced central capillary refill time
  - Weak peripheral pulses (exception: distributive shock may cause bounding pulses from widened pulse pressure)
- Manifestations related to poor cardiac output causing end-organ dysfunction
  - General: feeling unwell or weak
  - Brain: lethargy, headaches, dizziness, light-headedness, confusion, postural giddiness, syncope, blurring of vision, seizure
  - Renal: reduced urine output
  - Gastrointestinal (GI): abdominal pain
- Manifestations related to compensatory response to poor cardiac output
  - Tachycardia
  - Cold and clammy peripheries

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- BP is a function of cardiac output and systemic vascular resistance.

- The factors that determine BP are

  - Ventricular preload:

    - This determines the amount of ventricular filling.
    - It is affected by venous compliance and total blood volume.
    - A decrease in venous compliance, e.g., when the veins constrict, increases venous return to the heart, which then increases ventricular filling.

96

- Total blood volume is dependent on fluid intake, renal function, neurohormonal factors, and fluid losses.
- The greater the preload, the greater the length to which the cardiomyocyte is stretched, the greater the force of contraction (but only up to a certain point of stretching, i.e., Frank-Starling Law). Too much stretch and the force of the contraction decreases.

○ Cardiac contractility:

- This force of contraction of a cardiomyocyte is dependent on whether the cell is normal or not, as well as Frank-Starling Law.

○ Heart rate:

- This is determined by the state of the conducting system and neurohumoral factors.

○ Afterload:

- This is the force against which the ventricles must pump against to eject blood (i.e., systemic vascular resistance).
- It is mostly affected by the systemic vascular tone, but is also affected by blood viscosity, aortic/systemic vascular structure, or presence of intraluminal thrombosis.

- Hypotension occurs when the body is not able to maintain BP despite compensatory responses.

- Hypotension is the end-point of one of many pathological processes. These processes include

- Reduced preload (less blood returning to the heart, so the heart has not enough blood to pump out, hence reduced cardiac output) = hypovolemic shock
- Reduced myocardial contractility or abnormal heart rhythm (heart does not have enough power to pump out blood adequately, hence reduced cardiac output) = cardiogenic shock
- Reduced after load (reduction in systemic vascular resistance) = distributive shock
- Increased afterload (heart is pumping but does not have enough power to overcome the obstruction against outflow, hence reduced cardiac output) = obstructive shock

# 1. Hypovolemic Shock

• Causes

○ Inadequate intake
○ Loss of fluid

- Gastrointestinal losses, e.g., diarrhea or vomiting
- Renal losses, e.g., chronic kidney disease with polyuria, diabetes insipidus, poorly controlled diabetes mellitus, diabetes ketoacidosis
- Skin losses, e.g., burns, heat stroke
- Losses into interstitial space, e.g., capillary leak with sepsis, hypoalbuminemia

o Loss of blood

■ Internal hemorrhage (intra-abdominal, pelvis, pleural space, etc.)
■ Gastrointestinal bleed
■ External hemorrhage (wounds, etc.)

# 2. Cardiogenic Shock

• Causes

o Arrhythmias
o Impairment of cardiac muscle contraction

■ Congenital/genetic – cardiomyopathy, heart failure due to congenital heart disease
■ Acquired

□ Sepsis causing reduced myocardial function
□ Myocarditis
□ Cardiomyopathy, e.g., previous myocarditis, drug-induced (anthracyclines)
□ Traumatic cardiac contusion
□ Postoperative – after cardiopulmonary bypass

# 3. Distributive Shock

• Causes

o Anaphylaxis: when it causes peripheral vasodilation
o Septic shock: when it causes peripheral vasodilation
o Neurogenic shock from acute high spinal cord injury: when it causes loss of vascular tone, leading to peripheral vasodilation and peripheral venous pooling

# 4. Obstructive Shock

• Causes

o Tension pneumothorax
o Pulmonary embolism
o Cardiac tamponade
o Left ventricular outflow tract obstruction, e.g., severe coarctation of the aorta in the neonatal period

![img-16.jpeg](img-16.jpeg)

Continued on next page

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

Continued from previous page

![img-17.jpeg](img-17.jpeg)

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance.
- Assess work of breathing.
- Assess circulation to skin (color).

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

- Check pulse

- Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
- Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

- When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

- If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

# 3. Is there Evidence of Low Blood Pressure?

- Check against the norms to determine if BP is low.
  - Check if BP is less than the 5th percentile or below two standard deviations (SDs) of the mean for sex and age, or
  - Check if systolic BP < 70 + (age in years × 2), or
  - Check if mean arterial pressure < 40 + (age in years × 1.5)
- See the table below for normal ranges for vital signs (reference: AHA Pediatric Advanced Life Support Provider Manual²)

|  Age | Heart Rate (per Min) | Respiratory Rate (per Min) | Systolic BP (mmHg)  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Infant (1 month to 1 year) | 100–190 | 30–53 | 72–104  |
|  Toddler (1–2 years) | 98–140 | 22–37 | 86–106  |
|  Preschool (3–5 years) | 80–120 | 20–28 | 89–112  |
|  School-age (6–11 years) | 75–118 | 18–25 | 97–120  |
|  Adolescent (12–15 years) | 60–100 | 12–20 | 110–131  |

#### 4. Is there Evidence of Poor Cardiac Output?

- Look for evidence of impaired peripheral perfusion

  - Weak pulses, capillary refill > 2 seconds, cool peripheries, mottled skin

- Look for evidence of impaired organ perfusion

  - Altered mental state
  - Oliguria (<1 mL/kg/h)
  - Abdominal pain
  - Metabolic acidosis
  - Lactic acidosis

- Look for evidence of compensatory response

  - Tachycardia, peripheral vasoconstriction

- If there is no evidence of poor cardiac output

  - Check to see if the BP measurement technique is reliable and accurate (e.g., BP cuff not too big).
  - Repeat measurement.
  - If the repeat measurements are low and there is no evidence of poor cardiac output, them BP reading might be normal for that particular child; monitor closely.

- If there is evidence of poor cardiac output or compensatory response, determine the cause of the poor cardiac output.

#### 5. What is the Cause of the Poor Cardiac Output?

SIGNS AND CAUSES OF SHOCK

|  Pulse Pressure^{a} | Normal or Narrow | Normal or Narrow | Narrow | Wide  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Peripheries | Cool | Cool | Cool | Warm  |
|  JVP | Not raised | Raised or normal | Raised | Not raised  |
|  Liver size | Not enlarged | Enlarged if there is right heart failure | Enlarged if there is right heart failure | Not enlarged  |
|  Evidence of predisposing conditions | Dehydration, trauma, blood loss (internal or external) | Trauma to chest, risk factors for pneumothorax, risk factors for pulmonary embolism | Infection, heart disease, preceding viral illness, arrhythmia | Infection, anaphylaxis, trauma to spinal cord  |
|  Cause of poor cardiac output | Hypovolemic shock | Obstructive shock | Cardiogenic shock | Distributive shock  |

$^{a}$Pulse pressure, difference between systolic and diastolic BP (normal, 30–40 mmHg; wide, >40 mmHg; narrow, <20 mmHg).

- Symptoms and signs suggesting hypovolemic shock

  - Signs

    - The pulse pressure is normal or narrow.
    - The peripheries are cold.
    - The JVP is not raised.
    - The liver is not enlarged.

○ Look for the cause of the hypovolemic shock

■ Is there a history of inadequate intake?
■ Is there a history of increased losses?

□ GI losses:

- Vomiting, diarrhea, hidden gastrointestinal losses, e.g., ileus
- What is the cause of the vomiting? See Chapter 50 "Vomiting."
- What is the cause of the diarrhea? See Chapter 21 "Diarrhea."

□ Urinary losses:

- Polyuria, nocturia
- What is the cause of the polyuria or nocturia? See Chapter 33 "Frequent Urination."

□ Skin loss, e.g., burns, heat-related illness, scalded skin syndrome
□ Interstitial losses:

- The patient is swollen, edematous, gained weight, but has low intravascular volume.
- What is the cause of the edema? See Chapter 23 "Edema."

■ Is there evidence of dehydration?

□ Dry mucous membranes
□ Sunken eyes
□ Sunken fontanelles (if applicable)
□ Lack of tears
□ Poor skin turgor
□ What is the cause of the dehydration? See Chapter 19 "Dehydration."

■ Is there evidence of hemorrhage?

☐ For example, blood seen, signs of external injury, signs of internal injury (see Chapter 14 "Bruising and Bleeding" and Chapter 37 "Injuries").
☐ Pallor due to anemia may not be present in acute bleed; anemia may be seen if bleeding is subacute or chronic.
☐ Pallor may be seen due to peripheral vasoconstriction.

• Symptoms and signs of cardiogenic shock

○ Signs

■ The peripheries are cold.
■ The pulse pressure is normal or narrow.
■ The JVP is raised if there is right heart failure.
■ The liver is enlarged if there is right heart failure.
■ The apex beat is displaced if there is cardiomegaly.
■ Pitting edema is present if there is right heart failure.
■ Lung crepitations are present if there is left heart failure.

○ Are there symptoms of heart failure?

■ Effort intolerance, e.g., taking a long time to complete a bottle feed
■ Shortness of breath, exertional dyspnea
Cough
■ Sweating worsened by exertion
■ Orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea
■ Leg swelling

# • Look for the cause of the cardiogenic shock

■ Is there a history consistent with acute myocarditis?

□ Preceding viral illness

□ Lethargy

□ Insidious onset

□ Tachy/bradyarrhythmia (myocarditis may be associated with arrhythmias; the arrhythmias are likely to be picked up by the doctor; it is seldom a presenting complaint as the child may not recognize the symptom)

■ Is there a history suggestive of arrhythmia/palpitation?

■ Is there a history of congenital heart disease, cardiomyopathy?

■ Is there a history of sepsis? Trauma to thorax? Post-bypass?

■ Is there a history of drug use, e.g., anthracycline chemotherapy drugs?

■ Any family history of sudden death, heart disease, cardiomyopathy, arrhythmia?

# • Symptoms and signs suggesting distributive shock

# • Signs

■ The pulse pressure is widened.

■ The peripheries are warm.

■ The jugular venous pressure (JVP) is not raised.

■ The liver is not enlarged.

# • Look for the cause of the distributive shock

■ Is there evidence of sepsis?

□ Fever

□ Source of infection (see Chapter 28 "Fever (Acute)" and Chapter 29 "Fever (Recurrent, Persistent, or Prolonged)

□ Predisposition to sepsis, e.g., neutropenia

■ Is there evidence of anaphylaxis?

□ Periorbital swelling

□ Angioedema

□ Urticaria

□ Shortness of breath, wheezing

□ Feeling faint or syncope

□ Abdominal pain

□ Pallor

□ What is the potential allergen/trigger? (unidentifiable in up to 50% of cases)

■ Is there evidence of acute spinal injury?

□ Acute neurological deficit

□ Hypotension may be associated with inappropriate bradycardia because of autonomic dysfunction.

# • Symptoms and signs of obstructive shock

# • Signs

■ The peripheries are cold.

■ The pulse pressure is normal or narrow.

■ The JVP is raised if there is right heart outflow tract obstruction.

■ The liver is enlarged if there is right heart outflow tract obstruction.

○ Look for the cause of the obstructive shock

■ Unilateral chest pain, respiratory distress, hyper-resonance to percussion, reduced air entry, trachea deviated away from the affected side, displaced apex beat: tension pneumothorax

■ Risk factors for thromboembolism (e.g., central venous catheter, prolonged bed rest), hypoxia, cough, chest pain: pulmonary embolism

■ Muffled heart sounds, cannot feel apex beat well, low voltages on ECG: cardiac tamponade

■ Weak femoral pulses, radio-femoral delay, higher BP in the arms than the legs: coarctation of aorta

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

• Evidence of impaired peripheral perfusion

○ Weak pulses, capillary refill > 2 seconds, cool peripheries, mottled skin

• Evidence of impaired organ perfusion

○ Altered mental state

○ Oliguria (<1 mL/kg/h)

○ Abdominal pain

○ Metabolic acidosis

○ Lactic acidosis

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 18-month-old boy presents with ulcers in the mouth and rash over his palms and soles for 3 days. He has become more lethargic today. His mother thinks that he looks off color and is not his usual self.

He has been feeding poorly over the last 3 days because of his mouth ulcers. Over the last few days, he is barely eating and only drinks 60–90 mL of milk each time. He has sips of water through the day. He has not had anything to drink or eat today. Today, he is tired and lies down all the time. His urine output has significantly reduced and has become dark in color. His last urine output was 8 hours ago. The rash on his hands and feet has looked the same over the last 3 days and is not causing him too much discomfort. He does not suffer from any vomiting, diarrhea, cough, or fever. There is no contact history. He does not have any siblings. He does not go to childcare. His birth history was unremarkable. His immunizations are up to date.

On examination, he is lethargic and slightly pale. His temperature is 37.2°C. Heart rate is 161/min, respiratory rate is 38/min, and blood pressure is 70/50 mmHg. Oxygen saturation in room air is 97%. His

peripheries are cold. Peripheral pulses are weak and the capillary refill time is 3 seconds. There is a vesicular rash over his palms and soles. He has multiple shallow ulcers in his mouth and pharynx. His oral mucosa is dry. His eyes are not sunken, and skin turgor is normal. The apex beat is felt in the fifth left intercostal space, 1 cm lateral to the mid-clavicular line. Heart sounds are normal and there are no additional heart sounds or murmurs. Auscultation of the lungs reveals bibasal crepitations. He has mild subcostal retractions. The abdomen is soft and not distended. The liver edge is felt 3 cm below the right costal margin. The spleen is not enlarged and the kidneys are not ballotable. Pupils are equal and reactive. Neurological examination is normal. Neck is supple.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Hypotension with impaired peripheral perfusion and end-organ dysfunction.

- What is your diagnosis?

- Cardiogenic shock secondary to acute myocarditis from hand, foot, and mouth disease

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- BP readings are below norms
- Evidence of impaired peripheral perfusion (peripheries are cold, poor capillary refill, weak peripheral pulses)
- Evidence of impaired organ function (tired, lethargic, not his usual self, lies down all the time, decreased urine output)
- Symptoms of decreased effort tolerance and pulmonary congestion (poor feeding and breathlessness)
- Signs suggestive of heart failure:

- Displaced apex beat
- Bibasal crepitations (not likely to be infective as no fever)

- Hepatomegaly
- Narrow pulse pressure

- Presence of a viral illness (hand, foot, and mouth disease) where myocarditis is a possible complication.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Reduced fluid intake and signs of dehydration suggest hypovolemia as a cause of hypotension.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information on JVP (but this might be hard to see in a young child)
- No information provided on effort required during feeding
- No information on presence of sweating more than usual

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Hypovolemic shock from dehydration (poor feeding).

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Significantly reduced oral intake with a clear cause for reduced intake (oral ulcers)
- Dry mucous membranes

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Displaced apex beat
- Evidence of pulmonary congestion (bibasal crepitations), enlarged liver
- Absence of sunken eyes and reduced skin turgor (which suggests that dehydration may not be severe)

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Should have more signs of dehydration such as sunken eyes, reduced skin turgor.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Cardiogenic shock secondary to acute myocarditis as a result of hand, foot, and mouth disease.
- Hypovolemia may be a contributing factor.

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- There is evidence of impaired peripheral perfusion.
- There is evidence of impaired organ function.
- If it is myocarditis, patient is at risk for worsening heart failure and/or arrhythmia, which are potentially life-threatening.

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Call for help from a senior doctor immediately.
- Obtain venous access.
- Carry out tests to look for evidence of impaired perfusion:

- Capillary or arterial blood gas for metabolic acidosis.
- Urea and electrolytes for acute kidney injury.
- Liver function test for acute liver injury.
- Hemoglobin level, which may need to be optimized for management of shock.
- Consider carrying out a lactate level.
- Carry out tests to confirm acute myocarditis and heart failure:

- Electrocardiogram.
- Cardiac enzymes (creatine kinase, creatine kinase MB subunit, troponin-I).

☐ Chest X-ray.
☐ Refer to the cardiologist for 2D echocardiography.

■ Consult a senior doctor about careful fluid resuscitation with regular reassessment for response.

■ Consider inotropic support early.

■ Transfer the patient to the pediatric intensive care unit or high dependency when deemed stable for transport.

■ Institute infection control procedures (e.g., the patient needs to be put into isolation, droplet precaution, contact precaution)

■ Monitor vital signs continuously.

■ Watch out for arrhythmias and worsening heart function.

■ May need to inform health authorities, school.

■ May need to screen other siblings.

# References

1. Haque IU, Zaritsky AL. Analysis of the evidence for the lower limit of systolic and mean arterial pressure in children. Pediatr Crit Care Med 2007 Mar; 8(2): 138–144.
