# Blood Sugar (High)

Loke Kah Yin

# DEFINITION OF HIGH BLOOD SUGAR

- High blood sugar level or hyperglycemia refers to a blood glucose level that is above the normal range.
- The normal fasting blood glucose level (BGL) after ≥8 hours of fasting is between 4.0 and 6.0 mmol/L.
- The normal blood glucose 2 hours after eating can range up to 7.8 mmol/L.
- The oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) is used to assess the presence of an abnormal level of blood glucose in response to taking 75 g of glucose.
- The following table defines the blood glucose levels in an individual with normal glucose metabolism, impaired fasting glucose, impaired glucose tolerance, and overt diabetes mellitus. The glucose levels are provided in mmol/L.

|  Blood Glucose Level | Random BGL | Fasting BGL | BGL 2 hours post 75g OGTT  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Normal | <11.1 mmol/L | <6.1 mmol/L | <7.8 mmol/L  |
|  Impaired fasting glucose | <11.1 mmol/L | ≥6.1 mmol/L and <7 mmol/L | <7.8 mmol/L  |
|  Impaired glucose tolerance | <11.1 mmol/L | <7 mmol/L | ≥7.8 and <11.1 mmol/L  |
|  Diabetes Mellitus | ≥11.1 mmol/L | ≥7.0 mmol/L | ≥11.1 mmol/L  |

To calculate the glucose level in mg/dL, multiply the glucose level (mmol/L) by a factor of 18.

# SYMPTOMS OF HIGH BLOOD SUGAR

- Frequent urination, nocturia, polyuria: hyperglycemia induces an osmotic diuresis, causing polyuria (increased frequency and volume of urine passed), nocturia (nocturnal frequency).
- Thirst, polydipsia: the osmotic diuresis results in volume contraction, causing thirst and hence polydipsia.
- Tiredness, lethargy, altered conscious state.
- Weight loss: if the hyperglycemia is caused by type 1 diabetes mellitus, a net catabolic state can result in hunger and weight loss despite polyphagia.
- Asymptomatic and/or has abnormal screening test: mild hyperglycemia may go unnoticed. Obese children with type 2 diabetes mellitus are often detected only on screening the blood and urine glucose.

109

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

- The body controls blood glucose by monitoring the blood glucose level at the pancreas.
- The pancreas controls the release of two important hormones with opposing blood glucose regulation actions so as to maintain the blood glucose level within the normal range (homeostasis):

- Insulin is released from the beta cells in the islets of Langerhans of the pancreas in response to a rising blood glucose level. Insulin allows for glucose uptake into the body cells (primarily muscle and fat cells) for metabolic use. At the liver and muscle, it converts glucose into glycogen. The net effect is to lower and normalize the blood glucose level.
- Glucagon is released from the alpha cells of the pancreas in response to low blood glucose level. It converts stored glycogen into glucose (glycogenolysis), which is released into the blood stream to increase and normalize the blood glucose level.

- Other important hormones also influence blood glucose levels.

- "Stress hormones" such as epinephrine (which enhances both lipolysis and glycogenolysis) and steroid hormones such as cortisol (which enhances gluconeogenesis) increase the blood glucose level.

# CAUSES

- The most common causes of hyperglycemia are

- Type 1 diabetes mellitus: caused by inadequate production of insulin, and this commonly arises from progressive autoimmune destruction of the pancreatic beta cells.
- Type 2 diabetes mellitus: caused by a decreased response to insulin in the body tissues (insulin resistance). Insulin resistance is often caused by obesity, where there is a relatively decreased insulin production for the increased body mass index.
- Impaired glucose tolerance; impaired fasting glucose: These are the prediabetes states, and are often associated with obesity.

- The rarer causes of hyperglycemia are

- Single-gene disorders: such as maturity onset diabetes of the young (MODY) and mitochondrial diabetes.
- Drugs: steroids, tacrolimus, growth hormone injections.
- Stress (e.g., trauma, surgery, infection): due to high levels of epinephrine and cortisol.

![img-18.jpeg](img-18.jpeg)

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Signs and symptoms that indicate potentially life-threatening, severe diabetic ketoacidosis:
  - Hyperpnea
  - Headache, irritability, confusion, or an altered conscious level indicate the presence of evolving cerebral edema.
- Clinical signs that may suggest cerebral edema include an increasing BP with a slow pulse rate and papilledema.
- In diabetic ketoacidosis, because of the increased tissue osmolality, the skin turgor may not reflect the severity of the dehydration (skin turgor). If a child is in diabetic ketoacidosis, there is usually at least 5%–10% of dehydration.

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 7-year-old boy who was previously well is brought to the outpatient clinic for a 2 week history of increasing frequency of passing urine and having to get up every night at least four times to pass urine. His mother is very concerned that he is drinking up to three bottles of water a day. He complains of abdominal pain. He has lost 2 kg of weight and is easily tired. There is no family history of diabetes mellitus.

On clinical examination, his height and weight are at the 50th percentile. His blood pressure is 110/60 mmHg, pulse rate 100/min, and respiratory rate 22/min. His temperature is 37.0°C. He is alert, rational, and responsive. His tongue is slightly dehydrated but skin turgor is normal. The abdomen is soft and slightly tender in the periumbilical region. Apart from soft hepatomegaly measuring 3 cm below the costal margin at the right mid-clavicular line, the rest of the physical examination is normal.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

○ Polyuria, nocturia, and polydipsia, which suggest the possibility of diabetes mellitus (osmotic diuresis), diabetes insipidus (excessive free water excretion), or loss of renal concentration.

• What is your diagnosis?

○ Type 1 diabetes mellitus.

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

○ Classical age and presentation for type 1 diabetes mellitus (symptoms of polyuria, nocturia, thirst, weight loss, and lethargy).

○ The presence of mild tachypnea suggests the presence of mild primary metabolic acidosis with compensatory respiratory alkalosis. However, the child does not have drowsiness to indicate severe diabetic ketoacidosis.

○ Previously well with no past history of illness

○ No other symptoms or signs to suggest renal disease

○ No history of headache, vomiting, and loss of visual field defects in pituitary lesions to suggest a suprasellar tumor that may be associated with diabetes insipidus.

○ Mild hepatomegaly can be due to a fatty liver as a result of increased lipolysis in an insulin deficient state.

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

○ Central abdominal pain is unusual but can occur if there is significant ketosis.

• Is there any alternative diagnosis?

○ The alternative diagnoses include diabetes insipidus or chronic kidney disease with medullary concentrating defect.

• List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

○ Frequent urination, nocturia, thirst.

○ Weight loss may be due to dehydration.

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

○ Because the child was growing well, this is unlikely to be caused by a chronic disease such as chronic renal failure or a suprasellar tumor causing diabetes insipidus and panhypopituitarism. Moreover, there were no clinical clues such as pallor or a sallow appearance to suggest chronic kidney disease, or headache and early morning vomiting to suggest an intracranial tumor causing obstructive hydrocephalus.

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

○ Normal blood glucose level, abnormal urine osmolality.

• Is there any red flag symptom?

○ The presence of mild tachypnea suggests the presence of mild primary metabolic acidosis with compensatory respiratory alkalosis. This may be the beginning of ketoacidosis.

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Check his urine dipstick for glucose, ketones.

○ Check his random blood glucose level (≥11.1 mmol/L confirms diabetes mellitus).

○ Because his vital signs are stable (no evidence of impending shock), draw blood for urea, creatinine, electrolytes (include serum sodium, potassium,

bicarbonate), blood glucose level, and serum β-hydroxybutyric acid level. Because he is stable, there is no need to check for arterial blood gas.

- If there are ketones with hyperglycemia and dehydration, admit, escalate to a senior, set an intravenous line, rehydrate with 0.9% normal saline over 24–48 hours, and refer to the pediatric endocrinologist to commence subcutaneous injections of insulin.
- If the child has hyperglycemia with no ketosis and is drinking well, admit the child and escalate to a senior. Intravenous rehydration may not be needed, provided the child is closely monitored. Refer to the pediatric endocrinologist to commence subcutaneous injections of insulin.
- Always monitor the child's conscious state, vital signs (BP, pulse rate, and respiratory rate) and blood glucose level closely for complications of treatment (electrolyte shift, cerebral edema, hypoglycemia).

# Blood Sugar (Low)

Loke Kah Yin

# DEFINITION OF LOW BLOOD SUGAR

- Clinical hypoglycemia is defined as a blood sugar level that is low enough to cause symptoms and/or signs of impaired brain function.
- In general clinical practice, low blood sugar level or hypoglycemia is said to occur when the blood glucose level is <2.6 mmol/L or the capillary glucose level is <2.2 mmol/L.

# SYMPTOMS OF LOW BLOOD SUGAR

- The brain is primarily dependent on a continuous supply of glucose, which diffuses from the blood stream into the neurons.
- With hypoglycemia, the brain function can be compromised, resulting in neuroglycopenic symptoms and a compensatory adrenergic response.
- Asymptomatic: Some patients may not have any symptoms of hypoglycemia
- Neuroglycopenic symptoms:

- Headache
- Lethargy, lassitude, feeling faint, feeling tired
- Visual disturbances, speech disturbances
- Mental confusion, abnormal behavior
- Weakness
- Seizures
- Apnea
- Altered conscious state

- Adrenergic symptoms:

- Hunger
- Sweating
- Shakiness, trembling
- Pallor
- Nausea, vomiting
- Palpitations, tachycardia
- Nervousness, anxiety

- The symptoms of hypoglycemia are also dependent on the age group of presentation:

- Neonate: jitteriness, apnea, pallor, sweating, tachypnea
- Older child: hunger, weakness, tremor, anxiety, palpitations, headache, confusion, seizures, coma

115

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

- The blood glucose level is maintained in the normal range and prevented from decreasing too low by three major processes in the body:

- Ingestion and absorption of carbohydrates.

- Glycogenolysis, a process where glycogen is converted into glucose. This is facilitated mainly by glucagon. Conversely, insulin inhibits glycogenolysis and lipolysis. Thus, hyperinsulinism predisposes to hypoglycemia and concurrent lack of adequate lipolysis.

- Gluconeogenesis: these are the intracellular biochemical reactions that synthesize glucose, the actions of which are enhanced by cortisol and growth hormone. Hence, infants (who have immature compensatory mechanisms) with cortisol deficiency and/or growth hormone deficiency are at risk of hypoglycemia.

- In the presence of hypoglycemia, the normal physiological response is to utilize fat stores as an alternative energy source.

- Fat catabolism produces ketones, and this is the basis to classify the causes of hypoglycemia, i.e., hypoketotic hypoglycemia or ketotic hypoglycemia.

# CAUSES

# 1. Hypoketotic Hypoglycemia

- Hyperinsulinism

- Congenital hyperinsulinism of infancy

- Insulinoma

- Excess insulin injected (accidental or non-accidental) (e.g., patient with type 1 diabetes mellitus who had overestimated and administered excess insulin, non-accidental injection of insulin as a form of child abuse)

- Fatty oxidation defects: for example, medium chain fatty acyl CoA dehydrogenase deficiency

# 2. Ketotic Hypoglycemia

- Inadequate caloric/carbohydrate intake to meet the metabolic demand, e.g., starvation, viral illness with vomiting, sepsis

- Disorders of gluconeogenesis: deficiencies of pyruvate dehydrogenase, pyruvate carboxylase, enzymes of the oxidative pathway

- Disorders of glycogenolysis: glycogen storage diseases

- Endocrine disorders: adrenal insufficiency, growth hormone deficiency (cortisol and growth hormone deficiency both result in decreased gluconeogenesis)

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

• The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

○ If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

■ Check pulse

□ Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.

□ Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

■ When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

○ If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

## 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

## 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

• Take the critical samples

- When a child is hypoglycemic (blood glucose < 2.6 mmol/L) for no clear reason, it is important to quickly collect the critical blood sample so as to establish a cause for the hypoglycemia.
- The critical samples comprise of venous glucose (fluoridated tube), blood ketones, serum insulin, growth hormone, cortisol, lactate, and to freeze serum for the assessment of acylcarnitine profile and freeze urine for metabolic testing if the preliminary testing is suggestive of these disorders.

- If the hypoglycemic child is conscious, acutely correct the hypoglycemia by feeding the child with simple sugars and complex carbohydrate.
- If the hypoglycemic child is unable to feed or is unresponsive, administer intravenous (IV) 10% dextrose 2 mL/kg bolus followed by a maintenance drip with 10% dextrose so as to provide about 4–8 mg/kg/min of glucose. If there is no venous access, administer intramuscular glucagon 0.5 mg (<3 years) or 1.0 mg (>3 years).
- Recheck capillary glucose level after 15–30 minutes to ensure that the blood glucose level has normalized.
- If the child is still hypoglycemic, administer another dextrose bolus. Consider the need for IV hydrocortisone or continuous glucagon infusion. Inform the senior doctor.

# 4. Algorithm

![img-19.jpeg](img-19.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Symptomatic hypoglycemia
- Low blood pressure
- Irregular respiration
- Altered conscious state
- Seizure
- Recurrent episodes of hypoglycemia (need to find the cause)
- Hypoketotic hypoglycemia

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

You are called to review a neonate who was noticed at 2 hours of life to be jittery. He was born full term with a birth weight of 4.5 kg. His mother had gestational diabetes mellitus. Her latest HbA1C was 8.1%. On clinical examination, the baby is jittery. He is intermittently apneic. He appears Cushingoid, with a plethoric face and hirsutism. The apex beat and heart sounds are normal. There is a soft liver measuring 3 cm below the costal margin. The rest of the physical examination is normal. The nurse has just performed a capillary glucose measurement and it was 1.5 mmol/L.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Hypoglycemia causing jitteriness and intermittent apnea.

- What is your diagnosis?

- Infant of a diabetic mother. Mother had poorly controlled diabetes mellitus and, consequently, the baby has relative hyperinsulinism causing hypoglycemia after separation from the mother (i.e., no more glucose supply via the placenta).

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Clear history of poorly controlled gestational diabetes in the mother.
- Large for gestational age.
- Cushingoid appearance with plethora and hirsutism.
- Has symptoms of jitteriness and apnea, which are consistent with hypoglycemia.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information about temperature, heart rate, or blood pressure.
- No information about whether baby is cyanosed.
- No information about SpO₂.
- No information about maternal history of premature rupture of membrane, maternal fever, and maternal group B strep status.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Sepsis.

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Newborns are at high risk for sepsis.
- Septic babies can get hypoglycemic because of the infection.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Baby is only 2 hours old; a bit early for sepsis but still possible if mother had evidence of chorioamnionitis.

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

○ Fever or hypothermia
○ Low blood pressure

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ Baby has symptomatic hypoglycemia: intermittently apneic and jittery.
○ The baby appears to have polycythemia: polycythemia is associated with hyperbilirubinemia and hyperviscosity syndrome.
○ Baby also at risk for

■ Hypoparathyroidism (transient), which can also result in jitteriness and hypocalcemic seizures.
■ Cardiomyopathy.
■ Birth injuries because of large size.

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Because the diagnosis is quite obvious, one needs only to take blood for blood glucose level before instituting treatment for hypoglycemia.
○ The treatment consists of an immediate feed and repeating the capillary blood glucose in 15–30 min.
○ If the capillary glucose levels are still low, the child will need to have an IV line with a 10% dextrose bolus, followed by a maintenance 10% dextrose drip with 4 mg/kg/min, increasing the glucose infusion rate to 8–10 mg/kg/min, if necessary.
○ One should escalate to the senior.
○ If this is truly hypoglycemia due to poorly controlled maternal diabetes mellitus, the hypoglycemia should be transient, lasting from 2 to 4 days.
○ If the hypoglycemia is persistent, we should look for other causes of hypoglycemia.

# Breathlessness and Respiratory Distress

Michael T.C. Lim • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF BREATHLESSNESS AND RESPIRATORY DISTRESS

- Respiratory distress is defined by the constellation of clinical features that indicate difficulty in breathing. These features are commonly due to respiratory disease, but may be caused by a nonrespiratory etiology (e.g., something that is triggering the brain to increase the respiratory rate such as metabolic acidosis [e.g., diabetic ketoacidosis: DKA, inborn errors of metabolism: IEM, lactic acidosis], toxins, drugs, and hyperammonemia).
- Breathlessness (also known as dyspnea or shortness of breath) is defined as the sensation of having difficulty in breathing.

# SYMPTOMS OF RESPIRATORY DISTRESS

- Symptoms of respiratory distress may include

- Feeling breathless
- Increase in respiratory rate (tachypnea)
- Increase in breathing effort, e.g., nose flaring, intercostal or subcostal retractions, use of accessory muscles of respiration, diaphragmatic breathing
- Unusual noises present, e.g., wheezing, stridor, grunting
- Inability to do the usual activities, e.g., unable to bottle feed well, need to stop feeding to catch the breath, decrease in ability to speak, decrease in ability to do exercise
- Symptoms due to decreased oxygenation, e.g., turning blue, altered mental state
- Inability to breathe, cough, or speak (This is an emergency as these symptoms suggest complete airway obstruction!)

- The severity of the respiratory distress can be mild, moderate or severe (see table)

121

# SEVERITY OF RESPIRATORY DISTRESS

|   | No Respiratory Distress | Mild Respiratory Distress | Moderate Respiratory Distress | Severe Respiratory Distress  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Behavior | Normal | Normal | Mild/intermittent irritability | Very irritable or lethargic or decreased conscious level  |
|  Activity level | Normal | Normal or mildly restricted | Restricted, e.g., stopping while feeding, speaking in short sentences, reduced effort tolerance | Severely restricted. The inability to talk, cry, or cough suggests complete upper airway obstruction  |
|  Respiratory rate at rest | Normal | Normal or mildly increased | Increased respiratory rate | Markedly increased or markedly reduced  |
|  Signs of abnormal work of breathing (e.g., nose flaring, intercostal or subcostal retractions, use of accessory muscles of respiration, diaphragmatic breathing) | None | None | Fairly obvious signs of increased breathing effort | Very obvious signs of increased breathing effort; or very little chest movement seen  |
|  Central cyanosis | None | None | May not be obviously cyanosed centrally. SpO₂ or SaO₂ slightly decreased | Centrally cyanosed. SpO₂ or SaO₂ < 85%  |
|  Heart rate at rest | Normal | Normal or slightly increase | Slightly increased | Significantly increased; or bradycardia if collapse is imminent  |
|  Blood pressure at rest | Normal | Normal | Increased | Increased; or decreased if collapse is imminent  |

### PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON CAUSES

The following pathophysiological processes can cause respiratory distress:

- Problem with ventilation

- Upper airway obstruction
- Lower airway obstruction
- Muscles involved in breathing not working adequately

- Problem with perfusion or gas exchange issue

- Inability of blood to get to the lungs
- Inadequate gas exchange at the alveolar membrane level

• Problem with disordered central control of ventilation

• Respiratory center not triggering respiration appropriately
• Something else triggering the brain to increase the respiratory rate

# 1. Problem with Ventilation

That is, something is preventing air/oxygen from reaching the alveoli. The problem may be due to upper airway obstruction, lower airway obstruction, and/or muscles involved in breathing are not working adequately.

• Upper airway obstruction (if the obstruction is partial, it may manifest as stridor and drooling; if the obstruction is complete, it will usually manifest as inability to speak, inability to cough, increasing cyanosis, loss of consciousness)

• Intraluminal causes of airway obstruction

■ Foreign body/food aspiration
  □ Swallowing dysfunction
  □ Accidental choking
  □ Gastroesophageal reflux
■ Secretions
  □ Person with swallowing dysfunction (saliva)
  □ Immunological (anaphylaxis with secretions)
■ Large tonsils and adenoids (infection, malignancy, hypertrophy)

• Abnormal airway walls causing airway obstruction

■ Infection of the upper airways (croup, tracheitis, epiglottitis)
■ Immunological (anaphylaxis with swelling of upper airway mucosa)
■ Injuries (inhaled noxious gases, inhaled noxious chemicals, inhaled hot air, physical trauma)
■ Unfavorable anatomy
  □ Craniofacial anomalies, e.g., Pierre-Robin sequence
  □ Laryngeal lesions, e.g., laryngotracheobronchomalacia
  □ Subglottic lesions, e.g., tracheomalacia, tracheal or subglottic stenosis

• Extra-luminal mass causing airway compression

■ Retropharyngeal abscess

■ Tumor

• Lower airway obstruction (manifests as respiratory distress, may have additional sounds such as wheezing/rhonchi)

• Intraluminal causes of airway obstruction

■ Foreign body/food aspiration
  □ Swallowing dysfunction
  □ Accidental choking
  □ Gastroesophageal reflux with aspiration

■ Secretions/fluids

□ Aspiration of oral secretions
□ Infection (bronchiolitis, pneumonia)
□ Immunological (asthma, anaphylaxis with secretions)
□ Inadequate clearance, e.g., immobility and atelectasis
□ Pulmonary edema

○ Abnormal airway walls causing airway obstruction

■ Infection of the lower airways causing bronchospasm (bronchiolitis, viral-induced wheeze)
■ Immunological (asthma, anaphylaxis with bronchospasm)
■ Injuries (inhaled noxious gases, inhaled noxious chemicals, inhaled hot air, physical trauma)
■ Unfavorable anatomy, e.g., tracheomalacia, congenital lung malformation

○ Extra-luminal mass compressing the airway

■ Vascular ring
■ Enlarged mediastinal lymph node(s)
■ Tumor

• Muscles involved in breathing are not working adequately

□ At the level of the brain/brainstem

■ Causes of altered mental state (see Chapter 5)
■ Respiratory center not working adequately

□ At the level of the spinal cord and/or peripheral nerves

■ For example, trauma to spinal cord, Guillian–Barre syndrome

□ At the level of the neuromuscular junction

■ For example, myasthenia gravis
■ For example, neuromuscular blocking drugs such as atracurium and succinylcholine

□ At the level of the muscle

■ Splinting of diaphragm causing inadequate ventilation per breath, hence the need to breathe faster (e.g., abdominal pain, abdominal distension)
■ Multiple rib fractures causing flail chest
■ Myopathies and dystrophies

# 2. Problem with Perfusion or Gas Exchange Issue

That is, something is preventing arterial blood from reaching the alveoli, or there is a problem of gas exchange at the alveolar membrane level.

• Inability of blood to get to the lungs

○ Shock

■ Distributive (e.g., sepsis, anaphylaxis)
■ Hypovolemia
■ Cardiogenic shock

- Pulmonary embolism (consider in immobile patients or those with hypercoagulable state)
- Duct-dependent lesions in neonates with closing of patent ductus arteriosus

- Inadequate gas exchange at the alveolar membrane level

- Pneumothorax
- Pleural effusion
- Pulmonary edema

- Intravascular fluid overload (renal failure, nephritic syndrome, nephritic-nephrotic syndrome, congestive heart failure)
- Inadequate myocardial contractility

- Supraventricular tachycardia (other cardiac arrhythmias less common in children)
- Myocarditis (acquired)
- Congenital heart disease (e.g., large ventricular septal defect [VSD], large patent ductus arteriosus [PDA])
- Cardiac tamponade

- Submersion injury (near drowning)

- Lung parenchymal disease (collapse, pneumonia/bronchopneumonia, pneumonitis, pulmonary contusion, prematurity associated respiratory distress syndrome)
- Lack of adequate oxygen-carrying capacity or inadequate oxygen dissociation

- Severe anemia
- Carbon monoxide poisoning
- Methemoglobinemia

### 3. Problem with Disordered Central Control of Ventilation

- Respiratory center not triggering respiration appropriately

- Causes of altered mental state (see Chapter 5)
- Respiratory center not working adequately

- Something else triggering the brain to increase the respiratory rate

- Primary metabolic acidosis (e.g., DKA, IEM, lactic acidosis)
- Toxins
- Drugs
- Hyperammonemia

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

### 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

• The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

○ If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

■ Check pulse

□ Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.

□ Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

■ When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

□ If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

• Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
• The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
• If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
• If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

# 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

• History

○ Determine if it is really respiratory distress (see the symptoms section above)
○ Determine the severity of the respiratory distress (see the severity section above)
○ Is the patient able to cough, speak, or breathe?

■ Inability to cough, speak or breathe suggests complete upper airway obstruction. This is an emergency and requires immediate attention. Carry out the Heimlich maneuver if the patient is alert and choking is suspected; if the patient has loss of consciousness, start basic life support and escalate; look for the cause of the upper airway obstruction.

○ Is this acute distress? Acute on chronic distress? Chronic respiratory distress?

■ Acute distress means that the patient had no respiratory distress prior to the onset of symptoms.
■ Acute on chronic distress means that the patient has been having respiratory distress for quite a while, and there has been a sudden worsening of the respiratory distress.
■ Chronic distress means that the patient has been having respiratory distress for quite a while, and the severity of the distress has been about the same over this period of time.

• Is there unusual sound/noise, e.g., stridor, wheezing, and grunting?

■ Stridor and drooling (with or without cough)

☐ Suggests significant but incomplete upper airway obstruction.
☐ Biphasic stridor is suggestive of severe airway obstruction or a subglottic narrowing.
☐ Assess if the patient is stable or needs intervention.
☐ Ask for information which can help determine the cause of the upper airway obstruction.

- Any preceding event

- Choking, aspiration of feeds or foreign body
- Trauma to face/neck
- Exposure to allergen
- Exposure to noxious gas, chemicals, and hot air

- Potential risk factors or causative factor

- Symptoms of infection (fever, cough)
- History suggestive of gastroesophageal reflux
- History suggestive of unfavorable anatomy
- Swallowing difficulties (acute epiglottitis, swallowing dysfunction, extraluminal mass)
- Loss of weight or loss of appetite (malignancy)

■ Wheezing and rhonchi (with or without cough)

☐ Is suggestive of partial lower airway obstruction, the most common cause of which is bronchospasm.
☐ Assess if the patient is stable or needs intervention.
☐ Ask for information which can help determine the cause of the lower airway obstruction

- Any preceding event

- Rhinorrhea, fever to suggest an infectious trigger to wheezing
- Choking, aspiration of feeds or foreign body
- Trauma to chest
- Exposure to allergen
- Exposure to noxious gas, chemicals, and hot air

- Potential risk factors or causative factor

- Personal or family history of atopy to suggest predisposition to bronchodilator-responsive wheeze
- Symptoms of infection (fever, cough)
- History suggestive of gastroesophageal reflux
- History suggestive of unfavorable anatomy

■ Grunting

☐ It is a sign of distress usually seen in neonates and young babies.
☐ It is due to the patient trying to increase their own PEEP (positive end expiratory pressure) to help keep their alveoli open so as to improve oxygenation.

• Is there cough without stridor or wheezing?

- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with pneumothorax, pleural effusion.
- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with pulmonary edema and its causes.

☐ Intravascular fluid overload (renal failure, nephritic syndrome, nephritic-nephrotic syndrome, congestive heart failure)

☐ Inadequate myocardial contractility

- Supraventricular tachycardia (other cardiac arrhythmias less common in children)
- Myocarditis (acquired)
- Congenital heart disease (e.g., large VSD, large PDA)
- Cardiac tamponade

☐ Submersion injury (near drowning)

- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with lung parenchymal disease (collapse, pneumonia/bronchopneumonia, pneumonitis, pulmonary contusion, prematurity associated respiratory distress syndrome).
- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with mild lower airway obstruction (see above).
- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with mild upper airway obstruction (see above).

• Is there breathlessness only (i.e., no stridor, no wheezing, no cough)?

- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with pneumothorax, pleural effusion, and collapse of the lung.
- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with splinting of the diaphragm (any abdominal pain, distension).
- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with causes of inability of blood to get to the lungs.

☐ Is there any symptom and sign of shock?

- Distributive (e.g., sepsis, anaphylaxis)
- Hypovolemia
- Cardiogenic shock

☐ Is there any symptom or risk factor for pulmonary embolism (consider in immobile patients or those with hypercoagulable state)?
☐ Is this a neonate with closing of patent ductus arteriosus?

- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with causes of inadequate oxygen carrying capacity or inadequate oxygen dissociation.

☐ Severe anemia
☐ Carbon monoxide poisoning
☐ Methemoglobinemia

- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with causes of disordered control of respiration.

☐ Toxins
☐ Drugs
☐ Hyperammonemia
☐ Metabolic acidosis (e.g., DKA, IEM, lactic acidosis)

• Is the patient having decreased oxygenation and yet not having tachypnea (i.e., inappropriate response to hypoxia)?

- Assess if the patient's mental status is intact/normal/altered. If yes, look for the cause.
- Assess if the patient's respiratory center can trigger respiration appropriately.
- Assess if the muscles involved in breathing are not working adequately.

- Any trauma to chest?
- Is there sensory loss? A sensory level?
- Any exposure to drugs that cause muscle weakness/paralysis?
- Is there muscle weakness?

- Ask for signs and symptoms associated with causes of inadequate oxygen carrying capacity or inadequate oxygen dissociation.

- Methemoglobinemia

• If there is a chronic respiratory distress, look for the cause of the chronic respiratory distress. Carry out a systems review (including family history).

- Any underlying chronic respiratory disease?
- Any personal history of atopy?
- Any cardiac condition?
- Any metabolic disease?
- Any neurological condition?
- Any immunodeficiency?
- Any failure to thrive?
- Any renal disease?
- Any family history of genetic conditions, atopy, etc.?
- Any contact history for infection, tuberculosis, and household cigarette smoke?

• Physical examination

• Vital signs

- High heart rate: do not assume that it is because the child is anxious or frightened or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress.
- Low heart rate: this can be seen when there is prolonged severe respiratory distress or when the child is dying.
- High blood pressure: do not assume that it is because the child is anxious or frightened or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress.
- Low blood pressure: this can be seen when there is shock, when there is prolonged severe respiratory distress or when the child is dying.
- High respiratory rate: do not assume that it is because the child is anxious or frightened or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress.
- Low respiratory rate or poor respiratory effort: this can be seen when the central nervous system is depressed, when the respiratory

muscles are not working adequately, when the child is in severe respiratory distress, and when the child is tiring out.

- Decreased oxygenation and or central cyanosis: assess if the child is in severe respiratory distress. Do not assume that the low SpO₂ value is due to poor contact with saturation probe or patient movement.

- Is it really respiratory distress? How severe is the respiratory distress?

- Assess using the table on severity of respiratory distress.
- Irritable child: do not assume that it is because the child is anxious or frighten or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress.
- Lethargic or decreased conscious state: assess if the child is in severe respiratory distress.

- Posture of the child

- Learning forward and bracing the shoulder girdle are signs of increased breathing effort required.

- Any unusual sound

- Stridor (inspiratory, expiratory, or biphasic)
- Wheezing
- Grunting
- Coughing (wet sounding, dry sounding, barking cough, etc.)

- If there is any compromise in upper airway, particularly if severe, avoid upsetting the child as this can convert a partially obstructed airway in to a completely obstructed airway; decide on whether there is a need for urgent escalation to the senior and ENT and anesthesia.

- Do a respiratory examination if there is no severe upper airway obstruction.

- Chest wall
  - Signs of injury
  - Chest expansion increased, decreased, and asymmetrical

- Trachea position

- Apex beat

- Percussion note

- Air entry

- Adventitious sounds and their distribution

  - Sounds

    - Crepitations (fine or coarse)
    - Rhonchi (inspiratory, expiratory, both)

  - Distribution

    - Unilateral/bilateral
    - Corresponds to which part of the lung/anatomy

- Do a detailed cardiovascular examination.

- Examine the rest of the body, including growth parameters (head size, weight, height, body mass index) and development.
