Continued on next page

4. Algorithm

Continued from previous page

![img-21.jpeg](img-21.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Any sign of respiratory distress is worrying, but one should especially note the following red flags that are suggestive of severe respiratory distress (see the table on the severity of respiratory distress):

- Very irritable/lethargic/decreased conscious level
- Markedly increased respiratory rate
- Markedly reduced respiratory rate
- Very obvious signs of increased breathing effort
- No, poor, or irregular breathing effort
- Central cyanosis/hypoxia
- Inability to feed, talk, cry, or cough – suggests severe respiratory distress and/or complete upper airway obstruction
- Biphasic stridor at any time or stridor in a child at rest – suggests severe partial upper airway obstruction

- Patients with severe respiratory distress are at risk of cardiorespiratory collapse and/or hypoxic insult to the brain; attend to them immediately and monitor them closely

- Pay attention to the patients with moderate respiratory distress too as these patients may deteriorate despite starting treatment.

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 10-month-old boy presents with 1 week of rhinorrhea, wet cough, and vomiting after drinking milk. His stools have been loose over the past 2 days. He had fever for just over a day at the start of his symptoms. His cough has become more frequent over the past 4 days, and sounds wet. Over the last 2 days, his mother noticed that his breathing has been faster and he has not been drinking as much. He started vomiting out his milk after feeds due to the cough, and this has become more frequent over the past day. His mother brought him for evaluation because he stops breathing for up to 30 seconds and turns blue. This has happened about three times in the last 4 hours.

He was born at 34 weeks gestation, weight 2.6 kg at birth, and received noninvasive continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) ventilation and oxygen for 2 days to treat neonatal respiratory distress syndrome. He had one previous episode of wheezing with an upper respiratory tract infection at 6 months of age, but has otherwise been well. He lives with a 4-year-old sister and his parents. His father smokes cigarettes at home. His father and paternal grandmother have asthma. Physical examination reveals a drowsy looking boy, appears pink. His temperature is 38.1°C, heart rate 150/min, respiratory rate 50/min, blood pressure 100/65 mmHg, and oxygen saturation 89%. His weight and length are on the 25th percentile. Capillary refill time is 3 seconds at the sternal area, and his lips appear dry. He has a wet-sounding cough that is paroxysmal, but no whoop. There are intercostal and subcostal retractions with nasal flaring. Auscultation of his chest reveals course crackles throughout both lungs, with polyphonic expiratory wheeze. First and second heart sounds are heard, and there is no murmur. His abdomen is soft and nontender with active bowel sounds. His neurological examination is normal.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Severe respiratory distress with hypoxia, wheeze, crackles and intermittent apnea.

- What is your diagnosis?

- Bronchiolitis with severe respiratory distress; mostly likely caused by a respiratory virus infection.

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Young age.
- Started with cough and rhinorrhea and progressed to respiratory distress by days 3–4 of illness.
- Respiratory distress with cough.
- Bilateral wheeze and crackles on chest auscultation.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Loose stools.
- Intermittent apnea may be part of a severe respiratory tract infection in young infants, but other causes of apnea must be considered.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information provided about any positive contact history.
- No information provided about travel or immunization history (to assess likely etiology to infection).
- No information provided about other potential causes of apnea (e.g., did the vomiting lead to aspiration of his milk?).

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Apnea – this may be caused by the bronchiolitis, or secondary to milk aspiration from post-tussive vomiting.
- Hypoxia (oxygen saturation was 89% on room air).
- Symptoms and signs of dehydration.

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Stabilize the patient.

- Give oxygen.

- Put on noninvasive ventilation with continuous positive airway pressure in view of the recurrent apneas.
- Insert an intravenous cannula and start an intravenous drip for rehydration and maintenance of hydration.

- Stop oral feeding until respiratory distress has lessened.
- Start intravenous fluids to correct dehydration and then maintain hydration.
- Admit to the pediatric high dependency unit for further care.
- Escalate to the senior doctor immediately.
- Do regular temperature, heart rate, respiratory rate, and blood pressure recording.
- Continuously monitor oxygen saturation monitoring.
- Maintain a fluid balance chart.
- Consider sending a nasopharyngeal aspirate for viral immunofluorescence.

# Brief Resolved Event
in an Infant

Chew S.Y. • Michael T.C. Lim • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION AND SYMPTOMS OF A BRIEF RESOLVED EVENT

A brief resolved event is not a diagnosis. It is a term used to describe an acute, unexpected episode that occurred in an infant but had resolved by the time the infant reached medical attention. The episode was usually frightening to the caregiver and included several or all of the following symptoms:

- Absent, decreased or irregular breathing
- Color change, e.g., cyanosis, plethora or pallor
- Marked change in muscle tone, e.g., limpness or rigidity
- Altered level of responsiveness

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

Brief resolved events are classified into the following groups:

- Explained – an underlying cause is found.

- The most common and/or important identifiable causes are
  - Gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD)
  - Respiratory infection
  - Seizure or neurological disorder
  - Non-accidental injury (NAI)

- There are also less common causes.

- See the table given below for more information.

- Unexplained – no underlying cause or explanation is found after an appropriate history taking and physical examination.

- This is called a Brief Resolved Unexplained Event (BRUE), previously known as Apparent Life-Threatening Event (ALTE).
- Patients with BRUE are further classified into high-risk BRUE or low-risk BRUE.
- Patients with high-risk BRUE are at risk for having recurrent episodes or for having an undiagnosed serious underlying disorder.

136

# POSSIBLE REASONS FOR BRIEF RESOLVED EVENTS

|  Reason for Brief Resolved Events | Possible Associated Symptoms  |
| --- | --- |
|  **Gastrointestinal**  |   |
|  GERD | - Associated vomiting/regurgitation of feeds - Recent feeding - Occurred while awake and supine  |
|  **HEENT and respiratory**  |   |
|  **Respiratory tract infection, especially:** - Whooping cough (pertussis causes apnea) - Bronchiolitis - Pneumonia | - Fever or hypothermia - Cough - Coryza - Lower tract symptoms, e.g., wheezing, and respiratory distress - Apnea, hypopnea  |
|  **Airway obstruction** - Intraluminal causes     - Foreign body/food aspiration     - Secretions     - Large tonsils, adenoids - Abnormal airway walls     - Unfavorable anatomy (craniofacial anomalies laryngomalacia, subglottic stenosis, etc.)     - Anaphylaxis     - Injuries - Extra-luminal causes     - Retropharyngeal abscess, tumor | - Stridor, wheezing, obstructive apnea - Feeding difficulty - Copious nasal discharge – young infants often cannot handle their own secretions, or compensate for nasal congestion as they are obligate nose breathers - History of ingestion - History of choking  |
|  **Accidental smothering** | - Occurred while sleeping - Prone position - Soft objects (pillows, blankets, toys) near or covering face  |
|  **Non-accidental injuries**  |   |
|  - Smothering - Blunt trauma (especially to head and neck) - Shaken baby syndrome - Munchausen by proxy - Poisoning | - History of trauma - Recurrent severe episodes that occurred in the presence of single caretaker - Blood in nose and mouth - Sibling that died unexpectedly - Delayed presentation to care - Discrepancies in the historian's account - See Chapter 37 on injuries for more information  |
|  **Central nervous system**  |   |
|  Seizure | - Loss of consciousness - Eye deviation - Convulsive movements - Limp, stiffness - Post-ictal drowsiness  |
|  Apnea of infancy | - Preterm infants  |
|  Meningitis, encephalitis | - Fever - Neck stiffness - Altered state of consciousness  |

Continued

# POSSIBLE REASONS FOR BRIEF RESOLVED EVENTS—cont'd

|  Reason for Brief Resolved Events | Possible Associated Symptoms  |
| --- | --- |
|  Raised intracranial pressure | - Vomiting - Altered state of consciousness - Cushing reflex - Abnormal papillary examination  |
|  **Other infections**  |   |
|  - Sepsis - Urinary tract infection | - Fever/hypothermia - Lethargy/irritability - Localizing signs of infection  |
|  **Metabolic**  |   |
|  - Inborn errors of metabolism - Electrolyte abnormalities - Hypoglycemia | - History of feeding difficulties, vomiting - Poor growth - Altered state of consciousness - Family history of metabolic disorder or death in childhood - Consanguinity  |
|  **Cardiovascular**  |   |
|  - Arrhythmias - Congenital heart diseases - Heart failure | - Feeding difficulties - Poor growth - Diaphoresis - Breathless/cyanosis - Palpitations  |
|  **Medications**  |   |
|  - Drug toxicity - Drug side effects/adverse reaction - Reaction to vaccine (DPT) - Poisoning (intentional/unintentional) | - History of medication use - Use of alternative medication - Lethargy - Features seen in NAI  |
|  **Others**  |   |
|  - Fever - Acrocyanosis |   |

### CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

The aims of managing such patients are to:

1. Form an impression and determine if there is a need to resuscitate.
2. Perform primary assessment and determine if there is a need to intervene.
3. Perform secondary assessment

- To ascertain what the symptoms were.
- To look for possible etiology (detailed history, thorough physical examination).

4. Classify the event into

- Explained (an underlying cause is found)
- Unexplained (no explanation for a qualifying event is found after an appropriate history and physical examination) = BRUE

- High-risk infant

- Low-risk infant

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

  - Check pulse

    - Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
    - Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

  - When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

- If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

# 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

- The history should be taken preferably first hand from persons who observed the infant during and/or immediately after the event.

- Description of event

  - Who witnessed the event
  - When it occurred
  - Duration of event
  - What was seen during the event
  - Behavioral state – awake or asleep
  - Responsiveness – responsive, less responsive, unresponsive
  - Respiratory effort – none, shallow, irregular, or increased
  - Color change – cyanosis, pallor, or plethora; whether the color change affected the entire body, extremities, and/or perioral area
  - Tone – stiff, floppy/limp, or normal
  - Seizure like activity – abnormal movements, abnormal eye movements
  - Noise – cough, gag, choke, or gasp
  - How did it stop? Suddenly? Gradually? Any intervention needed?

• Was intervention needed?

- Degree of resuscitation – gentle stimulation, mouth-to-mouth resuscitation, CPR
- Involvement of medical personnel

• What was the infant like after the event?

- Back to normal or not back to normal?
- How long did it take for the infant to get back to normal?

• Circumstances and environment prior to the event

- Recent feeding
- Regurgitation or vomiting
- Sleep position – prone, supine, side
- Environment – crib, car seat, or in bed with parent, presence of soft objects

• Recent illness and other medical history

- Presence of symptoms suggesting current illness or injuries
- Use of any medication – herbal, prescription, or over the counter
- Recent vaccination
- History of similar events
- Relevant medical history – prematurity, chronic illnesses, GERD
- Pre/perinatal history and newborn screen
- Growth and developmental history
- Travel history
- Contact history

• Family and social history

- Family history of BRUE, SIDS (sudden infant death syndrome), genetic, cardiac, neurologic, or metabolic conditions
- Social history – exposure to smoke or use of alcohol or drugs by household members

• A detailed head-to-toe physical examination of all systems is required; in particular:

- Vital signs
- Growth parameters (head size, weight, height, body mass index)
- Skin color
- Cardiorespiratory examination
- Abdomen examination
- Neurological state and fontanelle
- Evidence of injury from non-accidental causes

# 4. Classify

• Classify into

- Explained (an underlying cause is found)
- Unexplained (no explanation for a qualifying event is found after an appropriate history and physical examination) = BRUE

- High-risk infant

- Low-risk infant

![img-22.jpeg](img-22.jpeg)

Continued on next page

5. Algorithm

Continued from previous page

![img-23.jpeg](img-23.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Age ≤ 60 days
- History of prematurity of less than 32 weeks and corrected gestational age <45 weeks
- Duration of event is >1 min
- Required intervention (CPR) to abort/terminate event.
- Similar previous event
- Significant medical illness
- Concerns identified from history (e.g., family history of sudden cardiac death)
- Suspected non-accidental injury (see Chapter 37 Injuries)
- Unwell looking
- Abnormal physical examination

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 3-week-old infant presents to the emergency department after he turned pale and blue while he was sleeping in his mother's arms. He had just finished breastfeeding and had fallen asleep. His mother describes him as being limp and unresponsive. His respiratory efforts were shallow and his lips blue. His mother rubbed his back and performed mouth-to-mouth resuscitation. The infant responded after 30 seconds. When the paramedics arrived, the infant was alert and not cyanosed. His mother reports a similar incident that happened last night when the baby was sleeping. This occurred about 45 min after feeding. Because he had returned to his baseline much faster, they did not seek medical care then.

The infant was born at term, and delivered vaginally without complications. His mother was well antenatally. She was found to be a carrier of Group B streptococcus (GBS) and received intravenous antibiotics at the onset of labor. There was no prolonged rupture of membranes, or maternal fever during the time of delivery. The infant remained well in the nursery for the next 2 days and was discharged.

He is breastfeeding normally and has been growing well. He occasionally vomits after feeding. The mother reports no fever or respiratory symptoms. His stooling and urine output have been normal. His behavior was normal prior to the incident. He is not taking any medication or herbal treatment.

The parents are the sole caregivers, and this is their first child. There are no smokers in the household. There are no sick contacts.

On examination, he is asleep. His rectal temperature is 38.1°C, heart rate 170/min, respiratory rate 35/min, blood pressure 70/40 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 98%. His weight is 3.5 kg (birth weight 3.4 kg). He has no dysmorphic features and his head circumference is on the 50th percentile. His pulses are well felt and regular. His capillary refill time is 3 seconds. Normal heart sounds are heard on auscultation. His tone and reflexes are normal. His Moro reflex is symmetrical. His anterior fontanelle feels full. There is no respiratory distress, and vesicular breath sounds are heard on

auscultation. His abdomen is soft and nontender. There are no masses or organomegaly. No rash or injuries are seen on the infant.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Brief resolved event (sudden, brief and now resolved episode of cyanosis, hypotonia, shallow breathing and unresponsiveness reported by the caregiver).

- What is your diagnosis?

- Likely an infective cause, e.g., meningitis or another focus of serious bacterial infection.

- List the findings in this case that supports this diagnosis.

- Fever
- Capillary refill is 3 seconds
- Tachycardia
- Fontanelle feels full
- Recurrent episodes suggest a significant pathology
- Risk factors for meningitis: neonate and maternal GBS carrier
- Mother received adequate intrapartum antibiotics, which reduces the risk for early GBS infection in the infant but not late GBS infection

- List the findings in this case that goes against this diagnosis.

- The infant was reported to be well with no history of fever, irritability, lethargy, or poor feeding.
- It occurred after feeding, and this may point towards GERD, with fever as a sign of micro-aspiration.

- If the diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or provided in this case.

- Skin examination findings, e.g., any rash

- What are your alternative diagnoses?

- Gastroesophageal reflux disease

- List the findings in this case that supports this alternative diagnosis.

- The two episodes occurred after feeding and the patient was supine.
- The patient has occasional vomits after feeds.

- List the findings in this case that goes against this alternative diagnosis.

- There was no vomiting or regurgitation at the time of event.
- There was no respiratory effort to suggest acute airway obstruction.
- Presence of fever.
- Presence of full fontanelle.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the most likely diagnosis?

- Meningitis

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

The red flag symptoms are

- Patient < 60 days old
- Multiple episodes
- The episode was not self-limiting and required mouth-to-mouth resuscitation
- Unwell child with abnormal physical findings – febrile, tachycardic, prolonged capillary refill time, and full fontanelle

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Carry out septic work up – full blood count, blood culture, lumbar puncture for cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) biochemistry, cell count and culture; urinalysis and urine culture.
- Carry out blood glucose as hypoglycemia may be present in an unwell neonate and can be the cause of such events.
- Carry out urea, creatinine, and electrolytes including ionized calcium to identify any electrolyte abnormality that can result in the presenting symptoms.
- Carry out an ECG to look for any rhythm abnormality.
- Start intravenous antibiotics at meningitic doses.
- Regularly monitor the circulation and conscious level (including fits and raised intracranial pressure).
- Escalate to the senior.

# Reference

1. Tieder JS, Bonkowsky JL, Etzel RA, et al. Clinical practice guideline: brief resolved unexplained events (formerly apparent life-threatening events) and evaluation of lower-risk infants. Pediatrics 2016;137(5):e20160590.

# Bruising and Bleeding

Koh Pei Lin • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF BRUISING AND BLEEDING

- Bruises and ecchymosis are skin discolorations caused by the escape of blood from damaged blood vessels into subcutaneous tissues.
  - Bruises are small (2 mm to 10 cm in diameter). Bruises are also known as purpura.
  - Ecchymoses are large (>10 mm).
- Abnormal bleeding is defined as prolonged or excessive bleeding from any site.
- The abnormal bleeding may be spontaneous, after a minor injury or after a hemostatic challenge.

# SYMPTOMS OF BRUISING AND BLEEDING

- Symptoms of bruising

- Skin discoloration (red, blue, purple, brown, green, yellow)
- Pain
- Tenderness
- Swelling

- Symptoms of abnormal bruising

- Bruising is common in normal children, especially in mobile infants (when they start to crawl or walk), toddlers, and school-age children; usually caused by accidental injuries (e.g., falls) and usually on bony prominences on the limbs and head, e.g., over the knees, shins, elbows, forehead.

- Bruising is more likely to be due to an abnormal cause when

- It is spontaneous.
- It is excessive.
- It occurs in uncommon sites, e.g., back, buttocks, upper arms, and abdomen.
- It occurs in nonmobile infants (usually before the age of 9 months).
- There is family history of similar symptoms.

- Symptoms of abnormal bleeding

- Bleeding is very common in the normal population.
- Bleeding is more likely to be due to an abnormal cause when it is
  - Spontaneous.
  - Recurrent.
  - Prolonged or excessive bleeding after hemostatic challenges, e.g., intramuscular vaccination, heel prick blood tests at birth, venepuncture, circumcision, dental procedures.

146

- Starts at a young age.
- Associated with a family history of similar symptoms.
- At certain sites
  - Mucocutaneous bleeding
    - Skin: petechiae, bruising/purpura, ecchymoses
    - Nose bleed (epistaxis)
    - Oral mucosal/gum bleeding
    - GI bleed: hematemesis, hematochezia, fresh blood in stool, melena
    - Hemoptysis
    - Hematuria
    - Menorrhagia (heavy menses)
    - Umbilical stump bleeding, delayed cord separation

- Bleeding into potential spaces

  - Brain
  - Joints
  - Muscle: muscle hematoma and joint bleeds (hemarthroses) – typical presentations of clotting factor deficiencies, e.g., hemophilia
  - Fascial planes, e.g., subcutaneous hematoma

- None of the above symptoms are specific to or pathognomonic of a particular disorder.

### PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

- Hemostasis is dependent on the interplay between the platelets, plasma clotting factors, state of the blood vessels, and force applied.
- The pathophysiology of excessive/abnormal bruising and/or prolonged/abnormal bleeding can be due to

  - Platelet disorder: thrombocytopenia or platelet dysfunction (congenital or acquired).

  - Clotting factor deficiency: single or multiple factor deficiencies (congenital or acquired).

  - Blood vessel abnormalities: connective tissue disorder, arteriovenous malformation, hereditary hemorrhagic telangiectasia (HHT), steroid use, vitamin C deficiency.

  - Force applied

    - May be nil, mild, moderate, critical. Bleeding with minimal force applied is usually abnormal.
    - Accidental versus non-accidental injury.

- It is important to note that the site of bleeding may not be unique to a particular pathophysiological mechanism, but there are some general guidelines (Table 14-1).:

### COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Petechiae are usually seen in disorders of platelets or blood vessels or secondary to injury (e.g., accidental injury due to coughing, non-accidental injury due to strangulation).

TABLE 14-1

SITES OF BLEEDING AND CAUSES OF BLEEDING

|  Site of Bleeding | Thrombocytopenia or Platelet Dysfunction | Single Clotting Factor Deficiency | Multiple Clotting Factor Deficiencies | Blood Vessel Abnormality | Injury (Accidental and Non-accidental)  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Skin (petechiae) | + |  |  |  | +  |
|  Skin (bruises, ecchymoses) | + | + | + | + | +  |
|  Persistent bleeding after minor skin abrasion | + |  |  | + |   |
|  Nose bleed (epistaxis) | + | + | + | + | +  |
|  Oral mucosal/gum bleeding | + | + | + | + | +  |
|  Menorrhagia | + | + | + |  |   |
|  GI bleed | + | + | + | + | +  |
|  Hematuria | + | + | + |  | +  |
|  Subcutaneous hematoma |  | + | + |  | +  |
|  Muscle hematoma |  | + | + |  | +  |
|  Joint bleed |  | + | + |  | +  |
|  Brain bleed | + | + | + | + | +  |
|  Retina hemorrhages |  |  |  |  | +  |
|  Umbilical cord bleeding |  | + | + |  |   |
|  Pulmonary hemorrhage/hemothorax | + | + | + | + | +  |
|  Delayed bleeding |  | + | + |  |   |

Note: "+" means present.

- Bleeding into soft tissue, muscle, and joints suggests the presence of a clotting-factor deficiency or secondary to injury (accidental or non-accidental).
- Platelet disorders and clotting-factor deficiencies will be discussed in detail below.
- Blood vessel causes will only be covered briefly here.
- For force-related injuries, see Chapter 37 on injuries for the approach to injuries (accidental versus non-accidental injury).

# 1. Causes of Platelet Disorders

- Isolated thrombocytopenia; no other cell-line involvement:

  - Lack of production

    - Congenital

      - Small platelets (Wiskott–Aldrich syndrome)

      - Normal-sized platelets (Down syndrome, 22q11 deletion syndrome, congenital amegakaryocytic thrombocytopenia, thrombocytopenia absent-radii [TAR] syndrome)

      - Giant platelet disorders (Bernard–Soulier syndrome, MYH9-related disorders)

    - Acquired

      - Drugs, e.g., sodium valproate

  - Destruction/consumption/sequestration

    - Congenital

      - Rare genetic causes

    - Acquired:

      - Immune: Alloimmune thrombocytopenia, maternal systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), immune thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP)

      - Infection: intrauterine infection, viral-induced thrombocytopenia (e.g., dengue fever)

      - Drugs: e.g., cephalosporins

      - Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS), thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP)

      - Hypersplenism due to portal hypertension

- Thrombocytopenia with other cell-line involvement (pancytopenia)

  - Lack of production

    - Bone marrow failure: aplastic anemia, Fanconi anemia, drugs, viral infections

    - Bone marrow replacement: leukemia, lymphoma, storage disease, e.g., Gaucher disease

  - Destruction

    - DIC, HUS, TTP

    - Severe hypersplenism due to portal hypertension

- Platelet function disorders

  - Congenital

    - Small platelets (Wiskott–Aldrich syndrome)

    - Normal-sized platelets (Down syndrome, DiGeorge syndrome, congenital amegakaryocytic thrombocytopenia, TAR syndrome)

    - Giant platelet disorders (Bernard–Soulier syndrome, MYH9-related disorders)

    - Platelet storage pool disorder (Hermansky–Pudlak syndrome)

    - von Willebrand disease

  - Acquired – uremia, cardiopulmonary bypass, liver dysfunction, antiplatelet drugs (e.g., aspirin)

• Manifestations of platelet disorders

○ Bleeding due to reduced platelet number or function is usually predominantly mucocutaneous bleeding, e.g., petechiae, bruising, nose bleed (epistaxis), oral mucosal/gum bleeding, menorrhagia (heavy menses).
○ Can also involve bleeding elsewhere.
○ See Table 14-1.

# 2. Causes of Clotting Disorders

• Congenital: usually single clotting factor deficiency

○ Hemophilia A (Factor VIII deficiency – X-linked recessive)
○ Hemophilia B (Factor IX deficiency – X-linked recessive)
○ Hemophilia C (Factor XI deficiency – autosomal inheritance)
○ von Willebrand disease
○ Other rare congenital bleeding disorders – deficiencies of Factor II, V, VII, X, and XIII, congenital fibrinogen deficiency (afibrinogenemia, hypofibrinogenemia)

• Acquired

○ Drugs: e.g., heparin, warfarin
○ DIC
○ Liver dysfunction, chronic liver disease
○ Vitamin K deficiency (vitamin K not given at birth or due to use of antibiotics)
○ Massive transfusion
○ Acute coagulopathy of trauma

• Manifestations of clotting disorders

○ Bleeding due to clotting factor disorders usually affects the soft tissue, muscles, and joints.
○ Can also involve bleeding elsewhere.
○ See Table 14-1.

# 3. Causes of Blood Vessel Abnormalities

• Connective tissue disorder, e.g., Ehlers–Danlos syndrome (EDS) classical type, EDS joint hypermobility type, EDS vascular type
• Small vessel vasculitides, e.g., Henoch–Schonlein purpura (HSP)
• Vascular abnormality, e.g., HHT, arteriovenous malformation (AVM)
• Drug-induced, e.g., vitamin C deficiency, steroid
• Manifestations of blood vessel abnormalities (see Table 14-1)

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Initial Impression

• Initial impression

○ Assess appearance
○ Assess work of breathing
○ Assess circulation to skin (color)

• The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

o If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

▪ Check pulse

□ Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.

□ Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

▪ When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

o If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

• Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
• The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
• If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
• If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

# 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

• History

o Detailed history of bleeding symptoms

▪ Duration
▪ Age of onset of bleeding symptoms
□ Early presentation in infancy = more likely a congenital problem
▪ Once or recurrent
▪ Spontaneous or after hemostatic challenges or after injury
▪ Immediate or delayed
□ Immediate is more often seen in platelet or blood vessel disorder.
□ Delayed is more often seen in clotting factor deficiency.
▪ Sites of bleeding (see Table 14-1)

o Is the red cell line affected?

▪ For example, is there pallor? Pallor may be due to chronic blood loss or decrease in red blood cell production.

o Is the white cell line affected? For example, is there recurrent infection?

o Gender

▪ Male patient: can be sex-linked recessive disorders, autosomal recessive, autosomal dominant disorder or acquired disorder.
▪ Female patient: can be autosomal recessive, autosomal dominant or acquired disorders.

o Systems review

■ Fever

□ Viral infections, e.g., dengue causing thrombocytopenia.

□ Sepsis leading to thrombocytopenia and DIC.

□ A history of prolonged fever, loss of weight, and bone pain may indicate an underlying hematological malignancy.

■ A history of recurrent infections and eczema may indicate an underlying immunodeficiency such as Wiskott–Aldrich syndrome (WAS).

■ Dysmorphic features.

■ Oculocutaneous albinism.

■ Kidney disease: uremia causes platelet dysfunction.

■ Jaundice/liver disease: clotting factor deficiencies and thrombocytopenia due to hypersplenism.

■ Skeletal abnormalities – Fanconi anemia, TAR.

■ Look for features that are suggestive of injuries (accidental and non-accidental). See Chapter 37 for more information on injuries.

○ Medication history

■ Was vitamin K given at birth?

■ Antibiotic use: can lead to acquired vitamin K deficiency and coagulopathy.

■ Antiplatelet agents, e.g., nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) cause platelet dysfunction.

■ Drugs that can induce thrombocytopenia, e.g., valproate.

■ Anticoagulant use (heparin, warfarin).

■ Drugs that can affect blood vessels, e.g., steroid, vitamin C deficiency.

■ Use of alternative medicine treatment.

○ Family history of bleeding tendencies

■ Affects every generation and both sexes = consider autosomal dominant disorder, e.g., von Willebrand disease(VWD), HHT.

■ Affects only male relatives on the maternal side of family = X-linked recessive disorder such as hemophilia A or B, Wiskott–Aldrich syndrome.

■ Consanguinity = autosomal recessive platelet disorders, e.g., congenital amegakaryocytic thrombocytopenia.

○ Psychosocial history

■ Look for features to suggest non-accidental injuries.

• Physical examination

○ Verify the presence of bleeding.

■ Examine for sites and extent of bleeding – petechiae, bruises, hematoma, hemoarthrosis (joint bleed), etc.

○ Do a head-to-toe examination.

○ Possible signs of involvement of other systems include

■ Signs of pallor; presence suggests chronic blood loss, involvement of red blood cell line production.

■ Signs of infection; its presence suggests involvement of white blood cell line, specifically neutropenia.

- Dysmorphic features – Down syndrome, DiGeorge syndrome, EDS vascular type.
- Oculocutaneous albinism – Hermansky–Pudlak syndrome.
- Pallor, hepatosplenomegaly, and lymphadenopathy may indicate malignancy or storage disease.
- Jaundice alone – liver dysfunction, destruction (e.g., HUS).
- Jaundice and hepatosplenomegaly – chronic liver disease.
- Splenomegaly alone – portal hypertension.
- Eczema – Wiskott–Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- Skin hyperelasticity, joint hypermobility – EDS.
- Skin telangiectasis-HHT.
- Abnormal neurological signs.
- Skeletal abnormalities, e.g., arthritis or joint deformities due to recurrent joint bleeds, TAR, Fanconi anemia.
- Features that suggest injuries (accidental and non-accidental injuries).

### 4. Investigations

Initial screening laboratory tests:

- Full blood count (FBC)
- Peripheral blood film
- PT/APTT (prothrombin time/activated partial thromboplastin time)

### 5. Algorithm

- Decide if the bleeding is normal/abnormal.
- If the bleeding is abnormal, decide if it is predominantly mucocutaneous or not mucocutaneous.
- Use history and physical examination information to further narrow down or prioritize the list of differential diagnoses (use Table 14-1).
- Look at the FBC and PT/PTT (Table 14-2) to narrow down the list of differential diagnoses.

LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Features of hemodynamic instability
- Features of severe anemia
- Features of intracranial bleed – headache, vomiting, drowsiness, seizure
- Features associated with non-accidental injuries
  - Retinal hemorrhages
  - Other features (see Chapter 37 Injuries)

TABLE 14-2

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS

|  Full Blood Count | Prolonged PT and APTT | Prolonged APTT, Normal PT | Prolonged PT, Normal APTT | Normal PT/PTT  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Normal | Vitamin K deficiency Warfarin overdose Multiple clotting factor deficiencies Fibrinogen deficiency Factor II deficiency Factor V deficiency Factor X deficiency Liver dysfunction Liver failure | Factor VIII deficiency (hemophilia A) Factor IX deficiency (hemophilia B) Factor XI deficiency (hemophilia C) Severe VWD Heparin | Warfarin use Factor VII deficiency Mild liver dysfunction Mild vitamin K deficiency | Mild VWD Platelet function disorder Blood vessel causes Accidental injury Non-accidental injury  |
|  Low platelet only | Liver disease with hypersplenism Massive transfusion Acute coagulopathy of trauma |  |  | Lack of production of platelets Destruction  |
|  Low platelet plus anemia and/or low WBC | Liver disease with severe hypersplenism DIC |  |  | Lack of production of platelets Destruction  |

### CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 2-year-old boy is brought to your clinic for the complaint of a painful swollen left knee. He fell down in the playground yesterday. His mother did not notice any swelling or bruising over his left knee at that time. The swelling occurred the day after. She reports that he is very active and tends to have a lot of bruises all over his body and limbs. He has no fever. He is taking multivitamins and is not on any medication. His mother recalls that she had a maternal uncle who passed away in childhood due to bleeding after a fall. On examination, the boy is alert, afebrile, heart rate 130/min, respiratory rate 24/min, and blood pressure 90/60 mmHg. He has multiple bruises over his upper limbs, lower limbs, and back. The bruises are of varying ages. His left knee is swollen, warm, and tender. He resists examination of his left knee, and he is unable to stand or walk. He has no external signs of head injury. You carry out a full blood count and PT/APTT. Full blood count is normal. PT 13 seconds (normal 12–14.5 seconds); APTT 80 seconds (27–36 seconds).

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Swollen and painful left knee
- Frequent and multiple bruises

- What is your diagnosis?

- Severe hemophilia A or B; with left knee hemoarthrosis and bruises

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Abnormal bruising and abnormal bleeding: frequent bruises, many bruises on his back, hemarthrosis
- Male sex – possible X-linked disorder
- Family history consistent with X-linked recessive disorder – maternal granduncle passed away due to bleeding at a young age
- Isolated prolonged APTT

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information about any prolonged bleeding after heel prick blood tests or muscle hematoma after intramuscular injections e.g. after vitamin K at birth or after intramuscular vaccination.
- No information about pallor or recurrent infection.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Severe VWD disease

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:
- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Abnormal bruising and abnormal bleeding: frequent bruises, many bruises on his back, hemarthrosis

- Isolated prolonged APTT

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

o VWD is usually an autosomal dominant condition. The family history is suggestive of X-linked recessive disorder (if we assume the mother is unaffected; no information is given on the mother)

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

o As VWD is usually an autosomal dominant condition, if the maternal grand uncle had this, we expect the mother to also have symptoms. No information was given on the mother's status

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

o No symptoms and signs of raised intracranial pressure suggestive of intracranial bleed.

o No symptoms to suggest hypovolemia or anemia.

- What is your plan for this patient?

o Confirm diagnosis by Factor VIII and Factor IX assays, von Willebrand screen (as severe von Willebrand disease can be misdiagnosed as hemophilia A because it is also associated with Factor VIII deficiency).

o RICE therapy for joint bleed – rest, ice (cold compress), compression bandage, elevation.

o Refer to the hematologist.

o Patient's results came back as follows: Factor VIII assay < 1% (severe Factor VIII deficiency/hemophilia A), Factor IX 89% (normal), von Willebrand screen normal.

o Treat bleeding with intravenous Factor VIII replacement therapy.

o Patient education on how to recognize bleeds, danger signs of intracranial bleed/internal bleeding, and immediate treatment.

o Prophylactic regular Factor VIII replacement therapy to prevent bleeds.

o Advice to avoid dangerous contact sports.

o Avoid intramuscular injections, to administer intramuscular injections via subcutaneous route.

o Avoid antiplatelet drugs, e.g., NSAIDs, aspirin.

o Perform genetic counseling.

# Chest Pain

Mahesh Babu Ramamurthy • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF CHEST PAIN

Chest pain is a term used to describe an uncomfortable sensation that occurs in the thoracic region.

# SYMPTOMS OF CHEST PAIN

- The symptoms can include

- Dull ache
- Burning sensation
- Crushing sensation
- Sharp pain

- The site of the chest pain may be

- Easily localized by the patient (i.e., can point to the site of the pain with one finger).
- Diffuse/vague, hard to be localized by the patient.
- Associated with radiation to other places, e.g., jaw, neck, arms, back.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

- The pathophysiology of chest pain is either organic or psychological.
- Organic causes

- It is important to remember that only structures that have pain receptors can give rise to the feeling of chest pain. The lung parenchyma does not have pain receptors; as such, pure lung parenchymal disease will not present with chest pain.

- Logically thinking, from exterior to interior these would include

- Skin

- Epidermis, which contains skin cells
- Dermis, which contains tough connective tissue, hair follicles, sweat glands, blood vessels, lymphatics, nerve endings
- Deeper subcutaneous tissue (hypodermis), which is made up of fat, connective tissue, blood vessels, nerves

- Peripheral nervous system
- Musculoskeletal system
- Pleural membrane
- Blood vessels, pericardium, myocardium
- Esophagus

- Psychological causes

- Anxiety, stress, psychosomatic pain
- Attention seeking behavior

157

COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Common Skin Causes

# • Causes:

• Traumatic injury: abrasion, laceration, scald, burn, bruising, soft tissue injury, etc.
• Infection/inflammation: insect bite, cellulitis, abscess, infected sebaceous cyst, necrotizing fasciitis, herpes zoster

# • Manifestations:

# • Traumatic injury

■ There may be a history of physical trauma (the lack of history does not exclude injury as there may be unreported non-accidental injury).
■ May be evident by bruising, wounds, swelling, etc.

# • Infection/inflammation

■ Pain and other features of inflammation like erythema, swelling, warmth, and tenderness.
■ May or may not be associated with break in epidermis.
■ Necrotizing fasciitis is rare but should be considered when the degree of pain is way more severe than clinical signs.
■ Herpes zoster

□ Sometimes pain/tingling sensation can precede the rash.
□ Acutely painful vesicles.
□ Typically, the vesicles are within a dermatome and do not cross midline.

# 2. Common Musculoskeletal System (MSK) Causes

# • Causes:

# • Muscle fatigue

■ Frequent coughing, coughing with great force, e.g., respiratory tract infection
■ Prolonged increased work of breathing, e.g., asthma
■ Deconditioning

• Trauma: fracture (rib, sternum)
• Costochondritis
• Tietze syndrome
• Slipping rib syndrome

# • Manifestations:

• MSK related pain is usually localized; aggravated by movement, coughing, and/or inspiration; relieved by rest.
• In muscle fatigue, there is usually a history of frequent coughing, great force in coughing, or prolong increased in work of breathing. Sometimes, if a patient is deconditioned, increase in work of breathing can result in pain.
• In costochondritis, multiple costochondral junctions are painful on palpation, but there is no swelling.

- In Tietze syndrome, usually only a single costochondral junction (usually second or third) is tender and swollen.
- Slipping rib syndrome presents as pain under the eight to tenth rib, increasing on flexion of the trunk. This pain might also be reproduced by a hooking maneuver.

### 3. Common Pleural Membrane Causes

- Causes:

- Pleural effusion, empyema, hemothorax, pneumothorax, dry pleurisy

- Manifestations:

- Pleuritic pain is sharp, localized, and increases with deep inspiration and coughing; relieved by shallower breathing.
- The pain can be localized to the side of the hemi thorax. The pain can sometimes be referred to the ipsilateral shoulder or the abdomen if the diaphragmatic pleura is irritated.
- The associated symptoms and signs will help differentiate the cause.

- Fever and cough (infection related pleurisy, pleural effusion, empyema, autoimmune causes of pleurisy)
- Trauma (hemothorax)
- Sudden onset of breathlessness (pneumothorax)
- Progressive onset of breathlessness (pneumothorax, hemothorax, pleural effusion)

### 4. Common Blood Vessels, Pericardium, Myocardium Causes

- Causes:

- Pericarditis
- Myocarditis
- Arrhythmias
- Aortic dissection, ruptured aortic aneurysm
- Pulmonary embolism
- Myocardial ischemia (hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy [HOCM], aortic stenosis, anomalous origin of left coronary artery from pulmonary artery [ALCAPA])

- Manifestations:

- Pericarditis (usually acute pericarditis)

- Central chest pain or left-sided chest pain.
- Severity of pain may worsen on lying down and improve on sitting forward.

- Myocarditis

- Most commonly infectious in etiology, so may be associated with fever and other symptoms of infection (cough, rhinorrhea, rash, ulcers, diarrhea).
- Only physical sign may be tachycardia disproportionate to fever or dehydration.

■ When more severe, it will manifest as symptoms of reduced cardiac output (reduced sensorium, cool peripheries, etc.).

○ Arrhythmia

■ Patient may describe altered heart rhythm, palpitations (see Chapter 45 Palpitations).
■ Syncopal episodes may be seen.

○ Aortic dissection

■ Sudden onset of pain; sometimes there may be a pop heard/felt
■ Persistent pain, not pleuritic in nature; may radiate to the back

○ Myocardial ischemia

■ Centrally located and may radiate to the left arm.
■ Described as heaviness or crushing pain.
■ Increases on exertion.
■ Palpitations and sweatiness are sometimes associated.

# 5. Common Esophagus Causes

• Causes:

○ Esophagitis, e.g., due to gastro esophageal reflux (GER) or gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD)

• Manifestations:

○ Pain is typically retrosternal or epigastric and burning in nature, increasing with feeds and certain posture.

# 6. Common Psychological Causes

• Causes:

○ Anxiety, stress, psychosomatic pain
○ Attention seeking

• Manifestations:

○ Seen more in adolescents than younger children.
○ Usually chronic recurrent pain, absent in the night, and does not seem to disturb sleep.
○ These children tend to be anxious and have other stressors that can be elicited in history.
○ Pain is otherwise nonspecific, non localized, and not accompanied by abnormal physical signs.
○ These children might also have rapid deep breathing.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Initial Impression

• Initial impression

○ Assess appearance
○ Assess work of breathing
○ Assess circulation to skin (color)

• The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

o If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

▪ Check pulse

□ Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.

□ Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

▪ When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

o If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

• Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
• The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
• If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
• If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

# 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

• History taking

o What exactly does the patient mean by chest pain?
o Where is the site? Any radiation?
o What is the severity of the pain?
o What makes it better? For example, sitting up, not breathing so hard, not exercising, not moving, not eating, not eating much, position?
o What makes it worse? For example, certain positions, coughing, breathing, exercise, movement, eating?
o Any trigger/preceding event? For example, trauma, fever?
o Ask for other associated symptoms and signs.

▪ Fever?
▪ Respiratory distress symptoms?
▪ Ill looking?
▪ Altered consciousness? Loss of consciousness?
▪ Any skin change?
▪ Any chest wall tenderness?
▪ Any chest wall trauma?
▪ Any palpitation?
▪ Any history of heart problems or chest problems?

o Do a full system review, including growth and development.
o Past medical history.
o Travel history.

- Contact history.
- Vaccination history.
- Family history.

- Do a head-to-toe examination paying special attention to the thorax, heart and lungs.

# 4. Algorithm

- Classify the signs and symptoms into the following groups so as to narrow down the differential diagnosis.
- Chest pain and fever

- Pleural effusion, empyema, dry pleurisy (infection, autoimmune)
- Pericarditis
- Myocarditis
- Skin/musculoskeletal infection

- Chest pain and cough/respiratory distress; chest pain varies with breathing (e.g., worse on deep inspiration, better when not breathing deeply)

- Muscle fatigue

- Frequent coughing/coughing with great force, e.g., respiratory tract infection
- Prolonged increased work of breathing, e.g., asthma
- Deconditioning

- Fracture
- Pleural effusion, empyema, hemothorax, pneumothorax, dry pleurisy (infection, autoimmune)

- Chest pain and cough/respiratory distress; chest pain does not vary with breathing

- Pleural effusion, empyema, hemothorax, pneumothorax
- Pulmonary embolism

- Chest pain and ill looking (altered mental state, hypotension, shock)

- Myocarditis
- Aortic dissection and ruptured aortic aneurysm
- Myocardial ischemia (HOCM, aortic stenosis, ALCAPA)
- Pulmonary embolism
- Pleural effusion
- Pneumothorax (especially tension pneumothorax)
- Hemothorax
- Multiple rib fractures causing flail chest
- HOCM
- Aortic stenosis
- Arrhythmias

# • Chest pain and skin changes

○ Skin infection (cellulitis, abscess, infected sebaceous cyst, herpes zoster)
○ Skin traumatic injury (insect bite, abrasion, laceration, bruising, soft tissue injury, etc.)
○ Tietze syndrome
○ Fracture

# • Chest pain and chest wall tenderness

○ Skin infection or traumatic injury (cellulitis, abscess, infected sebaceous cyst, necrotizing fasciitis, insect bite, abrasion, laceration, bruising, soft tissue injury, etc.)
○ Costochondritis
○ Tietze syndrome
○ Slipping rib syndrome
○ Fracture

# • Chest pain after trauma/external evidence of trauma

○ Skin traumatic injury (abrasion, laceration, bruising, soft tissue injury, etc.)
○ Fracture
○ Pneumothorax
○ Hemothorax
○ Aortic dissection and ruptured aortic aneurysm

# • Chest pain on exercise/movement

○ Skin infection (cellulitis, abscess, infected sebaceous cyst, herpes zoster)
○ Skin traumatic injury (insect bite, abrasion, laceration, bruising, soft tissue injury, etc.)
○ Muscle fatigue

■ Frequent coughing/coughing with great force, e.g., respiratory tract infection
■ Prolonged increased work of breathing, e.g., asthma
■ Deconditioning

○ Costochondritis
○ Tietze syndrome
○ Slipping rib syndrome
○ Fracture
○ Myocardial ischemia (HOCM, aortic stenosis, ALCAPA)

# • Chest pain related to meal

○ GER/GERD

# • Chest pain related to posture/position

○ Skin infection (cellulitis, abscess, infected sebaceous cyst, herpes zoster)

- Skin traumatic injury (insect bite, abrasion, laceration, bruising, soft tissue injury, etc.)
- Costochondritis
- Tietze syndrome
- Slipping rib syndrome
- Fracture
- GER/GERD
- Pericarditis

- Chest pain and palpitations

- Arrhythmia. Look for the cause (see Chapter 45 Palpitations)

- Chest pain and heart murmur

- HOCM
- Aortic stenosis

- Chest pain with minimal associated symptoms

- Pneumothorax
- Pleurisy and pleural effusion
- Pulmonary embolism
- Aortic dissection/rupture
- Myocarditis
- Pericarditis
- Arrhythmias
- Myocardial ischemia (HOCM, aortic stenosis, ALCAPA)
- Herpes zoster (early stages)
- Anxiety, stress, psychosomatic pain
- Attention seeking behavior

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Chest pain that wakes up the child from sleep
- Chest pain that is associated with respiratory distress
- Chest pain that is associated with fever
- Chest pain and ill looking (e.g., altered mental state, hypotension, shock)
- Chest pain after trauma
- Chest pain that is associated with arrhythmia/palpitations
- Chest pain that is associated with syncope and altered mental state
- Signs and symptoms suggestive of non-accidental injury (see Chapter 37 Injuries)
- Signs and symptoms suggestive of myocardial ischemia
- Family history of sudden death

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 14-year-old boy presents to the Children's Emergency with sudden severe chest pain on the left side since 1 hour ago.

He was brought in to the emergency by an ambulance after he complained of severe left-sided chest pain in school. He was in class

Chapter 10 Chest Pain

listening to a lecture when the pain suddenly started. The chest pain is described as a sharp pain in the upper part of the left side of his chest, not radiating, and not related to his breathing efforts. Coughing increases his pain. He also had difficulty breathing, which he did not experience before. There is no history of fever or having played contact sports prior to that or any history of trauma.

On questioning, he said that he did have left-sided chest pain (though not to the same intensity) twice in the last year. He was seen by his family doctor and was treated symptomatically. No investigations were done. He had a history of childhood asthma and was on inhalers till he reached the age of 6. Subsequently, he had no further wheeze, breathlessness, or cough. There is a history of asthma in mother.

Physical examination: weight 45 kg (25th percentile), height 171 cm (75th–90th percentile), temperature 36.5°C, heart rate 90/min, respiration rate 22/min, blood pressure 105/64 mmHg, and oxygen saturation 95% in room air. ENT was normal. There were slight intercostal retractions and significant pectus excavatum seen. Trachea was central. Apex beat was felt on the fourth intercostal space just medial to the mid-clavicular line. The left infraclavicular area over the second to the fourth intercostal space was hyper-resonant on percussion. The rest of the areas had normal resonance. Air entry on the right side was normal with normal breath sounds. On the left side, there was decreased air entry heard over the left supraclavicular area and infraclavicular area over the second and third intercostal space. Vocal resonance was decreased over the same area. No bronchial breathing, wheeze, or crepitations were heard. The rest of the examination on the left side was normal. Cardiovascular and abdominal examination was normal.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

○ Acute onset of left-sided chest pain

• What is your diagnosis?

○ Spontaneous pneumothorax on the left side

• List the findings that support this diagnosis.

○ Previously well child

○ Acute onset of left-sided chest pain

○ Pain worsened by coughing

○ Associated with breathlessness

○ No associated systemic symptoms like fever

○ Clinical findings of hyper-resonance with decreased air entry localized to the left upper hemithorax; not corresponding to lobar configuration

• List the findings that go against this diagnosis.

○ Known child with a past history of asthma

○ Pain not related to breathing efforts

• What are your differential diagnoses?

○ No good differential diagnosis given the signs and symptoms

• After the reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Spontaneous pneumothorax on the left

• Is there any red flag symptom in this child?

○ Patient has mild intercostal retraction suggestive of the presence of respiratory distress.

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Confirm the diagnosis with a chest X-ray PA view.

○ The chest X-ray can also be used to quantify the pneumothorax as mild or moderate or severe.

○ If the pneumothorax is less than 20% of the hemithorax volume, conservative management with observation in the wards. Give 100% oxygen by a nonrebreather mask to accelerate reabsorption of air from thorax.

○ If the pneumothorax is more than 20%, will need either aspiration or insertion of intercoastal drain and observation. Urgent referral to the pulmonologist.

○ Admit the patient.

○ Patient and the parents will need counseling on some lifestyle changes and restriction of activities like deep sea diving for life, high-altitude flying, and contact sports for 6 weeks.

○ He had two episodes of mild left-sided chest pain in the past year. This suggests that he may have had previous small pneumothoraxes. Assess if the patient has a bleb or any other underlying condition that predisposes to recurrent pneumothoraxes (e.g., Marfan syndrome, Ehlers–Danlos Syndrome Type IV).

# Constipation

Sajini Mary Varughese • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF CONSTIPATION

Constipation is defined as a decrease in the frequency of defecation or the painful passage of stool. If the symptoms are present for two weeks or more, it is known as chronic constipation.

# SYMPTOMS OF CONSTIPATION

- Less than three complete bowel movements per week
- Painful defecation with/without bleeding
- Hard pellet-like stools or large caliber stools that cause rectal outlet obstruction (Bristol Stool Chart Types 1 and 2) (Fig.16-1)
- Fecal incontinence
- History of stool withholding behavior and retentive posturing by the child
- Waxing and waning of abdominal pain with the passage of stool
- Previous or current anal fissures and previous history of constipation
- Poor appetite that improves with passage of large stool

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Consistency of stool:

- Bristol Stool Chart Types 3 and 4 are considered normal stools (Fig. 16-1).
- The frequency of defecation normally decreases with age and after the introduction of a weaning diet.

- Frequency of bowel movement:

- Table 16-1 shows the mean average frequency of bowel movements in children.

- Constipation in children may be

- Primary (also known as functional/idiopathic constipation).
- Secondary (from an underlying disease state).

- Almost 95% of children with constipation have functional constipation.

# 1. Primary (Functional) Constipation

- Normal defecation: When sufficient stool enters the normal rectum, stretch receptors and nerves in the intramural plexus are activated. Inhibitory neurons relax the internal anal sphincter, allowing stool to reach the external anal sphincter, which is composed of voluntary skeletal muscle. The urge to defecate is then signaled. If the child relaxes the external anal sphincter and increases intra-abdominal pressure with the Valsalva maneuver, the rectum is evacuated of stool. If, however, the child tightens the external anal sphincter and gluteal muscles, the fecal mass is pushed back into the rectal vault and the urge to defecate subsides.

167

| --- | --- | --- |
|  Type 1 |  | Separate hard lumps, like nuts (hard to pass)  |
|  Type 2 |  | Sausage-shaped but lumpy  |
|  Type 3 |  | Like a sausage but with cracks on the surface  |
|  Type 4 |  | Like a sausage or snake smooth and soft  |
|  Type 5 |  | Soft blobs with clear-cut edges  |
|  Type 6 |  | Fluffy pieces with ragged edges, a mushy stool  |
|  Type 7 |  | Watery, no solid peices. Entirely liquid  |
