A medical position statement of the North American Society for Pediatric Gastroenterology and Nutrition. J Pediatr Gastroenterol Nutr. 1999 Nov;29(5):612-26. Erratum in: J Pediatr Gastroenterol Nutr 2000 Jan;30(1):109.

\( ^{a} \) Approximately means ± 2 SD.

- Repeated stool withholding leads to stretching of the rectum and eventually of the lower colon, producing a reduction in muscle tone and stool retention. The longer the stool remains in the rectum, the harder the stool comes to the point of impaction.

- Risk factors for primary constipation include

- Inadequate water and fiber intake
- Dietary transition, e.g., weaning from breast to formula milk, introduction of solid food
- Psychosocial stress, e.g., toilet training, starting school, parental punishment

- Manifestations:

- Had normal passage of meconium at birth, i.e., within 48 hours.
- Symptoms appeared after the first month of life.
- Symptoms may coincide with the presence of risk factors (e.g., stress, transition diet).
- Child is generally well with normal growth centiles.
- Child has normal development.
- Examination of the abdomen, lumbosacral region, lower limb neurology, and anal region is normal, apart from the possible presence of fecal masses or anal fissures.
- There may be a positive family history of primary constipation.

# 2. Secondary Causes of Constipation

- See Table 16-2 for the differential diagnosis of secondary constipation.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. History

- Is the child really constipated?

- Clarify what the patient or caregiver means by constipation. The patient or caregiver often defines constipation differently from the doctor.
- What does the stool look like? Carry out a stool inspection.

- Is it primary (functional) constipation or secondary constipation?

- Timing of onset of constipation and potential precipitating factors
- Birth history and timing of passage of meconium
- Growth and general well-being of the child
- Developmental history

- Locomotor development and any weakness in the legs/tendency to fall
- Other developmental domains

- Abdominal symptoms such as distension, nausea, vomiting (bilious or not), and pain

- Diet and fluid intake
- Medication history

TABLE 16-2

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF SECONDARY CAUSES OF CONSTIPATION

|  Secondary Cause |   | Clinical Manifestation  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Gastrointestinal | - Congenital anal malformations - Stricture from longstanding inflammatory bowel disease | - Delayed passage of meconium, thin flat stools, abnormal anal position and patency - History of loss of weight and appetite, blood in stools, cramping abdominal pain, perianal skin tags and fissures  |
|  Neurological | - Hirschsprung disease | - Delayed passage of meconium (>48 hours of life) and/or constipation beginning early in life (<1 month old), gush of stool following digital examination (aganglionosis causing hypertonic sphincter), abdominal distension, bilious vomiting and fever (suggest enterocolitis due to stasis), associated with trisomy 21 but can occur in normal children  |
|   |  - Tethered cord, spina bifida, spinal cord trauma | - Abnormal lower limb neuromuscular signs, associated deformity such as talipes, abnormal lumbosacral spine and gluteal regions, e.g., dimple, pit, hairy patch, absent anal wink, patulous anus  |
|  Metabolic/endocrine | - Hypothyroidism - Hypokalemia - Hypercalcemia | - Developmental delay, wide fontanelle, short stature, hypotonia - Muscle weakness and cramps - Renal/biliary stones, bone pain, abdominal pain/nausea/vomiting, polyuria, depression  |
|  Genetic | - Trisomy 21 - Cystic fibrosis - Celiac disease | - Dysmorphism, developmental delay - Failure to thrive, respiratory problems, family history - Growth and developmental delay, family history  |
|  Medication | - Opioids, iron supplements, anticholinergics, - Vincristine | - History of medication use - Presence of other side effects, e.g., peripheral neuropathy with vincristine  |
|  Psychiatric | - Anorexia nervosa - Sexual abuse - Child abuse | - Intentional weight loss from restrictive intake - Extreme fear during anal examination, presence of fissures and hematomas - Evidence of non-accidental injuries  |

- Family history of constipation or specific diagnoses such as coeliac disease
- Psychosocial history; be aware of the possibility of child abuse
- Previous investigations for and treatment of constipation and response

## 2. Physical Examination

- Vital signs – assess for evidence of dehydration and sepsis
- Nutritional status

- Growth parameters and their serial trend
- Development
- Presence of dysmorphism
- Thyroid status
- Cardiorespiratory examination
- Abdominal examination

- Distension, tenderness, mass, and palpable fecal matter.
- Perianal examination – anal tone, wink, patency, position, presence of fissures/fistulae, presence of bruises with extreme fear during examination, which may point towards sexual abuse.
- Carry out a per-rectal examination – this is of value if the diagnosis of functional constipation is uncertain and Hirschsprung disease (HD) or anatomical abnormalities are suspected. It is best performed by a specialist competent in interpreting the findings such as a gush of stool in HD.
- Carry out a stool inspection.

- Spine and lumbosacral examination looking for sacral dimple/pit/nevi or hairy patch
- Neurological examination, especially lower limb neuromuscular examination
- Any evidence of non-accidental injury

### 3. Algorithm

![img-24.jpeg](img-24.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Secondary causes of constipation
- Complications of chronic constipation:
  - enterocolitis
  - intestinal obstruction
  - urinary tract infection
  - encopresis, enuresis causing social embarrassment
- Evidence of child abuse and/or sexual abuse

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 10-week-old baby boy presents to the Children's Emergency with abdominal distension. He was born at 38 weeks with a birth weight of 2.6 kg. He passed meconium at 30 hours of life and was discharged home after that. He had been breastfeeding well at home until 3 days prior to the visit to the Children's Emergency. His parents are concerned that he has been feeding poorly, and his abdomen is getting increasingly distended. He also vomited twice that morning, both times undigested milk. Since discharge after birth, he has been passing yellow seedy stools at home every 3 days but only with the help of glycerin suppositories purchased from the pharmacy. Today, his stools are foul smelling with some blood streaks in it.

Physical examination reveals a lethargic baby with a heart rate of 190/min, and the blood pressure was not recorded by the triage nurse. His temperature is 35.8°C. He is mottled and has a sunken anterior fontanelle. His abdomen is distended with visible bowel loops in the mid-abdomen. Bowel sounds are sluggish. Examination of the lumbosacral spine and lower limb is normal with no deformities. There are no anal fissures, whereas rectal examination produces a gush of blood-streaked foul-smelling stools. Baby was noted to have upslanting eyes, simian creases, and was hypotonic.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

  - Constipation, likely secondary constipation
  - An ill, septic infant with features of infection involving the gut
  - Dysmorphic child

- What is your diagnosis?

  - Enterocolitis, mostly likely secondary to Hirschsprung disease in a baby with trisomy 21. Baby is likely to be in septic shock.

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

  - Constipation

    - Passes motion but only with the help of glycerin suppositories.
    - Likely secondary as the onset is at <1 month of age.

  - Septic shock

    - Lethargic-looking baby with tachycardia, hypothermia, mottling, tachycardic, and no recordable blood pressure.

  - Enterocolitis

    - Poor feeding, abdominal distension with visible bowel loops, blood in the stools.

  - The gush of stool during rectal examination is almost diagnostic of HD.

  - Accompanying features of trisomy 21 further support the underlying diagnosis of HD as up to 15% of children with trisomy 21 may have HD.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

  - He had passed meconium within 48 hours of birth (although 50% of children with HD may pass meconium within 48 hours of birth).

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

  - Bilious vomiting as the disease progresses.

  - Presence of a tight anal sphincter.

  - The baby's current weight is not given. There may be evidence of poor weight gain and poor feeding in both HD and trisomy 21.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

  - Bacterial gastroenteritis with septic shock

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

  - Septic shock

    - Lethargic-looking baby with tachycardia, hypothermia, mottling, tachycardic, and no recordable blood pressure.

  - Bacterial gastroenteritis/enterocolitis

    - Poor feeding, abdominal distension with visible bowel loops, blood in the stools.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

  - nil

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

  - We expect more frequent stool output if there is a bacterial gastroenteritis

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Enterocolitis, mostly likely secondary to Hirschsprung disease in a baby with trisomy 21

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Septic shock
- Possibly gut perforation

• What is your plan for this patient?

- Escalate to the senior.
- Obtain intravenous access and resuscitate with fluids.
- Obtain blood cultures and start broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics, covering especially for Gram-negative and anaerobic organisms.
- Keep the baby nil by mouth and insert an nasogastric tube to decompress the gut.
- Check for electrolyte abnormalities and correct as necessary.
- Obtain an abdominal X-ray if perforation of the gut is suspected and initiate urgent referral to the pediatric surgeon.
- Admit to the ICU.
- Refer to the gastroenterologist and pediatric surgery.
- Assess for other features of trisomy 21 and associated problems such as hypothyroidism and cardiac abnormalities when the baby is more stable.

Cough

Daniel Goh

# DEFINITION OF COUGH

- Cough is a natural protective reflex that helps to clear the large airways of secretions, foreign particles, and irritants. It is also a major symptom of disorders of the respiratory system.
- Severe or prolonged coughing can cause unpleasant side effects, such as vomiting, chest pain, abdominal pain, headache, urinary incontinence, syncope, and also interfere with sleep as well as daily activities. It is a significant cause of anxiety in parents.

# SYMPTOMS OF COUGH

- A cough can be described by its duration, character, and timing.

- The duration is acute if it is present for less than 3 weeks; it is chronic when lasting longer than 3 weeks without evidence of waning.
- The character of the cough can be nonproductive (dry) or productive (when it sounds wet or when phlegm is coughed up).
- The timing of the cough may be only at night (also known as nocturnal cough), during the day, or may be present both night and day.

- There may be other associated symptoms, e.g., breathlessness, respiratory distress (see Chapter 12), cyanosis (see Chapter 18), noisy breathing (see Chapter 41).

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. The Cough Reflex Pathways

- Cough receptors are located mainly on the larynx, pharynx, trachea, and carina of the airway. There are far fewer receptors in the distal airways, and absent beyond the respiratory bronchioles.
- The afferent pathway is via the superior laryngeal nerves and vagus nerves to the cough center.
- The cough center is believed not to be a specific area but a function within the respiration and reflex networks of the medulla oblongata of the brainstem. Coughing can occur or be inhibited voluntarily, suggesting control from higher centers of the brain.
- The efferent pathway travels from the cerebral cortex and medulla via the vagus and superior laryngeal nerves to the glottis, intercostal muscles, diaphragm, and other major inspiratory and expiratory muscles.

175

## 2. The Phases of Normal Cough

- The normal cough mechanism starts with inhalation of air above functional residual capacity (inspiratory phase) followed by closure of the glottis and a build-up of pressure behind the glottis (forced expiration against a closed glottis).
- About 0.2 second after glottis closure, it reflexively opens with a turbulent expiratory flow (expiratory phase) at speeds of up to 100 mph, expelling any secretion or particulate matter that triggered the cough reflex, clearing the airway.

## 3. Common Causes of Acute Cough

- The most common cause of acute cough in childhood is a viral upper respiratory tract infection. Healthy preschool children in daycare can have up to eight viral respiratory infections with a cough each year, and these can last an average of 10 days (and up to 25 days but with gradual waning of symptoms).
- See Table 17-1 for features of the common causes of acute cough.

## 4. Common Causes of Chronic Cough

- The most common causes of chronic cough and their features are shown in Table 17-2.

TABLE 17-1

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF ACUTE COUGH

|  Dry or Productive Cough | Timing of Cough | Cause | Commonly Associated Symptoms and Signs  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Dry | All day | Upper respiratory infection | Rhinorrhea, possible fever and sore throat  |
|  Dry initially; may progress to productive | All day | Croup (acute laryngeal tracheobronchitis) | Barking cough, stridor, usually low grade fever  |
|  Dry initially; usually progresses to productive | All day | Pneumonia (viral or bacterial) | Upper respiratory tract prodrome, fever, may have wheezing (usually viral); tachypnea, chest or abdominal pain (pleuritic pain)  |
|  Dry initially; usually progresses to productive | All day | Bronchiolitis | Tachypnea, wheezing, crackles, retractions, occurs most commonly between 3 and 6 months old, but can occur up to 2 years old  |
|  Dry initially; may progress to productive | All day | Foreign body aspiration | History of choking may be elicited; no upper respiratory tract symptoms and no fever initially  |

TABLE 17-2

COMMON DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF CHRONIC COUGH

|  Dry or Productive Cough | Timing of Cough | Cause | Commonly Associated Symptoms and Signs  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Dry initially; may progress to productive | All day but may wax and wane; can be worse at night | Asthma | Associated with wheeze; predominantly nocturnal cough; may be exacerbated by allergens or exercise; accompanying atopy; family history of asthma  |
|  Dry initially; may progress to productive | All day but may wax and wane; usually aggravated when in a supine position | Gastroesophageal reflux disease | Infants and toddlers: Regurgitation after feeds, irritability and arching of the back, recurrent pneumonia (aspiration) and/or wheezing Older child: Chest pain or heartburn, wheezing, regurgitation, halitosis  |
|  Dry initially; may progress to productive | All day but may wax and wane; usually aggravated when in a supine position | Allergic rhinitis with postnasal drip | Itchy, sneezing, runny and/or blocked nose; may be associated with itchy eyes (allergic conjunctivitis); may be worse in supine position  |
|  Dry initially; may progress to productive | All day but may sometimes be worse at night | Post-viral respiratory infection cough | History of viral respiratory infections usually in close succession with resultant persistent cough; child is otherwise well, growing well, with no evidence of chronic disease (e.g., digital clubbing)  |
|  Productive | All day | Persistent bacterial bronchitis | Persistent or recurrent wet cough in an otherwise well child; often improves with a therapeutic trial of antibiotics  |
|  Dry | Not present when distracted or asleep | Habitual or psychogenic cough | No fever, no other symptoms; not present when distracted, aggravated under stress; cough usually explosive and barking or honking in nature  |

### 5. Rarer Causes of Chronic Cough

- These can cause a cough that may be dry or productive.
- These rare causes chronic cough can include

- Other causes of postnasal drip, e.g., sinusitis.
- Inhaled irritants (including cigarette smoke).
- Foreign body aspiration.
- Pertussis or parapertussis.
- Bronchiectasis (from various causes, including immunodeficiency, chronic aspiration syndrome, cystic fibrosis, primary ciliary dyskinesia, tuberculosis), which usually causes a wet cough and is associated with finger clubbing.

- Congestive cardiac failure.
- Interstitial lung disease.
- Vocal cord dysfunction.
- Tracheobronchomalacia.
- Extrinsic compression of the tracheobronchial tree (from vascular ring, lymph node).
- Drugs (e.g., angiotensin converting enzyme [ACE] inhibitors cause a dry and tickly cough in around 10% of patients treated with it).

### CLINICAL APPROACH

- The approach to diagnosing the cough involves

- Taking a good history.
- Carrying out a head-to-toe physical examination, including growth and development.
- Use the information gathered to determine if the cough is

- Acute or chronic.
- Dry or productive.
- Nocturnal, daytime, or both.

- Use the information gathered and the above tables to help you reach the correct diagnosis and differential diagnosis.
- Consider if there is a need to isolate or institute infectious disease control procedures.

### LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

The presence of any of the following findings warrants further evaluation and urgent management:

- Central cyanosis or hypoxemia on pulse oximetry
- Dyspnea and/or other signs of respiratory distress (e.g., retractions, use of accessory muscles of respiration, flaring of nostrils, speaking in short sentences, inability to speak, altered conscious state)
- Associated findings of poor growth, weight loss, digital clubbing, or general ill health
- Presence of risk factors for lower respiratory tract pathology, e.g., swallowing dysfunction, neuromuscular disorders

### CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 3-year-old boy presents to you with the problem of cough for the last 2 months. He has no significant past medical history, and an uneventful birth history with appropriate growth and developmental milestones to date. He is the second child of a nuclear family, and has an elder sister, who is attending school. The patient recently enrolled into a preschool 2.5 months ago. There is no personal or family history of asthma or allergies, and there is no cigarette smoke exposure in the home. The family lives in an apartment, which has been home for over 6 years. They do not have any pet.

The cough began with some runny nose and a low-grade fever, with the cough persisting to date. The cough is dry and occurs both day and night. It has a fluctuating course: After the initial cough for several days, the cough slowly waned over 1–2 weeks. This was followed by a few cough-free days. There was a sudden recurrence of the cough, apparently aggravated by running nose. There were two transient low-grade fever episodes in the past 2 months. He was seen by a doctor on at least two occasions and was noted to have some mild wheezing. The child was otherwise active and happy without evidence of breathing difficulty, apart from an occasional transient blocked nose. He has no history of regurgitation or symptoms associated with meals or posture. He was given some cough medications, which did not help significantly.

On examination, his temperature is 37.1°C, heart rate 94/min, respiratory rate 24/min, and oxygen saturation on room air 99%. His height is on the 50th percentile. His weight is on the 75th percentile. He is alert and active and playful. His throat, nose, and ears are normal. There is no digital clubbing or chest wall deformity. Heart sounds are normal. On auscultation of the lungs, there are vesicular breath sounds with no adventitious sounds. Percussion note and vocal resonance are normal and symmetrical. The abdomen is soft and nontender, and there is no organomegaly. No skin rashes or eczema.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- Chronic cough in an otherwise well child
- What is your diagnosis?
- Post-viral respiratory infection cough

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Dry and nonproductive cough that occurs both day and night.
- History suggestive of two separate episodes of viral respiratory infections followed by post-viral respiratory tract infection cough, i.e., a cough that had a fluctuating course. After the initial cough for several days, the cough slowly waned over 1–2 weeks. This was followed by a few cough-free days. There was a sudden recurrence of the cough, apparently aggravated by running nose. Risk factor for catching viral respiratory tract infection: school attendance, especially being newly introduced to school environment.

- Child is clinically well, growing and developing well, with no abnormal clinical findings and no evidence of chronic disease (no clubbing or chest wall deformity).

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- He was seen by a doctor on at least two occasions and was noted to have some mild wheezing.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- Information about any aggravating factor, e.g., supine position, environmental factors.
- Information about when those transient fevers occurred, i.e., at the start of the coughs.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Asthma
- Allergic rhinitis with postnasal drip

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Asthma: dry cough, chronic cough, happens in both day and night, the doctor's finding of wheezing.
- Allergic rhinitis with postnasal drip: dry cough, chronic cough, happens in both day and night, has runny nose.

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Asthma: no atopic history. The cough is not predominantly nocturnal.
- Allergic rhinitis with postnasal drip: the doctor's finding of wheezing. The cough is not predominantly nocturnal, and no information known about whether it is aggravated by being in a supine position. Apart from some runny nose, there are no other features suggestive of allergic rhinitis.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Two episodes of viral respiratory tract infections, each followed by post-viral infection cough

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- None.

- What is your plan for this patient?

- No need for any investigation.
- Reassure parents and monitor the symptom progress, looking out for any new sign or symptom, especially red flags.
- Recognize that the cough can cause interference with sleep and daytime function, as well as cause anxiety on both the caregivers and child.

- Advice on preventive measures to reduce viral infections, e.g., healthy lifestyle and balanced diet with adequate sleep and exercise. Also discuss the role of influenza vaccine.
- Will not use any cough suppressant. Suppressing the cough reflex disables the clearance of airway irritants and may result in damaging effects. In addition, according to the American Academy of Pediatrics, the use of cough medicines to relieve cough symptoms is supported by little evidence and thus not recommended for treating cough in children.

# Cyanosis

Michael T.C. Lim • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF CYANOSIS

- Cyanosis is defined as a bluish discoloration of the skin and mucosa. Cyanosis can be seen when the concentration of deoxygenated hemoglobin in the capillaries is 5 g/dL or more.
- Cyanosis can be central or peripheral.
- Central cyanosis is said to be present when cyanosis is seen centrally (in the tongue) and peripherally (e.g., in fingers, hands, toes, feet, lips). This occurs when the local tissue oxygen content and the systemic arterial oxygen content are low (hypoxemia).
- Peripheral cyanosis is said to be present when cyanosis is seen only peripherally (e.g., cyanosis in fingers, hands, toes, feet, and lips) but NOT in the tongue. This is often accompanied by mottling of the surrounding skin. This is seen when the local tissue oxygen content is low, but the systemic arterial oxygen content is normal. The most common reason is peripheral vasoconstriction, e.g., from cold temperatures.
- "Acrocyanosis" refers to bluish discoloration of the extremities.

# SYMPTOMS OF CYANOSIS

- Blue lips
- Blue tongue
- Bluish discoloration of the extremities (fingers, toes, hands, feet)

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

- Cyanosis is seen when the level of deoxygenated hemoglobin in the capillaries is 5 g/dL or more.
- The degree of oxygen desaturation needed to result in such levels of deoxygenated hemoglobin is dependent on the hemoglobin concentration.

- A child with a hemoglobin level of 18 g/dL will be centrally cyanosed when the arterial oxygen saturation drops to 85%.
- A child with a hemoglobin level of 9 g/dL will only be centrally cyanosed when the arterial oxygen saturation drops to below 65%.
- Therefore, in anemic children, central cyanosis is seen only in severe hypoxemia.

182

# COMMON CAUSES AND DIAGNOSES ASSOCIATED WITH CENTRAL CYANOSIS

Central cyanosis may be caused by:

- Problems with ventilation

- Severe upper airway obstruction
- Severe lower airway obstruction
- Muscles involved in breathing not working adequately

- Problems with perfusion or gas exchange

- Inability of blood to get to the alveoli
- Inadequate gas exchange at the alveolar membrane level

- Problems related to central control of ventilation

- Breath-holding spells
- Respiratory center not triggering respiration appropriately

1. Problems with Ventilation

- It means something is preventing air/oxygen from reaching the alveoli.
- This may be due to

- severe upper airway obstruction,

- severe lower airway obstruction, and/or

- muscles involved in breathing are not working adequately.

- Severe upper airway obstruction (if the obstruction is partial, it may manifest as stridor and drooling; if the obstruction is complete, it will usually manifest as inability to speak, inability to cough, increasing cyanosis, and loss of consciousness)

- Intraluminal causes of airway obstruction

- Foreign body/food aspiration
  - Swallowing dysfunction
  - Accidental choking
  - Gastroesophageal reflux, gastroesophageal reflux disease

- Secretions

  - Person with swallowing dysfunction (saliva)

  - Immunological (anaphylaxis with secretions)

- Large tonsils and adenoids (infection, malignancy, hypertrophy)

- Abnormal airway walls causing airway obstruction

- Infection of the upper airways (croup, tracheitis, epiglottitis)
- Immunological (anaphylaxis with swelling of upper airway mucosa)
- Injuries (inhaled noxious gases, inhaled noxious chemicals, inhaled hot air, physical trauma)

- Unfavorable anatomy

  - Craniofacial anomalies, e.g., Pierre-Robin sequence
  - Laryngeal lesions, e.g., laryngotracheobronchomalacia
  - Subglottic lesions, e.g., tracheomalacia and tracheal or subglottic stenosis

○ Extra-luminal mass causing airway compression

- Retropharyngeal abscess
- Tumor

• Severe lower air way obstruction (manifests as respiratory distress; may have additional sounds such as wheezing/rhonchi)

○ Intraluminal causes of airway obstruction

- Foreign body/food aspiration

  □ Swallowing dysfunction
  □ Accidental choking
  □ Gastroesophageal reflux with aspiration

- Secretions/fluids

  □ Aspiration of oral secretions
  □ Infection (bronchiolitis, pneumonia)
  □ Immunological (asthma, anaphylaxis with secretions)
  □ Inadequate clearance, e.g., immobility and atelectasis
  □ Pulmonary edema

○ Abnormal airway walls causing airway obstruction

- Infection of the lower airways causing bronchospasm (bronchiolitis, viral-induced wheeze)
- Immunological (asthma, anaphylaxis with bronchospasm)
- Injuries (inhaled noxious gases, inhaled noxious chemicals, inhaled hot air, physical trauma)
- Unfavorable anatomy, e.g., tracheomalacia, congenital lung malformation

○ Extra-luminal mass compressing the airway

- Vascular ring
- Enlarged mediastinal lymph node(s)
- Tumor

• Muscles involved in breathing not working adequately

○ At the level of the spinal cord and/or peripheral nerves

- For example, trauma to spinal cord, Guillain–Barre syndrome

○ At the level of the neuromuscular junction

- Myasthenia gravis
- Neuromuscular blocking drugs such as atracurium and succinylcholine

○ At the level of the muscle

- Splinting of diaphragm causing inadequate ventilation per breath, hence the need to breathe faster (e.g., abdominal pain, abdominal distension)
- Multiple rib fractures causing flail chest
- Myopathies and dystrophies

# 2. Problems with Perfusion or Gas Exchange

• It means something is preventing arterial blood from reaching the alveoli, or there is a problem of gas exchange at the alveolar membrane level.

• Inability of blood to get to the alveoli

o Shock

■ Distributive (e.g., sepsis, anaphylaxis)
■ Hypovolemic
■ Cardiogenic
■ Obstructive

o Pulmonary embolism (consider in immobile patients or those with the hypercoagulable state)

o Right to left shunt

■ Cyanotic congenital heart disease with decreased pulmonary blood flow and right to left shunt
□ Tetralogy of Fallot
□ Tricuspid atresia
□ Ebstein anomaly
□ Critical pulmonary stenosis

■ Cyanotic congenital heart disease with increased pulmonary blood flow and right to left shunt

□ Hypoplastic left heart syndrome (left to right shunt via patent foramen ovale and right to left shunt via ductus arteriosus)
□ Total anomalous pulmonary venous drainage
□ Truncus arteriosus

■ Transposition of the great arteries – where systemic and pulmonary circulations are in parallel, and dependent on right to left shunting to get fully saturated blood from pulmonary circulation to systemic circulation

■ Pulmonary hypertension with right to left shunt at ductus level or patent foramen ovale

□ Eisenmenger syndrome
□ Persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn

• Inadequate gas exchange at the alveolar membrane level

o Pneumothorax

o Pleural effusion

o Pulmonary edema

■ Intravascular fluid overload (renal failure, nephritic syndrome, nephritic-nephrotic syndrome, congestive heart failure)
■ Inadequate myocardial contractility

□ Supraventricular tachycardia (other cardiac arrhythmias less common in children)
□ Myocarditis (acquired)
□ Congenital heart disease (e.g., large ventricular septal defect, large patent ductus arteriosus)
□ Cardiac tamponade

■ Submersion injury (near drowning)

o Lung parenchymal disease (collapse, pneumonia/bronchopneumonia, pneumonitis, pulmonary contusion, prematurity associated respiratory

distress syndrome, transient tachypnea of the newborn, interstitial lung disease)

• Lack of adequate oxygen carrying capacity or inadequate oxygen dissociation

■ Polycythemia
■ Methemoglobinemia

### 3. Problems Related to Central Control of Ventilation

• Breath-holding spells – typically occurs at 6 months to 2 years of age
• Respiratory center not triggering respiration appropriately

• Causes of the altered mental state (see Chapter 5)

• Respiratory center not working adequately, e.g., central hypoventilation syndrome

# COMMON CAUSES AND DIAGNOSES ASSOCIATED WITH PERIPHERAL CYANOSIS

• Cold environment
• Fever
• Sepsis
• Shock (except for distributive shock, which is "warm" shock)
• Hypoglycemia
• Acrocyanosis, primary
• Hypothermia
• Raynaud phenomenon, which may be primary or secondary

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

### 1. Initial Impression

• Initial impression

• Assess appearance
• Assess work of breathing
• Assess circulation to skin (color)

• The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

• If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

■ Check pulse

☐ Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
☐ Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

■ When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

• If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

## 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

## 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

- History taking

  - Determine if it is really cyanosis (see Symptoms section above).
  - Determine if it is central or peripheral cyanosis (see Symptoms section above; confirm by measuring with a pulse oximeter).
  - If there is central cyanosis, determine how severe it is and determine the cause.

    - Is the patient able to cough, speak, or breathe?

      - Inability to cough, speak, or breathe suggests complete upper airway obstruction. This is an emergency and requires immediate attention. Do the Heimlich maneuver if the patient is alert; if patient has loss of consciousness, start basic life support and escalate; look for the cause of the upper airway obstruction.

    - Is there any unusual sound/noise, e.g., stridor or wheezing?

      - Look for causes of upper airway and lower airway obstruction.
      - See Chapter 41 on noisy breathing.

    - Is there cough or grunting without stridor or wheezing?

      - Look for causes of upper airway and lower airway obstruction.
      - Look for symptoms and causes of pneumothorax and pleural effusion.
      - Look for symptoms suggestive of pulmonary edema and its causes.
      - Look for symptoms and causes of lung parenchymal disease (collapse, pneumonia/bronchopneumonia, pneumonitis, pulmonary contusion, prematurity associated respiratory distress syndrome).

    - Is there only breathlessness/tachypnea even at rest (i.e. no stridor, no wheezing, usually no cough)?

      - Look for symptoms and causes of pneumothorax, pleural effusion, and collapse of the lung.
      - Look for causes of splinting of the diaphragm (any abdominal pain, distension).
      - Look for causes of inability of blood to get to the lungs.
      - Look for causes of inadequate oxygen carrying capacity or inadequate oxygen dissociation.
      - Look for congenital cyanotic heart disease with increased pulmonary flow.

■ Is the patient having central cyanosis and yet not having tachypnea at rest (i.e., inappropriate response to hypoxia)?

☐ Assess if the patient's mental status is intact/normal/altered. If yes, look for the cause.
☐ Assess if the patient's respiratory center can trigger respiration appropriately.
☐ Assess if the muscles involved in breathing are not working adequately.
☐ Assess for causes of inadequate oxygen carrying capacity or inadequate oxygen dissociation.
☐ Look for congenital cyanotic heart disease with decreased pulmonary flow.

■ If there is chronic central cyanosis, look for the cause. Do a systems review (including family history).

☐ Any underlying chronic respiratory disease?
☐ Any cardiac condition?
☐ Any neurological condition?
☐ Look for the effect on the child

- Effort tolerance
- Growth
- Development

○ If it is only peripheral cyanosis, look for the cause

■ Cold environment
■ Fever
■ Sepsis
■ Shock (except for distributive shock, which is "warm" shock)
■ Hypoglycemia
■ Acrocyanosis, primary
■ Hypothermia
■ Raynaud phenomenon, which may be primary or secondary

• Physical examination

○ Vital signs

■ High heart rate – do not assume that it is because the child is anxious, frightened, or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress.
■ Low heart rate – this can be seen when there is prolonged hypoxia and the child is dying.
■ High blood pressure – do not assume that it is because the child is anxious, frightened, or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress.
■ Low blood pressure – this can be seen when there is shock, when there is prolonged hypoxia, or when the child is dying.
■ High respiratory rate – do not assume that it is because the child is anxious, frightened, or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress.

- **Low respiratory rate or poor respiratory effort** – this can be seen when the central nervous system (CNS) is depressed, when the respiratory muscles are not working adequately, when the child is in severe respiratory distress, when the child is tiring out, or when the child is dying.

- **Temperature** – fever, hypothermia.

○ **Is there central cyanosis or peripheral cyanosis?**

- Look at the color of the tongue, lips, fingers, hands, toes, and feet.
- Look for clubbing (can be seen in chronic hypoxemia, lung disease, etc.).
- Look for polycythemia (can be seen in chronic hypoxemia but may be absent if the child is also iron deficient).
- Confirm with pulse oximetry. If there is decreased oxygenation and or central cyanosis, do not assume that the low SpO₂ value is due to poor contact with saturation probe or patient movement. It may really be low (hypoxemic).

○ **Is there respiratory distress? How severe is the respiratory distress?**

- Assess the severity of respiratory distress; see the table above (also see Chapter 12 on breathlessness, respiratory distress).

○ **Mental state of the child**

- Irritable child – do not assume that it is because the child is anxious, frightened, or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe hypoxia.
- Lethargic or decreased conscious state – may be a sign of severe hypoxia.

○ **Posture of the child**

- Leaning forward and bracing the shoulder girdle are signs of increased breathing effort required.

○ **Any unusual sound**

- Stridor (inspiratory, expiratory, or biphasic)
- Wheezing
- Grunting
- Coughing (wet sounding, dry sounding, barking cough, etc.)

○ **If there is any compromise in upper airway, particularly if severe, avoid upsetting the child as it can convert a partially obstructed airway into a completely obstructed airway; decide on whether there is a need for urgent escalation to the senior, ENT, and anesthesia.**

○ **Do a respiratory examination if there is no severe upper airway obstruction:**

- Chest wall
  - Signs of injury
  - Chest expansion increased, decreased, or asymmetrical
- Trachea position
- Apex beat

- Percussion note
- Air entry
- Adventitious sounds and their distribution

☐ Sounds

- Crepitations (fine or coarse)
- Rhonchi (inspiratory, expiratory, both)

☐ Distribution

- Unilateral/bilateral
- Corresponds to which part of the lung/anatomy

○ Do a detailed cardiovascular examination:

- Feel for the pulses
- Feel for the apex beat
- Listen to the heart sounds, murmur

○ Examine the rest of the body (including growth and development).

4. Algorithm

Continued from previous page

![img-26.jpeg](img-26.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Central cyanosis is a red flag for hypoxemia/low oxygen state.
- Central cyanosis is seen only in an anemic child when the hypoxemia is severe.
- Be vigilant of the child with central cyanosis and the following signs:

- Respiratory distress
- Stridorous breathing
- Apnea or hypoventilation
- Murmurs, right ventricular heave, thrill, single or narrow second heart sound (indicate cardiac pathology with right to left shunting)

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 4-year-old boy presents with a history of blue discoloration of his lips since he was 4 weeks old. He was asymptomatic in the first month, and gradually developed bluish discoloration of lips and skin, which worsened with crying and suckling. Since 3 months of age, this was associated with rapid breathing when crying and feeding. He has developed breathlessness on exertion over the last 1.5 years. Ordinary play with other children and climbing stairs cause breathlessness (NYHA II).

In recent months, he has been experiencing loss of consciousness amounting to three to four episodes per month during exertion, with each episode lasting 3–5 min. These episodes would either resolve with a squatting posture or calming by his mother.

There is no past medical history of recurrent respiratory tract infections, feeding problems, recent fever, vomiting, convulsions, or headaches. He was born at full-term by normal vaginal delivery. Birth weight was 2.3 kg. There were no maternal illnesses in the antenatal period, and antenatal scans were reported normal. There was no history of bluish discoloration, jaundice, or cyanosis during the neonatal period. The child is developmentally normal. The family history is noncontributory.

On examination, the patient is alert and active. His weight is 10 kg (<3rd percentile) and height is 95 cm (3rd percentile). Temperature is 37°C, heart rate is 98/min, respiratory rate is 22/min, blood pressure is 84/60 mmHg (left arm, supine), and SpO₂ is 82% on room air. There is no pallor or jaundice. He is cyanosed (lips, tongue, and fingers). There is clubbing of his fingers. There is no edema. There is no use of accessory muscle of respiration. There is no chest-wall deformity. Heart sounds are dual, with a loud single S2. There is a harsh systolic murmur grade 4/6 at the upper left sternal edge, which does not radiate to the back or axilla. Air entry is equal with no added sounds. His abdomen is soft and nontender. Liver edge is just palpable and soft. There is no other mass palpable. Examination of the central and peripheral nervous systems is normal.

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

What is the clinical problem?

Cyanosis with shortness of breath on exertion

What is your diagnosis?

Tetralogy of Fallot

List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

Cyanosis, clubbing.
Not tachypneic.
Air entry is equal with no added sounds.
History of hypoxic spells.
S1 and S2 audible with loud single S2. Systolic murmur grade 4/6 at the upper left sternal edge, which is harsh and not radiating to the back or axilla.

List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

Nil

If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information about ECG findings - expect right-axis deviation and right ventricular hypertrophy.
- No information about chest radiograph findings - expect decreased pulmonary markings (pulmonary oligemia) and a normal-sized heart but boot-shaped.
- No information about whether he is dysmorphic. Need to determine if this is just an isolated heart condition or the child has a syndrome or other organ involvement, e.g., 22q11 deletion syndrome, which is associated with this type of congenital heart disease, as well as failure to thrive.

What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

Another congenital heart condition that causes central cyanosis and decreased blood flow to the lung.

For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

Cyanosis, clubbing

- Heart murmur
- Not tachypneic
- Air entry is equal with no added sounds

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Hypoxic spells are usually not common in other congenital heart conditions.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Not sure. Cyanotic congenital heart disease is very specialized knowledge.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Tetralogy of Fallot

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Symptoms of chronic significant hypoxia and central cyanosis:

- Clubbing
- Shortness of breath on exertion
- Cyanotic spells with loss of consciousness (syncope) with exertion, indicating severe hypoxia
- Failure to thrive

- Symptoms and signs suggestive of congenital heart disease

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Referral to pediatric cardiology for echocardiogram to confirm diagnosis of Tetralogy of Fallot.
- Management of episodes of severe cyanotic spells

- Supplemental oxygen

- Morphine

- Knee-chest position

- Consider propranolol to prevent future spells and reduce severity

- Antibiotic prophylaxis when needed to reduce the risk of bacterial endocarditis
- Treat respiratory infections early.
- Consider flu vaccination.
- Consider looking for iron deficiency.
- Consider referral to the geneticist.
- Definitive treatment for Tetralogy of Fallot is surgery. Blalock-Taussig shunting procedure, aortic/pulmonary shunt, intracardiac repair, balloon pulmonary valvuloplasty, and/or valve replacement.
