# Dehydration

Jeremy Lin

# DEFINITION OF DEHYDRATION

- Dehydration is defined as depletion of total body water. Often, this is associated with various electrolyte abnormalities.
- Dehydration is often used interchangeably with hypovolemia or volume depletion.

# SYMPTOMS AND SIGNS OF DEHYDRATION

- Symptoms and signs of dehydration vary according to its severity.
- The severity is graded according to the amount of water loss as a percentage of body weight.
- Severe dehydration can result in death.
- There are three different types of dehydration (isonatremic, hyponatremic, hypernatremic).
- Table 19-1 shows the symptoms and signs that are most commonly associated with isonatremic dehydration (as opposed to hyponatremic or hypernatremic dehydration).

TABLE 19-1

SYMPTOMS AND SIGNS OF ISONATREMIC DEHYDRATION

|   | **Euvolemia** (Normal Body Fluid Volume) | **Mild Dehydration** | **Moderate Dehydration** | **Severe Dehydration**  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Body weight loss | 0%–2% | 3%–5% | 6%–9% | ≥10%  |
|  General condition | Alert, active, not thirsty | Alert, thirsty | Irritable/ lethargic, thirsty | Drowsy/ unconscious, thirsty  |
|  Urine output | Normal | Normal or slightly reduced | Reduced | Anuric  |
|  **Hemodynamics**  |   |   |   |   |
|  • Pulse volume | Full | Full | Full | Weak/low  |
|  • Pulse rate | Normal rate | Normal rate | Rapid rate | Rapid rate  |
|  • Blood pressure | Normal | Normal | Normal/low or postural fall | Low  |
|  Respiration | Normal | Normal | Deep, maybe rapid | Deep and rapid  |

196

TABLE 19-1

SYMPTOMS AND SIGNS OF ISONATREMIC DEHYDRATION—cont'd

|   | Euvolemia (Normal Body Fluid Volume) | Mild Dehydration | Moderate Dehydration | Severe Dehydration  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Skin |  |  |  |   |
|  • Temperature | Normal | Normal | Cool | Cool, mottled  |
|  • Turgor (skin pinching) | Normal | Normal | Reduced, returns slowly | Tenting, returns very slowly  |
|  • Capillary refill | Normal (<2 seconds) | Normal (<2 seconds) | (2–3 seconds) | (>3 seconds)  |
|  Other external changes |  |  |  |   |
|  • Anterior fontanelle (in <18 months old) | Normal | Normal | Sunken | Markedly sunken  |
|  • Buccal mucosa | Moist | Moist to slightly dry | Dry | Parched  |
|  • Eyes | Normal | Normal | Sunken | Markedly sunken  |
|  • Tears | Present | Present | Absent | Absent  |

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Normal Physiology

- The body is made up largely of water.
- Each day the body loses water

- A certain amount of obligate water loss through the skin and, to a lesser extent, through the stools. Obligate water loss is also known as insensible water loss.
- The body also loses water through urine output.

- In order to replace these losses, the body gets water by

- Absorbing water that has been ingested into the gut
- Producing water through the metabolism of food (this contributes only a very small amount)

TABLE 19-2

BODY FLUID COMPARTMENTS

|  TOTAL BODY WATER (TBW) – 60% OF BODY WEIGHT  |   |   |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Intracellular fluid (ICF) – 40% of body weight | Extracellular fluid (ECF) – 20% of body weight  |   |
|   | Interstitial fluid – three-fourth of ECF (15% of body weight) | Intravascular fluid (plasma) – one-fourth of ECF (5% of body weight)  |
|  Assessed clinically by looking at skin turgor, eye balls, and anterior fontanelle (if still open) | Difficult to assess clinically when interstitial fluid is low; pitting edema is seen when there is a large amount of interstitial fluid | Assessed by looking at the heart rate, blood pressure, jugular venous pressure, capillary refill, tears, urine output  |

- When any water loss is not adequately replaced, several physiological processes kick in:

○ Thirst:

■ When the body's water decreases, the osmolality will rise. At a certain threshold, this will trigger a sensation of thirst. The sensation becomes stronger as the body's osmolality rises, motivating a person to drink the needed fluids. When the body's osmolality is restored, thirst is suppressed.

○ Vasopressin:

■ When the body's osmolality rises even further, it triggers the pituitary gland to secrete vasopressin (also called antidiuretic hormone [ADH]) into the bloodstream. Vasopressin stimulates the kidneys to conserve water and excrete less urine by increasing water resorption in the distal tubule. This manifests as a decrease in urine output. When the body's osmolality is restored, the pituitary gland secretes little vasopressin, enabling the kidneys to excrete excess water in the urine.

○ Fluid shifts from one compartment to the other:

■ Water flows passively (by osmosis) from one compartment of the body to another. This passive flow allows the larger volumes of fluid in the cells and the interstitial space to act as reservoirs to maintain the intravascular volume.

# 2. Pathophysiology of Dehydration

- Dehydration occurs when the rate of total body fluid loss is greater than the rate of water absorption.
- Regardless of the cause, children are at higher risk than adults for dehydration because

○ Children have a higher body surface area-to-volume ratio and, accordingly, a proportionally higher insensible water loss than adults.
○ Young children are less able to communicate their thirst and/or cannot independently access the fluids they need.
○ Children have a higher frequency of gastroenteritis (diarrhea and vomiting) than adults.

- The causes of dehydration are loss of body fluid, inadequate water intake/absorption.

○ Loss of water/body fluid through the following organs:

■ Gastrointestinal tract (most common): vomiting, diarrhea, losses through surgically created stoma
■ Kidneys: central diabetes insipidus, glucosuria in diabetes mellitus (causing osmotic diuresis), diuretic therapy, chronic kidney disease with concentrating defects
■ Skin and mucous membranes: insensible water loss through the skin because of fever, burns, scalds, hot ambient temperature, and persistent tachypnea of any cause

• Inadequate water intake/absorption

■ Inability to access water: altered mental state, immobile, inability to communicate need for water, lack of water supply
■ Inability to respond to thirst: altered mental state, severe neurological disease
■ Decreased intake:

□ Intentional e.g., eating disorder

□ Unintentional e.g., poor appetite due to fever, pain, or concurrent illness

■ Inability to retain e.g., vomiting
■ Inability to absorb e.g., gastroenteritis, inflammatory bowel disease, short gut syndrome

# 3. Three Different Types of Dehydration (isonatremic, hypernatremic, hyponatremic)

• Isonatremic dehydration (80% of the cases of dehydration)

○ Serum sodium is 130–150 mmol/L.
○ This is the most common type of dehydration.
○ It occurs when there are equal losses of sodium and water, e.g., in viral gastroenteritis where the diarrheal fluid is isonatremic.
○ The fluid loss is proportionally equal in the intracellular fluid (ICF) and extracellular fluid (ECF) compartments.
○ The child often manifests with the usual physical signs of dehydration (skin and other external changes) (Table 19-2).

• Hypernatremic dehydration (15% of the cases of dehydration)

○ Serum sodium is >150 mmol/L.
○ This occurs when there are more water losses than sodium losses, e.g., insensible fluid losses, urinary concentrating defects (diabetes insipidus).
○ As a result of the hypernatremia (and hence increased serum osmolality), water shifts from the ICF to ECF.
○ The child often appears less ill compared to a child with a similar degree of isonatremic dehydration. Examination clues are "doughy" skin, decreased skin turgor, sunken eyeballs, but yet heart rate and blood pressure are not as severely affected (because the intravascular volume is initially preserved).

• Hyponatremic dehydration (5% of the cases of dehydration)

○ Serum sodium is <130 mmol/L.
○ This occurs when there are more sodium losses than water losses, e.g., losses of fluid with high sodium (renal salt wasting, cholera diarrhea, third-space losses).
○ As a result of hyponatremia (and hence decreased serum osmolality), water shifts from ECF to ICF.
○ The child often appears more ill compared to a child with a similar degree of isonatremic dehydration. Examination clues are fairly good skin turgor, but yet the heart rate and blood pressure are more severely affected.

![img-27.jpeg](img-27.jpeg)

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

5.5 Chapter 1: Dehydration

# 1. Is There Dehydration? Are There Risk Factors for Dehydration?

- The diagnosis of dehydration is based on the history and physical examination of the child:

- What is the fluid intake like?
- What are the fluid losses like?
- What is the balance between intake and output?
- Are there symptoms and signs of dehydration? The earliest symptom of dehydration is thirst. The earliest sign of dehydration is a decrease in urine output. (See Table 19-1 for features seen in isotonic dehydration.)
- Are there risk factors for dehydration? (See causes for dehydration as listed above.)

- Risk factors for increased losses.
- Risk factors for reduced oral water intake.

- The child's weight is a useful indicator of hydration status because any acute weight loss is usually due to loss of body water. However, a weight just prior to illness is not often available.

- If there is no dehydration and there are no risk factors, monitor child.
- If there is no dehydration but there are risk factors for dehydration:

- Treat risk factors.
- Provide sufficient fluid to maintain euvolemia.
- Monitor hydration status.

# 2. If There Is Dehydration, Assess the Degree and Type of the Dehydration and Determine How Best to Treat It

- Use history and physical examination to determine the degree and type of dehydration. (Use Table 19-1 to assess the degree of the dehydration if it is isotonic dehydration.)
- The degree of dehydration and type of dehydration will determine the urgency, rate, and route of fluid replacement therapy.
- For mild dehydration

- Treat risk factors.
- If able to tolerate orally, trial of oral fluid (volume = maintenance + replacement).
- If the child refuses to take orally but has no excessive gut losses, enteral tube feeds is an option.
- If unable to tolerate oral feeds, give intravenous fluids (maintenance and replacement).
- Monitor the hydration status.

- For moderate dehydration

- Determine the type of dehydration (isonatremic, hyponatremic, hypernatremic):

- Clinically
- Biochemically

- Determine if there is any complication (acute kidney injury, acid-base, electrolyte, or glucose abnormalities).
- Treat risk factors and complications.
- Start intravenous fluids (volume and type of fluid are dependent on the type of dehydration and presence of complications).
- If able to tolerate orally, encourage oral fluid intake while on IV fluids
- Enteral tube feeds are still an option, and especially useful in children whose venous access is difficult.
- Monitor the hydration status and state of complications.
- May need to monitor electrolytes.

# • For severe dehydration

- Give intravenous fluid bolus/resuscitation 10–20 mL/kg 0.9% saline and assess response.
- Determine the type of dehydration (isonatremic, hyponatremic, hypernatremic):

  - Clinically

  - Biochemically

- Determine if there is any complication (acute kidney injury, acid-base, electrolyte or glucose abnormalities).
- Treat risk factors and complications.
- Start intravenous fluids (volume and type of fluid are dependent on the type of dehydration and presence of complications),
- If able to tolerate orally, encourage oral fluid intake while on IV fluids
- Enteral tube feeds are still an option, and especially useful in children whose venous access is difficult.
- Monitor the hydration status and state of complications.
- May need to monitor electrolytes.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Altered mental state (severe dehydration, hypotension, uremia, acidosis, hyponatremia, hypoglycemia)
- Seizure (hyponatremia, hypoglycemia)
- Signs and symptoms of severe dehydration, including shock (see Table 19-1)
- Hypernatremia: rapid rehydration causing cerebral edema and coning
- Hyponatremia: may be a risk factor for seizure
- Hypokalemia

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 8-month-old boy presents to you with an acute 2-day history of vomiting and diarrhea associated with fever. There is a positive contact history with his older sibling, who had similar symptoms 4 days ago.

Over the past day, he has not been able to tolerate his usual milk feeds. He vomits soon after every feed. He has profuse watery stools,

about 10 episodes in the past 24 hours. He had a fever up to 39°C. He has become increasingly tired. His parents are not sure about his urine output.

On examination, his temperature is 38.1°C, heart rate 162/min, blood pressure 80/50 mmHg, respiratory rate 40/min, and oxygen saturation on room air is 97%. His weight is 7.9 kg, compared to 8.5 kg a week ago (7% loss). He looks drowsy but is still arousable. His pulse is thready. He has mottled skin with cool peripheries and a capillary refill time of 3 seconds. He has tented skin on pinching. His eyes and anterior fontanelle are sunken. His buccal mucosa and tongue appear dry. His abdomen is soft with no organomegaly. His bowel sounds are hyperactive. There is no palpable bladder. He is breathing deeply with each breath. His heart and lung examinations were normal.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Acute vomiting and diarrhea with risk factors for dehydration
- Signs and symptoms of severe dehydration

- What is your diagnosis?

- Acute viral gastroenteritis with severe dehydration

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Acute viral gastroenteritis:

- Vomiting, diarrhea, fever, and positive contact history

- Risk factors for severe dehydration

- High-risk patient <1 year old
- High-risk history with significant losses and decreased intake

- Severe dehydration

- Drowsy but arousable
- Tachycardia out of proportion to fever

- Weak pulse
- Poor peripheral perfusion (mottled skin, cool to touch)
- Skin tenting
- Sunken fontanelle
- Dry mucosal surfaces
- Sunken eyes

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

○ Weight loss is 7% (10% or more dehydration is seen with severe dehydration; but bear in mind that hyponatremic and hypernatremic dehydration may not look like isotonic dehydration).

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

○ Urine output should be low, but this is difficult to accurately ascertain on history.
○ No information about serum sodium to confirm isonatremic type of dehydration.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

○ Bacterial gastroenteritis with dehydration and maybe septic shock

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

○ Symptoms of vomiting, diarrhea.
○ Looks quite ill (drowsy but arousable, tachycardia out of proportion to fever, weak pulse, mottled skin, cool to touch).
○ Insufficient weight loss to account for severe dehydration.
○ Bacterial gastroenteritis can be infectious so a positive family history of diarrhea is supportive of this.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

○ No history of bloody diarrhea

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

○ No history of blood or mucoid diarrhoea (but not all bacterial gastroenteritis is associated with bloody or mucoid diarrhea)

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Acute viral gastroenteritis with severe dehydration

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ Severe dehydration with risk for impending shock, end-organ damage and death.
○ There is an altered mental state (can be due to dehydration, uremia, acidosis, hyponatremia, hypoglycemia).
○ There is possible metabolic acidosis (tachypnea and deep respirations).
○ There is risk of acute kidney injury.

- What is your plan for this patient?

○ Secure intravenous access.
○ Carry out a capillary blood glucose. Correct hypoglycemia if present.
○ Obtain blood for serum creatinine, urea, electrolytes (sodium, potassium, chloride, bicarbonate), and glucose.
○ Fluid resuscitation with 100 mL of 0.9% saline (approx. 10 mL/kg) and repeat as necessary after assessing hemodynamic response and ensuring end-organ perfusion is adequately achieved.

- Review results to check the type of dehydration (isonatremic/hypotremia/hypernatremia) and presence of other complications, e.g., acute kidney injury, electrolyte imbalance.
- Admit the patient for further monitoring and management.
- Total intravenous fluids ordered must include fluid deficit, maintenance fluid requirements, and ongoing losses. (Type of fluid is dependent on the type of dehydration.)
- Cannot encourage oral fluid intake at this point in time as the child is drowsy and hence has risk for aspiration.
- Symptomatic treatment for fever and gastroenteritis.

# Developmental Delay

Chong Shang Chee • Denise L.M. Goh

• Jennifer S.H. Kiing

# DEFINITION OF DEVELOPMENTAL DELAY

- The four domains of development are (1) gross motor, (2) vision and fine motor, (3) hearing and speech, and (4) personal social domains.
- Developmental delay by definition is 2 standard deviations (SDs) below the mean, i.e., below the 97th percentile when compared to age-matched peers.
- In clinical practice, the 90th percentile is used as the limit for normal development. i.e., at this age, 90% of the age-matched peers are able to achieve this milestone. This cut-off is used to define the "red flags" for development as there are published clinical charts based on this cut-off.
- Developmental delay can be isolated (delay in one domain only) or global (delay in two or more domains).
- The term "developmental delay" is usually used for a child who is under 6 years of age.
- Any developmental delay beyond 6 years of age tends to be associated with longer term impairments in learning or function. Delays in this age group are more likely to be described using the term "'disability."

# SYMPTOMS OF DEVELOPMENTAL DELAY

- The child is usually brought to attention because the child is perceived to be slower than peers of the same age.
- Sometimes the child is brought to attention because the developmental gap between the siblings is widening.
- Global developmental delay may first be noticed as an isolated developmental delay, but over time, the delays in the other domains will become obvious.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Proper functioning of the brain, its related central nervous system structures, peripheral nervous system, muscle, and special sense organs are all needed for normal development.
- Perturbation in one or more of these structures can result in an isolated or global developmental delay.

# 1. Causes of Isolated Developmental Delay

- Isolated gross motor delay

  - Genetics/biological, e.g., maturational delays, family history of late walking

206

- Neurological cause, e.g., cerebral palsy
- Muscular cause, e.g., Duchenne muscular dystrophy
- Environmental, e.g., use of walker

- Isolated fine motor delay

- Vision impairment
- Environmental, e.g., lack of fine motor opportunity

- Isolated speech delay

- Genetics/biological, e.g., hearing impairment, maturational speech delay (only expressive language is affected), and developmental language disorder (usually both receptive and expressive language delays)

- Environmental, e.g., inadequate linguistic stimulation or exposure

- Isolated personal social delay

- Environmental, e.g., neglect, social deprivation, and excessive screen time

# 2. Causes of Global Developmental Delay (GDD)

- Causes (see Table 20-1)

- Conditions that affect the brain (i.e., central causes)

- Genetics/biological factors
- Environmental factors

TABLE 20-1

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF GLOBAL DEVELOPMENTAL DELAY

|  First Onset of Signs and Symptoms | Genetics/Biological Factors | Environmental Factors  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Prenatal | - Chromosomal disorders - Single gene disorders - Other genetic syndromes and associations - Cortical malformations/cerebral dysgenesis - Intrauterine infections - Familial history of delays/developmental disorders | - Other teratogens (maternal drug use, e.g., alcohol, tobacco, illicit drugs, environmental toxins, severe maternal illness, maternal malnutrition)  |
|  Perinatal | - Prematurity - Birth asphyxia/hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy - Birth injuries - Central nervous system infections - Intracranial hemorrhage - Other neonatal illness or infection | - Parental rejection or neglect - Maternal stress or depression  |

Continued

TABLE 20-1

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF GLOBAL DEVELOPMENTAL DELAY—Cont'd

|  First Onset of Signs and Symptoms | Genetics/Biological Factors | Environmental Factors  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Postnatal | - Traumatic birth injury - Hypoxic–ischemic insult to the brain - Intracranial hemorrhage/thrombosis - Meningitis, encephalitis - Seizure disorders - Degenerative brain disorders - Raised intracranial pressure - Hypothyroidism - Other chronic medical illness | - Inadequate environmental stimulation - Impaired child–caregiver relationship - Severe family poverty or stress - Child abuse and neglect - Drugs/poisons - Severe malnutrition - Prolonged institutionalization  |

• These conditions can have an onset at the

- Prenatal period (from conception to birth)
- Perinatal period (38 weeks to 4 weeks post delivery)
- Postnatal period (any time after birth)

• Manifestations

• Delays in two or more domains (see Tables 20-2 to 20-5)

TABLE 20-2

NORMAL GROSS MOTOR MILESTONES AND RED FLAGS FOR DELAYED DEVELOPMENT

|  Domain – Gross Motor | Normal 50th Percentile (i.e., 50% of Children Have Achieved This) | Signs of Developmental Delay (Red Flags)  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Newborn | Significant head lag when pulled to sit Has primitive reflexes – step, Moro, Babinski, Atonic Neck Reflex (ATNR) | Very floppy baby (frog-like posture, etc.)  |
|  3 months | Head control steady | Significant head lag  |
|  6 months | Rolls over in both directions Supports body on extended arm when in prone position Sits with support Bears weight | Does not roll over Appears stiff Appears floppy  |
|  9 months | Sits independently Crawls on hands and knees Pulls to stand Cruising | Does not sit unsupported Does not weight bear  |
|  12 months | Stands unsupported Walks a few steps | Does not stand with support  |
|  18 months | Walks up stairs – one step at a time (holding on) Begins to run | Does not walk independently  |
|  2 year | Kicks a ball without falling over Runs Jumps with 2 feet off the ground | Does not walk steadily Does not walk upstairs holding on  |

TABLE 20-2

NORMAL GROSS MOTOR MILESTONES AND RED FLAGS FOR DELAYED DEVELOPMENT—Cont'd

|  Domain – Gross Motor | Normal 50th Percentile (i.e., 50% of Children Have Achieved This) | Signs of Developmental Delay (Red Flags)  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  3 years | Rides a tricycle (three wheels) Stands on one foot for 3 seconds Walks upstairs alternating feet | Falls a lot Does not run or jump  |
|  4 years | Hops on one foot Walks downstairs alternating feet | Does not stand on one foot for 3 seconds  |
|  5 years | Skips, stands on one foot for 10 seconds | Does not hop on one foot  |

TABLE 20-3

NORMAL VISION AND FINE MOTOR MILESTONES AND RED FLAGS FOR DELAYED DEVELOPMENT

|  Domain – Vision and Fine Motor | Normal 50th Percentile (i.e., 50% of Children Have Achieved This) | Signs of Developmental Delay (Red Flags)  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Newborn | Able to look momentarily at a person's face 10–12 cm away Has primitive reflex – grasp | Does not look at a person's face 10–12 cm away  |
|  3 months | Follows past midline Tries to reach for objects | Does not follow to midline Does not track a face or objects  |
|  6 months | Reaches out for objects Hands come to the midline Transfers objects (7 months) | Does not reach out for objects  |
|  9 months | Pincer grasp Poke at objects | Does not transfer objects  |
|  12 months | Cast objects Puts a cube in a cup | No pincer grasp  |
|  18 months | Scribbles Builds a tower of two to four cubes | Not able to put a cube in a cup  |
|  2 years | Copies a vertical line (2.5 years) Builds a tower of up to six cubes | Does not scribble Does not build a tower of four cubes  |
|  3 years | Copies a circle (3 years) Copies a cross (3.5 years) Builds a tower of up to nine cubes Builds a tunnel with three bricks | Does not copy a vertical line Does not build a tower of six cubes  |
|  4 years | Copies a square, X Cuts shape with scissors | Does not build a tower of nine cubes Does not copy a circle  |
|  5 years | Copies a triangle | Does not copy a square Does not draw pictures  |

TABLE 20-4

NORMAL HEARING AND SPEECH MILESTONES AND RED FLAGS FOR DELAYED DEVELOPMENT

|  Domain – Hearing and Speech | Normal 50th Percentile (i.e., 50% of Children Have Achieved This) | Signs of Developmental Delay (Red Flags)  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Newborn | Responds and startles to loud sounds Quietens to mother's voice | Does not startle or respond to loud sounds  |
|  3 months | Turns to voice Coos ("ah," "oh" sounds) | Does not coo or make sounds Does not respond to loud noises  |
|  6 months | Babbles (nonspecific) | Does not respond to sounds in the environment Does not coo ("ah, oh" sounds)  |
|  9 months | Uses "mama," "dada" nonspecifically Understands "no" Gestures "bye bye," "up" | Does not babble sounds that start with consonants (e.g., "mamama," "bababa")  |
|  12 months | Uses one word with meaning (besides "mama," "dada") Follow a one-step command with gesture | Does not say single words like "mama" or "dada" Does not wave or shake head to mean "no"  |
|  18 months | Points to one body part Follow a one-step command with no gesture Has around 10 words | Says less than five words  |
|  2 years | Has two to three word phrases Speech is 50% understandable Has 50+ words Follows a two-step command | Does not use two-word phrases Less than 15 single words Does not follow a simple one-step instruction Does not point to two pictures  |
|  3 years | Has three to four word phrases Speech is 75% understandable Has ~200 words Follows a three-step command Asks "Why" questions Understands prepositions – in, on | No three-word phrases No sentences Does not understand a two-step instruction Speech is unclear Does not name four pictures  |
|  4 years | Has sentences Speech is 100% understandable Says full name Tells a story Uses past tense | Does not understand a three-step command Does not understand "same" and "different"  |
|  5 years | Has 5000 words Uses future tense Understands jokes | Does not say name Does not use plural and past tense Does not describe daily activities Speech is not 100% understandable  |

TABLE 20-5

NORMAL PERSONAL SOCIAL MILESTONES AND RED FLAGS FOR DELAYED DEVELOPMENT

|  Domain – Personal Social | Normal 50th Percentile (i.e., 50% of Children Have Achieved This) | Red Flags (Signs of Developmental Delay)  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Newborn | Comforted by a familiar adult Quietens when picked up | Does not quieten to mother's voice  |
|  3 months | Smiles spontaneously Social smile at the mother | No social smile  |
|  6 months | Expresses emotions Knows a familiar face Likes to interact with people May be starting to have stranger anxiety Likes to look at self in the mirror Responds to play Laughs | Does not give affection to caregivers  |
|  9 months | Afraid of strangers Has favorite toys | Does not respond to name Does not look to objects that are pointed out  |
|  12 months | Cries when separated from a parent Plays peek-a-boo Points to share interest | No eye contact with familiar people  |
|  18 months | Anxious with strangers Simple pretend play – feed a doll, use a phone | Does not copy others No simple pretend play Does not point to share interest  |
|  2 years | Copies others Plays parallel next to another child Tests limits Uses "no" and "mine" | Does not know what to do with common household things like spoon, phone Is not interested in other children  |
|  3 years | Matches colors Identifies one color Identifies shapes Dresses and undresses self Has pretend play with dolls, animals, and people | Does not do simple puzzles Does not have pretend play Does not want to play with other children  |
|  4 years | Identifies four colors Remembers parts of a story Has a preferred friend | No interest in games Ignores other children and nonfamiliar people  |
|  5 years | Counts to 10 Recites ABCs Recognizes some letters Understands real from imagined Has a group of friends Follows rules | Does not play games Not able to do activities of daily living, i.e., brush teeth, undress Cannot tell real from imagined  |

![img-28.jpeg](img-28.jpeg)

Continued on next page

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

Continued from previous page

![img-29.jpeg](img-29.jpeg)

# 1. Is the Development Normal or Delayed?

- Ask for the age of the child.
- Ask, in particular, if the child is premature. If the child is premature, assess using the corrected age (but only up to 2 years of corrected age).
- Take a developmental history and carry out a developmental assessment.
- See Tables 20-2 to 20-5. They indicate the expected milestones for each domain for each age group. They also list symptoms suggestive of delay and also the red flags.

- Assess the child's developmental milestones in each domain (by history and developmental assessment) and decide if a delay and/or red flag is present.
- Determine how many domains are affected.
- Things to note about milestones charts

- In the Tables 20-2 to 20-5, "normal milestones" refer to the 50th percentile, i.e., half of all children achieve this milestone at a younger age, whereas the other half are older when they achieve this milestone.
- If a child has not reached a "normal milestone," this does not necessarily mean they are delayed.
- The uppermost limit of achieving a milestone is the 90th percentile. This is the age at which 90% of typically developing children would have achieved that milestone.
- The "red flags" are signs of developmental delay.
- If you are reading other sources, please pay attention to what is their definition of normal.

## 2. If Delay Affects One Domain (Isolated Delay)

- Which domain is affected?

- Isolated gross motor delay?
- Isolated fine motor delay?
- Isolated speech delay? Is it receptive speech alone? Or expressive speech? Or both?
- Isolated personal social delay?

- Determine the cause/etiology

- Do a thorough history (including onset, course, any preceding trigger/event, associated symptoms, systems review, antenatal history, birth history, perinatal history, gestation age, growth, family history, caregiver, social history).
- Observe the child's behavior and interaction with the family and caregivers.
- Carry out a thorough physical examination, including plotting growth percentiles.

- For isolated gross motor delay

- The causes are genetics/biological, neurological, muscular, and environmental.
- If the cause is not clear, consider investigating for an underlying cause or referring to a specialist for further evaluation.
- If the cause is clear, determine if input from another specialist is required. Refer as needed.

- For isolated fine motor delay

- The common causes are vision impairment and environmental, e.g., lack of fine motor opportunity.
- Refer the child for formal vision testing (screening by waving an object in front of a child's line of vision is unreliable, especially if the vision impairment is mild to moderate).

# • For isolated speech delay

o Causes of expressive speech delay only:

■ Familial, maturational speech delay, developmental language disorder, and hearing impairment.
■ Environmental, e.g., inadequate linguistic stimulation or exposure.

o Causes of both expressive and receptive speech delay:

■ Hearing impairment and developmental language disorder (usually both receptive and expressive language delays).
■ Environmental, e.g., inadequate linguistic stimulation or exposure.

o Refer the child for formal hearing testing (screening hearing with a bell is unreliable, especially if the hearing impairment is mild to moderate).

# • For isolated personal social delay

o The causes are usually environmental, e.g., neglect, social deprivation, and excessive screen time.

• If there is evidence of vision impairment, refer the child for formal vision testing (screening by waving an object in front of a child's line of vision is unreliable, especially if the vision impairment is mild to moderate).
• If there is evidence of hearing impairment, refer the child for formal hearing test.
• Keep in mind that delay involving only one domain may be the first sign of global developmental delay. If there is global developmental delay, the delays in the other domains will become obvious with them. Hence, it is important to review and reassess the child periodically.

# 3. If Delay Affects Two or More Domains (Global Developmental Delay)

• Refer the child for formal hearing testing (screening hearing with a bell is unreliable, especially if the hearing impairment is mild to moderate).
• Refer the child for formal vision testing (screening by waving an object in front of a child's line of vision is unreliable, especially if the vision impairment is mild to moderate).

# • Which two or more domains are affected?

o Only gross motor and fine motor delays (consider a neuromuscular cause).
o Only speech and personal social delays (consider autism spectrum disorder, hearing impairment).
o For all other combinations of domains (consider a central cause).
o All domains delayed (consider a central cause).

# • Do a thorough history to determine the cause/etiology, look for complications:

o Obtain information about the onset. Prenatal? Perinatal? Postnatal? After an event?
o Obtain information about the course of the child's development. The various patterns are

■ The child is making progress (static cause).
■ The child's development has plateaued (static cause, first sign of regressive condition).

■ The child regressed (other further static insults, repeated insults, neuroregressive condition).

○ Obtain information about the parent's or caregiver's impression about whether there was a preceding event or trigger.

○ Ask for associated symptoms

■ History may be suggestive of genetic/biological causes (see the pathophysiology section).
■ History may be suggestive of environmental causes (see the pathophysiology section).

○ Obtain information about antenatal history, birth history, perinatal history, gestational age, and newborn screening and the results.

○ Carry out a systems review, including growth.

○ Look for complications (swallowing dysfunction, aspiration, reflux, seizures, ambulation-related issues, scoliosis, contractures, sores, etc.)

○ Obtain a family history. It may reveal a history of family members with a similar condition. Is there consanguinity? Are there recurrent miscarriages, abnormal babies, etc.?

○ Obtain a social history: who is the caregiver, what kind of care does the child get, socioeconomic status, family dynamics, caregiver skill, screen time.

• Physical examination:

○ Observe the child's behavior and interaction with the family and caregivers. Look for evidence of caregiver competence, risk for non-accidental injuries, etc.

○ Carry out a thorough physical examination, including plotting growth percentiles

○ Plot serial data points as far as possible. Pay close attention to the head size. What is the trend? Plateauing? Growing too fast? Not growing enough?

○ Some of the possible signs and their interpretation are listed below:

■ Short stature: suspect hypothyroidism, Turner syndrome, other genetic conditions.
■ Short and obese: suspect Prader–Willi syndrome, hypothyroidism, panhypopituitarism.
■ Tall stature: suspect Klinefelter syndrome.
■ Failure to thrive: see Chapter 25.
■ Head size

□ If it is increasing too fast (i.e., crossing percentiles) or if there is macrocephaly, consider excluding causes of raised intracranial pressure, some syndromes, some inborn errors of metabolism.

□ If the head circumference is plateauing or if it is not growing as fast (i.e., crossing percentiles), or if there is microcephaly, consider many of the causes listed in Table 20-1.

■ Dysmorphism

□ Suspect a genetic condition, e.g., chromosomal disorder, single gene defect.

☐ Suspect some intrauterine infections and some intrauterine teratogenic exposure (drugs, poisons).

■ Neurocutaneous stigmata

☐ Café au lait lesions: suspect neurofibromatosis type 1.

☐ Ash leaf macules: suspect tuberous sclerosis complex.

■ Abnormal neurological signs: many of the listed causes are associated with abnormal neurological signs.

■ Other organ involved: suspect genetic conditions, intrauterine infections, intrauterine teratogenic exposure, end-organ damage as a result of previous prematurity or hypoxic ischemic injury, other chronic illness.

■ Symptoms and signs of non-accidental injuries.

■ Symptoms and signs of lack of caregiver skills.

■ Symptoms and signs of seizures.

■ Family history of global developmental delay, recurrent miscarriages, early deaths or consanguinity: suspicious of a genetic cause.

• Look for other comorbidities that commonly exist in patients with global developmental delay:

○ Seizures, swallowing dysfunction, aspiration pneumonia, atelectasis, malnutrition, lack of mobility and its related issues, musculoskeletal issues (scoliosis, contractures).

○ Caregiver stress, financial strain, strain on family and marital relationships.

• If the cause is obvious, determine if input from another specialist is required. Refer as needed.

• If the cause is not obvious, consider investigating for an underlying cause or referring to a specialist for further evaluation.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

• The red flags in the tables are indications of delay and require that the child be referred for evaluation.
• The red flags listed below suggest that there is a serious underlying medical condition requiring urgent further evaluation and treatment (ensure that this child is seen urgently by the relevant experts):

○ Regression at ANY time in the child's life
○ Symptoms of raised intracranial pressure
○ Suspicion of neglect or non-accidental injuries (see Chapter 37 on injuries)
○ Previously undiagnosed seizures

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A mother presents to clinic with her 3-year-old son because of concerns about his speech development.

• Developmental history

○ Gross motor – He walked by 14 months, ran by 2 years. He is able to walk upstairs and downstairs with alternating steps. He is not able to hop on one foot.

- Vision and fine motor – He uses a palmar grasp. He can build a tower of three cubes. He is scribbling with a crayon and he cannot copy a vertical line.
- Hearing and speech – He uses around 20 single words, including "papa," "mama," "milk," and "dog." He does not use two-word phrases. When he wants something, he points to what he wants and says "ah, ah" and gestures a great deal. He waves "bye bye" and does a flying kiss. He does not consistently follow a two-step instruction, though he can understand a one-step instruction without gestures.
- Personal social – He is able to match colors. He can undress himself. He has pretend play with a cocking set and cooks meals for his mother to share in. He shares his toys with other children.

- Antenatal and perinatal history: normal pregnancy. There is no history of exposure to drugs or alcohol. Antenatal screens were normal. He was born at term with a birth weight of 2.7 kg. There were no complications after birth.

- Family history: Parents are nonconsanguineous. His 4-year-old cousin on his mother's side has developmental delay, but no further details are known.

- Social history: Both parents work long hours. He is cared for by his elderly grandmother and a domestic helper. He is the only child and not yet in school. The domestic helper speaks reasonable English but most of the time does not talk to or play with him as she is busy with household chores. Grandmother speaks a Chinese dialect. He gets about 2–3 hours of screen time a day.

Developmental assessment done was consistent with the developmental history given. On examination, his height and weight are on the 25th percentile and his head circumference is on the 50th percentile. He does not appear dysmorphic. Neurological examination is normal apart from an intermittent strabismus. He has one café au lait lesion measuring <5 mm on his right thigh and a Mongolian spot over his buttock.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.
- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- This boy has delays in two domains: fine motor and speech domains.

- What is your diagnosis?

- Global developmental delay.

- Aggravating factors may include the lack of English language stimulation, too much screen time, and few opportunities for interactive play in the home setting.

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- This boy is 3 years old.

- His fine motor domain is around an 18-month level because he scribbles with a crayon but is not yet copying a vertical line, which is a 2-year-old task. He can build a tower of three cubes so he is at an 18-month level but not at the 2-year level of building six cubes.

- His speech domain is around an 18-month level because he can say up to 20 single words and has gestures to communicate. He has not achieved a 2-year-old level of using two word phrases. He can follow a one-step instruction without gestures, which also corresponds to an 18-month-old level.

- His personal social domain is at a 3-year level as he does have pretend play with others and he is willing to share his toys. He can match colors. He is affectionate with his mother and grandmother.

- His gross motor milestones are at a 3-year level because he can walk upstairs with alternating steps but not hop with one foot, which is a 4-year level task.

- History suggests a poor home situation with poor language modeling.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- Should have hearing tested and hearing should be normal.

- Should have vision tested and the vision should be normal.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Hearing impairment

- Vision impairment

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Vision impairment can also cause fine motor delays (cannot see well enough to perform fine motor tasks well).

- Hearing impairment can explain why his speech is delayed.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

o Vision impairment

■ He has speech delay.
■ He has appropriate gross motor skills that we might expect to be delayed in a child with poor vision.
■ He makes good eye contact with his caregivers, suggesting he can see them well.

o Hearing impairment

■ He has fine motor delay.
■ He is responsive to people around him.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present.

o Vision impairment: He will fail the vision test.
o Hearing impairment: He will fail the hearing test.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

o Global developmental delay.
o Aggravating factors may include lack of interaction with other children and poor language environment at home, and excessive screen time.
o Still need to rule out coexisting hearing impairment.
o Still need to rule out coexisting visual impairment.

- Is there any complication?

o No

- Is there any red flag symptom?

o The speech and fine motor red flags are present in this 3-year-old child (refer to the milestone charts).

- What is your plan for this patient?

o Formal vision and hearing tests.
o Advise the family to reduce screen time and to provide a language-rich home environment. Also consider enrolling the child in school or playgroup to increase his language exposure and opportunities for play with other children.
o Access early intervention as his delays are significant.
o Review of his development after these measures to determine how he progresses.
o Offer referral to a geneticist as this is the first child and GDD is present.
o Offer health advice to mother before her next conception.
