# Diarrhea

Sajini Mary Varughese • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF DIARRHEA

- Diarrhea is defined as the passage of loose or watery stools.
- Diarrhea can also be defined

- In relative terms, e.g., more frequently than normal.
- In absolute terms, e.g., more than three times a day.

- Occasionally, diarrhea is defined as stool output of >10 g/kg/day in infants and young children or >200 g/day in the adolescent as in the adult.
- Acute diarrhea is defined as diarrhea that lasts no longer than 14 days.
- Chronic or persistent diarrhea is defined as diarrhea that lasts longer than 14 days.

# SYMPTOMS OF DIARRHEA

- The common symptoms of diarrhea are watery and/or frequent stools.
- This may be associated with blood in the stools, mucus in the stools, or a different odor from the usual.
- There may be symptoms related to the following complications:

- Dehydration (see Chapter 19)
- Sepsis (see Chapter 9 Blood Pressure (Low), Chapter 28 Fever (Acute))
- Low blood glucose (see Chapter 11)
- Electrolyte imbalances (altered mental state, lethargy)

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- The basis of all diarrhea is the failure of normal net absorption of water and electrolytes from the intestines, resulting in net loss of water and electrolytes in the stools.
- Such a derangement can be due to one or more of these factors:

- An osmotic force that acts in the lumen to drive water into the gut
- An active secretory state induced in the enterocytes
- Mucosal invasion/inflammation of the intestine
- A gut motility disorder

# 1. Acute Diarrhea

- Common causes

- Intestinal infections

- Viral: rotavirus, norovirus, adenovirus, etc.
- Bacterial: Salmonella (nontyphoid), Shigella, Campylobacter, Clostridium difficile, enterotoxigenic Escherichia coli, cholera, etc.
- Parasitic: giardia, amoeba, etc.

222

- Nonintestinal infections, e.g., urinary tract infection, sepsis
- Surgical conditions, e.g., appendicitis
- Medication related, e.g., antibiotics, laxatives, poisons
- Malabsorption, e.g., lactose intolerance
- First/early presentation of chronic diarrhea (see below for the causes)

# • Manifestations

- Large volume watery stools without any blood generally point toward a small intestine enteritis. This is typically caused by viruses (rotavirus, norovirus, etc.) and enterotoxigenic bacteria (enterotoxigenic E. coli, cholera, etc.). There is often accompanying fever, vomiting, and abdominal pain.
- Frequent small volume mucoid, bloody stools (also known as dysentery) with/without tenesmus likely indicate bacterial enteritis. Common organisms include Shigella, Salmonella, Campylobacter, C. difficile, toxin-producing E. coli.
- Severe abdominal pain and/or distension and the presence of projectile or bilious vomiting would point toward a surgical cause and must be evaluated urgently.
- Diarrhea that is aggravated by certain foods, e.g., lactose-containing products, is suggestive of a food intolerance/malabsorption (which may be related to genetics or secondary to recent gastrointestinal insult).
- Important complications to evaluate for include dehydration, electrolyte disturbances, hypoglycemia, sepsis, and hemolytic uremic syndrome (especially with enterotoxigenic E. coli and Shigella).

# 2. Chronic Diarrhea

# • Common causes

- Physiological: toddler's diarrhea, where the child has a good appetite and appropriate weight gain despite the presence of food particles in the stool
- Spurious diarrhea: loose stools due to chronic constipation
- Intestinal infections: parasites, e.g., giardia, amoeba
- Medication related: antibiotics, laxatives, chemotherapy
- Increased gut motility: irritable bowel syndrome, hyperthyroidism
- Malabsorption: lactose intolerance (genetics or post-gastrointestinal insult), fructose intolerance, pancreatic insufficiency, fat malabsorption
- Allergic/inflammatory: cow's milk and soy protein intolerance, eosinophilic gastroenteritis, inflammatory bowel disease (IBD), protein-losing enteropathy, celiac disease
- Immunodeficiency, e.g., IgA deficiency, human immunodeficiency virus, secondary causes of immunodeficiency
- Anatomic: short gut syndrome, blind loop syndrome, intestinal lymphangiectasia
- Tumors: secretory VIPoma, neuroblastoma, gut lymphoma

# • Manifestations

- Blood in the stool may indicate an infective, inflammatory, or allergic enteropathy.
- Bulky, greasy stools usually indicate fat malabsorption; may be caused by pancreatic insufficiency or cholestatic liver disease.
- Watery nonbloody stools may be

- Due to osmotic diarrhea (e.g., lactose intolerance, fructose intolerance). Osmotic diarrhea will stop when the patient is fasted.
- Due to secretory diarrhea (e.g. cholera). Secretory diarrhea will persist when the patient is fasted.
- Motility related, e.g., irritable bowel syndrome, hyperthyroidism.

- The presence of flatus, explosive stools and buttock excoriation suggests carbohydrate malabsorption.
- Dietary triggers suggest food intolerances or malabsorption or irritable bowel syndrome.
- Pertinent family history of inherited malabsorption syndromes such as celiac disease may point toward the diagnosis.
- Important complications to evaluate for include dehydration, electrolyte disturbances, hypoglycemia, sepsis, failure to thrive, nutritional deficiency, developmental delay, and other extragastrointestinal tract manifestations.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Is There Really Diarrhea?

- What is the normal stool pattern like for this child?
- How often is the current stool output? Does it vary depending on food intake?
- What is the volume of the current stool produced? Does it vary depending on food intake?
- What is the current stool like?

- Watery?
- Mucoid?
- Bloody?

- The blood may appear bright red (fresh) or dark brown.
- Is the blood mixed with the stool? Blood mixed with stool is suggestive of enterocolitis (from dysentery, IBD, protein allergy) or bowel ischemia (from, e.g., intussusception).
- Is the blood coating the stool? This is more suggestive of fissures or hemorrhoids.

- Has pus? (suggestive of dysentery, IBD)
- Oily? (suggestive of fat malabsorption)
- Floats in the toilet? (suggestive of fat malabsorption)

# 2. Is It Acute or Chronic Diarrhea?

- When did the diarrhea start and how long has it been going on?

- Less than 2 weeks = acute
- More than 2 weeks duration = chronic

• Were there similar episodes in the past?

o If there are, take a history of each episode in order to determine if each episode is a self-limiting disease or if each episode is a recurrence/persistence of the same illness.

### 3. If It Is Acute Diarrhea

- Determine the likely cause and possible complications from thorough history and physical examination.
- History taking: Determine the likely cause.

o Ask for the associated symptoms.
o Potential associated symptoms include

■ Fever.
■ Vomiting. If there is vomiting, is it bilious or bloody?
■ Abdominal pain. If there is abdominal pain, what is the nature of the abdominal pain? Colicky (intestinal in nature)? Constant (peritonitis, surgical causes, bowel ischemia)? How severe is the pain?
■ Contact history with someone similarly affected
■ A history of contact with other ill individuals, child care attendance, recent travel with food indiscretion, and swimming in contaminated water suggests an intestinal infection.
■ Are there other symptoms or signs of a parenteral infection such as urinary tract infection, pneumonia, etc.?

o Hand hygiene history.
o Food hygiene history.
o Diet history: assess if there is any relationship between a food intake and diarrhea, e.g., lactose.
o Recent use of antibiotic and drugs use may give clues that the diarrhea is related to these agents.
o Travel history.
o Consider if there is a need to isolate and/or institute infectious control measures.

• History taking: Assess if there is any complication

o Sepsis, hemodynamic instability

■ Symptoms: skin mottling, cold clammy peripheries, feeling unwell, feeling weak, lethargic, dizziness, altered conscious state, reduced urine output
■ See Chapter 9 Blood Pressure (Low)

o Dehydration

■ Symptoms: thirsty, reduced urine output, weight loss, altered conscious state
■ See Chapter 19 Dehydration

o Hypoglycemia

■ Symptoms: lethargic, altered conscious status, seizure, hunger, weakness, tremor, anxiety, palpitations, headache, confusion
■ See Chapter 11 Blood Sugar (Low)

○ Electrolyte imbalance

• Physical examination

○ Vital signs

■ Fever (infection, inflammation)
■ Heart rate (increased in dehydration, shock, fever, pain, anxious)
■ Respiration rate (increased in severe dehydration, shock, acidosis)
■ Blood pressure (low in severe dehydration, shock)
■ SpO₂ (low in shock)
■ Capillary refill (prolonged in severe dehydration, shock)
■ Growth parameters (head size, weight, height, body mass index)
■ General status
■ Conscious status (altered conscious status seen in hypoglycemia, severe dehydration, shock)
■ Weight loss
■ Thirsty (sign of dehydration)

○ Hydration status (see Chapter 19 Dehydration)

○ Abdomen

■ Guarding and rebound tenderness suggest a surgical abdomen.
■ Tenderness suggests inflammation and surgical abdomen.
■ Mass suggests surgical abdomen and impacted fecal mass.
■ Hyperactive bowel sounds = increased motility.
■ Sluggish bowel sounds and absent bowel sounds = ileus

○ Look at the groin

○ Look at the buttock and anus

■ Excoriation
■ Fissure
■ Mass
■ Fistula, ulcer

○ Head-to-toe examination

○ Stool inspection

# 4. If It Is Chronic Diarrhea

• Determine the likely cause and possible complications from thorough history and physical examination.
• History taking: Determine the likely cause.

○ Ask for the associated symptoms.

○ Potential associated symptoms include

■ Fever.
■ Vomiting. If there is vomiting, is it bilious or bloody?
■ Abdominal pain. If there is abdominal pain, what is the nature of the abdominal pain? Colicky (intestinal in nature)? Constant

(peritonitis, surgical causes, bowel ischemia)? How severe is the pain?

- Contact history with someone similarly affected.
- Other symptoms or signs of a parenteral infection such as UTI, pneumonia, etc.
- History of chronic constipation with presence of impacted stools
- Any aggravating or alleviating factor?

○ Hand hygiene history.

○ Food hygiene history.

○ Diet history: assess if there is any relationship between a food intake and diarrhea, e.g., lactose.

○ Travel history.

○ Carry out a systems review

- Eye
- Joints
- Skin
- Anal lumps, ulcers
- Oral ulcers
- Is there a history of recurrent infections suggestive of immunodeficiency? (See Chapter 32 Frequent Infections)

○ Ask for family history of similar symptoms, disease, e.g., Inflammatory bowel disease, irritable bowel syndrome, cystic fibrosis, celiac disease

• History taking to assess for complications

○ Hemodynamic instability

- Symptoms: skin mottling, cold clammy peripheries, feeling unwell, feeling weak, lethargic, dizziness, altered conscious state, reduced urine output
- See Chapter 9

○ Dehydration

- Symptoms: thirsty, reduced urine output, weight loss, altered conscious state
- See Chapter 19

○ Hypoglycemia

- Symptoms (lethargic, altered conscious status, seizure, hunger, weakness, tremor, anxiety, palpitations, headache, confusion)
- See Chapter 11

○ Electrolyte imbalance

○ Failure to thrive

○ Macro and micronutrient deficiency

- Iron deficiency
- Folate/vitamin B12 deficiency

- Zinc deficiency
- Protein deficiency

○ Developmental delay

• Physical examination

○ Vital signs

- Fever (infection, inflammation)
- HR (increased in dehydration, shock, fever, pain, anxious)
- RR (increased in severe dehydration, shock, acidosis)
- BP (low in severe dehydration, shock)
- SpO₂ (low in shock)
- Capillary refill (prolonged in severe dehydration, shock)

○ Growth parameters (head size, weight, height, body mass index)

- Growth failure suggests a significant underlying cause.

○ General status

- Conscious status (altered conscious status seen in hypoglycemia, severe dehydration, shock)
- Weight loss
- Muscle bulk
- Thirsty (sign of dehydration)
- Pallor (anemia of chronic illness, iron deficiency, folate/vitamin B12 deficiency)
- Peripheral edema (suggestive of hypoalbuminemia due to protein malabsorption, protein-losing enteropathy, or protein malnutrition)

○ Hydration status

- See Chapter 19

○ Oral

- Ulcers

○ Abdomen

- Guarding and rebound tenderness suggest a surgical abdomen.
- Tenderness suggests inflammation and surgical abdomen.
- Mass suggests surgical abdomen, tumor.
- Hyperactive bowel sounds = increased motility.
- Sluggish bowel sounds and absent bowel sounds = ileus.

○ Look at the groin

○ Look at the buttock and anus

- Excoriation
- Fissure
- Mass, fistula, ulcer

○ Head-to-toe examination, especially

- Joints
- Skin
- Development

○ Stool inspection

1

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Young age < 6 months with dehydration, severe diarrhea
- Hemodynamic instability
- High fever
- Very unwell, toxic looking
- Lethargy, altered mental state
- Seizures
- Inability to retain, failed oral rehydration solution (ORS) trial
- Vomiting of bile or blood
- Severe abdominal pain
- Presence of abdominal distension, tenderness, guarding or mass
- Suspected surgical condition
- Presence of blood in the stools in the absence of perianal excoriation or fissure
- Severe dehydration
- Oliguria, anuria
- Failure to thrive
- Immunodeficiency, immunosuppressed
- Recent surgery
- Caregiver cannot provide adequate care at home

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 3-month-old baby girl, who has been fully breastfed since birth, presents with a 1-month history of frequent loose stools with bright red blood specks in her stools. She has a normal perinatal history and her parents are nonconsanguineous. Her mother has a history of atopic eczema in infancy and childhood asthma. Two weeks prior to her current consultation, she was diagnosed as having bad infantile colic by her family doctor due to her fussiness and "gassiness" and was started on wind drops. She has been otherwise feeding well and gaining weight well. There has been no fever or vomiting. Physical examination reveals a young infant who is on the 75th percentile for height and weight. There is a mild eczematous rash over her cheeks and neck. Her abdomen is soft and not distended, and the bowel sounds are normal. No masses are felt in the abdomen. There are no perianal fissures or excoriation. Respiratory, cardiac, and neurological examinations are normal.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- A young infant with chronic diarrhea and blood in the stools

- What is your diagnosis?

- Cow's milk protein allergy

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- The infant is thriving.
- There has been no fever or vomiting throughout this period.
- She has skin manifestations of atopy as well.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Typical history would include exposure of the baby to cow's milk infant formula. However, in some totally breastfed babies, the cow's milk protein exposure is through mother's diet. It would therefore be important to specifically ask about cow's milk intake (in any form) in the breastfeeding mother.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- The mother's dietary history is not provided. If she takes significant dairy products in her diet, this could explain the baby's symptoms.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Inflammatory bowel disease

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Has chronic diarrhea and blood in the stools

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Very rare for infants in this age group to get inflammatory bowel disease

- Features of atopy

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Some may have extra-gastrointestinal manifestations like fever and arthritis.
- Some may have a positive family history of IBD.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Cow's milk protein allergy

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- None but if left untreated, complications may set in.

- What is your plan for this patient?

- The baby's mother should be encouraged to continue breastfeeding and avoiding all milk and milk products in her own diet.
- If severe and unresponsive to the above management, consider the use of an elemental milk formula.

# Dysuria

Perry Y.W. Lau • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF DYSURIA

Dysuria is a burning, tingling, stinging, or painful sensation that occurs when passing urine.

# SYMPTOMS OF DYSURIA

- Burning, tingling, stinging, or painful sensation when passing urine.
- The location of the abnormal sensation can be at the urethra and/or external genitalia.
- Often associated with other symptoms such as frequency, urgency, incontinence, and/or refusal to void.
- Need to distinguish this from bladder discomfort. Bladder discomfort is felt at the suprapubic area, and bladder discomfort usually worsens as bladder volume increases.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Due to conditions affecting mainly the renal and urological structures (see Table 22-1)

  - Inflammatory conditions

    - Infection affecting the urogenital tract can cause inflammation of the urothelium of bladder and urethra, stimulating the sensory nerves and resulting in pain.
    - Inflammation from adjacent abdominal organs (e.g., intestine) can also affect function and sensation in the bladder.
    - Chemical irritants

  - Noninflammatory conditions

    - Biological irritants
    - Chemical irritants
    - Trauma
    - Anatomical obstructions

- Due to conditions affecting mainly the external genitalia (see Table 22-2)

  - Inflammatory conditions

    - Infection
    - Noninfectious skin conditions, e.g., autoimmune or inflammatory skin conditions
    - Chemical irritants

  - Noninflammatory conditions

    - Chemical irritants
    - Trauma
    - Anatomical obstructions

231

TABLE 22-1

DYSURIA DUE TO CAUSES AFFECTING MAINLY THE RENAL AND UROLOGICAL STRUCTURES

|   | Gender | Causes | Urine Analysis (Urine Dipstick and Microscopy)  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Type of Inflammatory Condition  |   |   |   |
|  Infection | Both | Bacterial cystitis, which may be complicated by pyelonephritis Bacterial urethritis (sexually transmitted infections) Urethritis associated with reactive arthritis | Pyuria May have hematuria Culture positive  |
|   |  Both | Viral cystitis Viral urethritis | Pyuria May have hematuria Culture negative  |
|  Inflammation from adjacent abdomi- nal organs | Both | Appendicitis | Pyuria culture negative  |
|  Chemical irritants | Both | Bubble bath, harsh soap, detergent, topical medication | Pyuria Culture negative  |
|  Type of Noninflammatory Condition  |   |   |   |
|  Biological irritants | Both | Hypercalciuria Gross hematuria (secondary to hemorrhagic cystitis, urolithiasis, trauma, tumor) | Hematuria  |
|  Chemical irritants | Both | Bubble bath, harsh soap, detergent, topical medication | Usually normal  |
|  Trauma (accidental/ non-accidental/ iatrogenic) | Both | Genitourinary instrumentation or surgery Foreign body (e.g., stent) Pelvic irradiation Sexual activity/abuse | Usually normal; may have some hematuria  |
|  Anatomical narrowing or obstruction | Both | Neurogenic bladder Urethral stricture Primary bladder dysfunction | Usually normal  |
|   |  Male Female | Meatal stenosis, posterior urethral valve Urethral prolapse (in prepubertal girl), urethral diverticulum | Usually normal Usually normal  |

TABLE 22-2

DYSURIA DUE TO CAUSES AFFECTING MAINLY THE EXTERNAL GENITALIA

|   | Gender | Causes  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Type of Inflammatory Condition  |   |   |
|  Infection | Male | Balanitis Epididymo-orchitis Sexually transmitted infection (STI)  |
|   |  Female | Vulvovaginitis (STI, candidiasis)  |
|  Noninfectious skin conditions | Both | Lichen sclerosus, psoriasis, Stevens–Johnson syndrome, Behcet syndrome  |
|  Chemical irritants | Both | Bubble bath, harsh soap, detergent, topical medication  |
|  Type of Noninflammatory Condition  |   |   |
|  Chemical irritants | Both | Bubble bath, harsh soap, detergent, topical medication  |
|  Trauma (accidental/non-accidental/iatrogenic) | Both | Genitourinary instrumentation or surgery Sexual activity/abuse  |
|  Anatomical narrowing or obstruction | Male | Meatal stenosis, phimosis, paraphimosis  |
|   |  Female | Labial adhesions  |

### CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

- Take a good history, conduct a physical examination, including examination of the external genitalia, and look at the urinalysis results.

### 1. History: Is It Really Dysuria

- Is it a burning/tingling/stinging/painful sensation that comes on with voiding/urination?
- If it is dysuria, find out the timing, persistence, severity, duration, and exact location of the dysuria.

  - Does dysuria occur at the start of urination (indicating urethral or external genitalia pathology)?

  - Does dysuria occur at the end of urination (indicating bladder pathology usually)?

- What are the other associated symptoms and signs?

  - Frequency or urgency? (See point 2 below.)
  - Gross hematuria? (See point 3 below.)
  - Symptoms of abnormal voiding pattern such as weak stream, post-void dribbling, sensation of incomplete voiding, urgency, urge incontinence, pelvic holding maneuvers? (See point 4 below.)
  - Symptoms of abnormal external genitalia? (See point 5 below.)
  - Previous abnormal urine analysis results? (See point 6 below.)

### 2. Patient Has Dysuria Associated with Frequency or Urgency

- Presence of these symptoms indicate urethral and/or bladder irritation.
- Ask for the associated symptoms.

- Conduct a head-to-toe examination, including examining the external genitalia

- Possible findings include

- Associated with foul-smelling urine is suggestive of urinary tract infection.
- Associated with suprapubic pain is suggestive of cystitis.
- Associated fever, flank pain, and costovertebral angle tenderness is suggestive of pyelonephritis.

- Associated with being sexually active is suggestive of sexually transmitted infections (STIs). Look for discharge.

- Associated with conjunctivitis and arthritis of large joint: Think of urethritis associated with reactive arthritis. Reactive arthritis is an autoimmune condition that develops in response to an infection, e.g., Salmonella, Shigella, Campylobacter, or Chlamydia trachomatis.

- Associated with fever and lower abdominal pain with guarding at lower abdomen: Think of appendicitis.

- Associated with the use of bubble bath, harsh soap, detergent, topical medication: Think of chemical irritation.

- Associated with recent genitourinary surgery or instrumentation: Predisposes to infection, inflammation, or pain due to trauma.

- Associated with pelvic irradiation.

- Associated with sexual activity (need to consider sexual abuse in children).

- Associated with cola or red urine or clots may be suggestive of gross hematuria. Look for the cause (see the next section).

- Note that conditions that cause microscopic hematuria may not be associated with change in urine color.
- Calcium microcrystals in the urine (hypercalciuria) can cause injury to urothelium, resulting in dysuria and hematuria.

### 3. Patient Has Dysuria Associated with Gross Hematuria

- Conditions that cause gross hematuria include renal stones, hemorrhagic cystitis (infective or noninfective causes), and renal tumor that is bleeding.

- Ask for the associated symptoms.

- Conduct a head-to-toe examination, including examining the external genitalia.

- Possible findings include

- Association with loin-to-groin pain is suggestive of urolithiasis.
- A past history and/or family history of urolithiasis is suggestive of urolithiasis.
- Association with recent history of trauma is suggestive of trauma causing bleed.
- Association with an abdominal mass on palpation. Need to exclude a renal mass/tumor.
- If it is hemorrhagic cystitis, look for the cause that can include

- Viral cystitis, e.g., adenovirus (associated conjunctivitis)
- Bacterial cystitis
- Pelvic irradiation

- Drugs
- Thrombocytopenia

### 4. Patient Has Dysuria Associated with Symptoms of Abnormal Voiding Pattern

- Symptoms of abnormal voiding pattern include weak stream, post-micturition dribbling, sensation of incomplete voiding, urge incontinence, urine holding maneuvers.
- If any of these symptoms and signs are present, suspect bladder outlet obstruction.
- Ask for the associated symptoms.
- Conduct a head-to-toe examination, including examining the external genitalia.
- Examine especially to see if the bladder is palpable.
- Examine for causes of bladder outlet obstruction.

- Neurogenic bladder

- Neurospinal dysraphism (myelomeningocele, lipomeningocele, tethered cord)
- Diastematomyelia
- Sacral agenesis
- Cerebral palsy
- Spinal trauma
- Spinal tumors

- Other anatomical causes of bladder outlet obstruction

- A history of genitourinary surgery or instrumentation is a predisposition to bladder outlet obstruction.
- Suspect urethral stricture if there is a history of urethral catheterization.
- In boys, suspect meatal stenosis or posterior urethral valve.
- In girls, suspect urethral diverticulum.

- Functional cause of bladder outlet obstruction

- Primary bladder neck dysfunction (incomplete opening of the bladder neck during voiding)

### 5. Patient Has Dysuria Associated with Signs and Symptoms of Abnormal External Genitalia

- Ask for the associated symptoms.
- Conduct a head-to-toe examination, including examination of the external genitalia.
- In a male:

- Associated with balanitis (infection, chemical irritant, inflammation)
- Associated with penile discharge (suspect STI, urethritis)
- Associated with phimosis or paraphimosis
- Associated with skin lesions at glans penis (chemical irritation or skin diseases, e.g., lichen sclerosus)
- Associated with skin sores/ulcers at glans penis (suspect herpes, syphilis, chancroid)

- Associated with tender testes/epididymis on palpation (suspect epididymo-orchitis)
- Associated with use of bubble bath, harsh soap, detergent, topical medication
- Associated with genitourinary surgery or instrumentation
- Associated with sexual activity

- In a female:

- Associated with vaginal irritation and/or discharge (suggestive of vulvovaginitis)
- Associated with introital erythema (indicates vulvovaginitis)
- Associated with anal pruritus that worsens at night (may indicate pinworms causing vulvovaginitis)
- Associated with purulent vaginal discharge (suspect STIs, candidiasis, sexual abuse)
- Associated with skin lesions at vulva (can be due to chemical irritation or skin diseases)
- Associated with labial adhesions
- Associated with the use of bubble bath, harsh soap, detergent, topical medication
- Associated with genitourinary surgery or instrumentation
- Associated with sexual activity

# 6. What Is the Urinalysis Result?

- Urinalysis includes urine dipstick and microscopic examination.
- Urinalysis is the most useful test in a patient with dysuria as it helps to narrow down the differential diagnosis.
- The possible differential diagnoses of dysuria based on urinalysis are listed in Table 22-1. (Also see Chapter 3 Abnormal Urine Dipstick Test.)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Dysuria with pyuria plus

- Presence of systemic features (fever, lethargy, vomiting) and signs (flank pain, costovertebral angle tenderness) suggests pyelonephritis coexisting with cystitis.
- Presence of systemic features (fever, lethargy, vomiting) and tenderness or guarding at right iliac fossa/lower abdomen may suggest appendicitis.
- Symptoms and signs of hypovolemia/shock.

- Dysuria with hematuria plus

- Clots in the urine. This indicates significant bleeding into the urinary tract. Assess for symptoms and signs of anemia and hypovolemia due to bleeding.

- Dysuria with urinary retention plus

- Presence of signs and symptoms of bladder outlet obstruction (weak urinary stream, post-micturition dribbling, palpable bladder)

1

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 7-year-old boy presents with the problem of burning sensation in his urethra during voiding for 1 day. He mentions that it hurts to void and takes a longer time to void because of this burning sensation. This is associated with cloudiness of urine. On further questioning, the boy mentions that this burning sensation has happened four times over the last 3 months. This burning sensation usually clears by the next few urinations. Urinary stream is normal. At times, the urine looks slightly brown in color. Mother thinks that there is no increase in urinary frequency or urgency. There is no fever, flank pain, or penile discharge. There is no history of trauma to his genitals. He has no major medical history of note and is not taking any medication. He has been growing and developing appropriately. He still wets his bed every night and has never been dry at night. He has no daytime urinary incontinence. Family history is unknown because he is an adopted son.

On examination, his temperature is 36.8°C, heart rate 82/min, respiratory rate 16/min, and blood pressure 100/59 mmHg. His height and weight are on the 50th percentile. He is not pale. There is no periorbital or lower limb edema.

Auscultation reveals normal heart sounds and vesicular breath sounds. The abdomen is soft and nontender. There is no mass felt. Bowel sounds are normal. His genitalia are normal.

A urine dipstick is done. The results are as follows: leukocytes trace, nitrite negative, urobilinogen negative, protein trace, hemoglobin 3+, ketone negative, bilirubin negative, and glucose negative.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

○ Dysuria with cloudy urine and possible hematuria (dipstick finding)

• What is your diagnosis?

○ Hypercalciuria

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

○ Cloudy urine can be due to the presence of excessive calcium crystals in urine.

○ Dysuria is due to calcium crystals irritating the urothelium.

○ Absence of pyuria.

○ Presence of hematuria (needs urine microscopic examination to differentiate between hematuria and hemoglobinuria).

○ There is absence of other symptoms and signs to suggest acute glomerulonephritis (hypertension, hepatomegaly, edema) as the cause of hematuria.

○ Recurrent dysuria that resolves spontaneously without antibiotic treatment. Dysuria in hypercalciuria can resolve with increased fluid intake.

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

○ Nocturnal enuresis. This can be a separate problem. There are no other abnormal voiding symptoms such as daytime incontinence or poor urinary stream. Longer time to void occurs with the burning sensation but does not happen when there is no burning sensation.

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

○ Associated recurrent flank or abdominal pain in child with renal calculi

○ History of passage of urinary calculus or sediment

○ A family history of urolithiasis

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

○ Cystitis or urethritis

• For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

○ Presence of cloudy urine.

○ Presence of hematuria (needs confirmation with urine microscopic examination).

○ Cystitis is the most common cause for dysuria in children. It can be caused by bacteria or viruses.

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

○ Recurrent dysuria that resolves spontaneously without antibiotic treatment

○ Absence of pyuria (leukocyte trace on dipstick)

○ Absence of recent urinary tract procedure, e.g., catheterization (risk factor for cystitis or urethritis)

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

○ No information provided on any associated conjunctivitis, rhinitis, cough, and sore throat. Suspect adenovirus infection if these symptoms are present. Adenovirus can cause hemorrhagic cystitis.

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Hypercalciuria

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ No red flag.

○ There is no flank to groin pain to suggest urolithiasis associated with hypercalciuria.

- There is no fever and flank pain to suggest pyelonephritis.
- There is no fever or abdominal symptoms and signs to suggest acute appendicitis.
- There is no gross hematuria with clots in the urine.

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Carry out urine microscopy and phase contrast to confirm the presence of hematuria and to determine if the red blood cells are isomorphic or dysmorphic. (In hypercalciuria, red blood cells in urine are mainly isomorphic.)
- Conduct urine culture.
- Analyze the urine calcium/creatinine ratio.
- If urine calcium/creatinine ratio is elevated, consider check serum creatinine, electrolytes, bicarbonate, calcium, phosphate, and magnesium. Kidney ultrasound may be required to look for nephrocalcinosis or urolithiasis. If detected, a specialist referral for full nephrocalcinosis or urolithiasis workup is required. High fluid intake is recommended.

# Edema

Ng K.H. • Yap H.K.

# DEFINITION OF EDEMA

- Edema is a physical sign that occurs when there is an accumulation of fluid in the interstitial space.
- Edema can affect external areas and/or internal organs.
- Edema that affects the external areas are more easily seen by the eye, e.g., periorbital edema, limb edema, and sacral edema.
- Edema affecting the internal organs are less easy to see from the outside e.g:

- Cerebral edema
- Pulmonary edema
- Macular edema

- In this chapter, the term edema is used to describe edema that affects external areas.

# SYMPTOMS AND SIGNS OF EDEMA

# Symptoms

- Swelling, e.g., periorbital swelling, abdominal distension, leg swelling, ankle swelling.
- Swelling that is localized to only part of the body, or swelling that affects many parts of the body (generalized).
- Swelling that is worse in the dependent areas, and the symptoms may worsen as the day progresses.
- Feeling tight, tense, or warm.
- Feeling dull ache or pain.
- Inappropriate rapid increase in weight if there is a lot of swelling.
- Patient may notice that the skin/swelling has indentation (pitting edema).

# Signs

- Pitting edema:

- Swelling that will show indentation/pitting when pressure is applied on the swollen area against a bony surface, e.g., pressing on the tissue of a swollen ankle against the medial malleolus.
- Severity of edema may be described based on the depth of skin indentation and the extent/distribution of the edema.
- The location and extent may change when the position of the patient changes (i.e., it may be more obvious in the dependent positions).
- The location and extent of the swelling help to determine the cause.

240

• Nonpitting edema:

○ Swelling that will not show indentation/pitting after pressure is applied on the swollen area against a bony surface.
○ Swelling may be detected by the inability to tent the skin (e.g., Kaposi-Stemmer sign, where there is inability to tent the skin on the dorsum of the second toe using a pincer grasp).
○ The location and extent of the swelling help to determine the cause.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY OF PITTING EDEMA AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

• Pitting edema occurs when there is excess interstitial fluid, and when the fluid is of low viscosity and low in protein.
• Pitting edema can be

○ Localized to a specific region of the body (e.g., one limb only, face only).
○ Generalized i.e., affecting the whole body or multiple different sites of the body

• The pathophysiology of localized pitting edema

○ Increased hydrostatic pressure within the blood vessels due to increased capillary pressure/venous congestion; e.g.

■ venous thrombosis (acute, chronic)
■ compression of vein by an extraluminal mass

○ Increased capillary permeability; e.g.

■ type I hypersensitivity reaction, angioedema, anaphylaxis
■ infection such as cellulitis, abscess
■ inflammation such as insect bite, contact dermatitis
■ trauma such as burns, bruise

■ Manifestations

□ Has predisposing factors to thrombosis, veins distended
□ Skin changes associated with infection and inflammation (erythema, warmth, tenderness)
□ Evidence of insect bite and other injuries
□ Acute onset of lip swelling, periorbital swelling, and urticaria (angioedema, anaphylaxis)
□ Relevant trigger history, e.g., topical medication, NSAIDs, food

• The pathophysiology of generalized pitting edema

○ Increased hydrostatic pressure within the blood vessels due to increased plasma volume

■ For example, renal failure, nephritic syndrome, congestive cardiac failure
■ Manifestations

□ Signs of intravascular fluid overload, e.g., raised jugular venous pressure, turgid hepatomegaly, peripheral edema, gallop heart rhythm, lung crepitations, hypertension
□ Decreased urine output in nephritic syndrome or renal failure

☐ Gross hematuria in nephritic syndrome
☐ Displaced apex beat in chronic congestive cardiac failure

• Reduced colloidal or oncotic pressure within blood vessels, i.e., hypoalbuminemia.

■ For example, nephrotic syndrome, liver failure, protein losing enteropathy, severe malnutrition
■ Manifestations

☐ Evidence of third spacing, e.g., periorbital edema, pleural effusion, ascites, limb edema

☐ Other signs are dependent on the underlying cause, e.g., liver failure, malnutrition, diarrhea

• Increased capillary permeability

■ For example, due to sepsis
■ Manifestations: Associated skin changes seen in sepsis, e.g., flushing, warmth

• Other mechanisms

■ Premenstrual
■ Drug induced (vasodilators, calcium channel blockers)

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY OF NONPITTING EDEMA AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

• Nonpitting edema occurs when there is excess interstitial fluid, and when the fluid is of high viscosity, e.g., rich in protein. Sclerotic skin changes also add to the "nonpitting" nature of the skin.

• Causes

• Lymphedema

■ Primary obstruction of lymph flow in the lymphatic system
☐ For example, congenital lymphedema (Milroy disease)
☐ Manifestations: presents at or shortly after birth. More likely to be generalized than localized; the lower limbs are the sites most often affected. If chronic, there may be associated skin changes (fibrotic, thickened verrucous skin).

■ Secondary obstruction of lymph flow in the lymphatic system

☐ For example, lymph node or mass compressing on or blocking lymphatic vessels.
☐ Manifestations: usually not present at birth. More likely to be localized; can have associated skin changes if chronic (fibrotic, thickened verrucous skin); may have evidence of the underlying cause.

■ Secondary to destruction of the lymphatic system

☐ For example, due to

- Lymph node dissection/ surgical removal
- Radiotherapy
- Trauma
- Filariasis

1

☐ Manifestations: usually not present at birth; more likely to be localized; can have associated skin changes if chronic (fibrotic, thickened verrucous skin); may have evidence of the underlying cause.

# Myxedema

- Localized to pretibial: Graves' disease
- Generalized: hypothyroidism
- Manifestations

  - Dry, thick skin
  - Yellow to orange skin discoloration
  - Peau d'orange appearance in Graves' disease

Continued on next page

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

Continued from previous page

![img-31.jpeg](img-31.jpeg)

"A good history and physical examination are needed in order to determine the cause of the swelling."

### 1. Is There Swelling? Is the Swelling Pitting or Nonpitting Edema?

- Determine if there is swelling.
- If there is swelling, determine if it is due to edema as opposed to another cause e.g. a lump.
- If it is due to edema, determine if it is pitting or nonpitting.

- Look to see if there is skin indentation after pressure is applied on the swelling against a bony surface.

- Attempt to pinch a skin fold, classically on the dorsal surface of the toes (usually second toe: Kaposi-Stemmer sign) or fingers. If a skin fold cannot be lifted, then there is nonpitting edema.

- Note: Nonpitting edema generally suggests lymphedema and myxedema. However, pitting edema may occur in the early stages of lymphedema before fibrosis occurs in the subcutaneous tissue.

### 2. Pitting Edema: Is the Distribution of the Pitting Edema Generalized or Localized?

- Is the pitting edema unilateral or bilateral?

- Unilateral = more likely localized.
- Bilateral = more likely generalized.
- If the edema appears to be more severe on one side, check if it is because the edema was dependent on the position of the patient.

- Does the pitting edema involve both upper and/or lower limbs?

- Pitting edema involving upper limbs only

  - This is usually seen in localized edema.

- Pitting edema involving lower limbs only

  - This happens in the early stages of generalized edema as the edema is noticeable only in the most dependent parts of the body.

  - This also happens when there is localized edema.

- Pitting edema involving upper limbs and lower limbs = generalized edema.

- Does the pitting edema involve only the face?

- If it is only the face

  - This can be due to localized edema.

  - It can also be due to mild generalized edema that has gravitated to the face after a night of sleeping.

### 3. If the Pitting Edema Is Generalized

- If the pitting edema appears generalized, assess the intravascular fluid status (overload, normal or low)

- Intravascular fluid overload = increased plasma volume (renal failure, nephritic or nephritic-nephrotic syndrome, congestive cardiac failure)

  - Look for evidence for renal cause

    - Decreased urine output?
    - Hematuria? Proteinuria? Hypoalbuminemia?

☐ History or signs of renal conditions or renal failure?
☐ Recent or concurrent infections?
☐ Rash, joint pain or swelling, mouth ulcers?
☐ Family history of autoimmune or renal disease?

■ Look for evidence of cardiac conditions or cardiac failure.

○ Intravascular fluid volume normal or low, plus third spacing (e.g., ascites, pleural effusion) = hypoalbuminemia (nephrotic syndrome, liver failure, protein-losing enteropathy, severe malnutrition)

■ Look for the underlying cause

☐ Renal: Decreased urine output? Frothy urine? Nephrotic-range proteinuria? Family history of nephrotic syndrome or renal disease?
☐ History or signs of liver failure?
☐ Chronic diarrhea?
☐ Malnutrition or poor nutritional intake?

○ Intravascular fluid volume normal or low, plus evidence of sepsis (e.g., flushing, fever, wide pulse pressure) = sepsis

○ Intravascular fluid volume normal

■ Taking any medication that may cause edema?
■ Is edema cyclical and coincides with menstrual cycle?

### 4. If the Pitting Edema Is Localized

• Are there overlying skin changes?

○ Sclerosis, hyperpigmentation: chronic venous congestion (chronic deep vein thrombosis, chronic venous compression by an extra luminal mass)

○ Skin erythema and warmth

■ Acute deep vein thrombosis, compartment syndrome
■ Type 1 hypersensitivity reaction (angioedema, anaphylaxis)
■ Infection (cellulitis, abscess) (note: cellulitis may be a cause or complication of generalized edema)
■ Inflammation (contact dermatitis, insect bite)
■ Trauma (burns, bruise)

○ No skin changes:

■ Deep vein thrombosis, compartment syndrome
■ Type 1 hypersensitivity reaction (angioedema, anaphylaxis)

• Look for evidence for the underlying cause.

### 5. If the Edema Is Nonpitting

• Consider lymphedema or myxedema.
• Determine the cause
• Are there reasons for possible obstruction or destruction of lymphatics?

○ Lymph node dissection/surgical removal
○ Radiotherapy
○ Tumor
○ Trauma

- Is the child from a tropical area endemic for filariasis?
- Lymphadenopathy, prior lymphangitis suggesting filariasis?

- If there is no known cause for secondary lymphedema, look for signs and symptoms of hyperthyroidism of hypothyroidism.

- If no

  - Consider hereditary or primary lymphedema. Primary lymphedema usually affects lower limbs and may be asymmetrical.

- If there is hypothyroidism, consider that the swelling is due to myxedema. Severe myxedema may be indistinguishable from lymphedema.
- If there is hyperthyroidism, consider myxedema due to Graves' disease.
- If the location is pretibial or if there is peau d'orange appearance, consider myxedema due to Graves' disease.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Involvement of the scrotal area – at risk for cellulitis and subsequent necrotizing fasciitis.
- Rapid onset and/or severe pain – this may suggest an acute life-threatening cause such as compartment syndrome, acute deep vein thrombosis, angioedema, infection.
- The presence of the following requires prompt escalation, evaluation, and management:

- Decreased intravascular fluid volume
- Intravascular fluid overload
- Cardiac failure
- Respiratory distress
- Hypertension
- Tachycardia and/or hypotension
- Abdominal pain or tenderness in the presence of ascites (spontaneous bacterial peritonitis)
- Abdominal pain or tenderness in a patient with nephrotic syndrome and ascites (spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, severe intravascular fluid depletion, renal vein thrombosis)

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 7-year-old boy presents to you for the problem of bilateral puffy eyes over the last 2 weeks. These are noticeably worse in the mornings and have been progressively worse. Over the last three days, he has also developed bilateral ankle swelling, progressive abdominal distension, and a mild cough. Today, he develops vague generalized abdominal pain. There is no fever, urinary frequency, or urgency.

He has no major medical history of note. He has been growing and developing appropriately. He attends school and is not taking any medication, including traditional medications. There is no skin rash, joint pains, or mouth ulcers. The remaining review of systems is negative.

1

On examination, his temperature is 36.2°C, heart rate 90/min, respiratory rate 15/min, blood pressure 98/58 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 99%. His height is 118 cm (25th percentile). His weight was 20 kg two weeks ago (25th percentile). His current weight is 24 kg. He is alert and not pale or jaundiced. Pupils are equal and reactive. There is significant bilateral periorbital edema. His throat, nose, and ears are normal. His neck is supple. His jugular venous pressure is normal. Capillary refill is 2 seconds. Bilateral pitting edema is noted in both ankles up to mid-thigh, worse on the left compared to right. Heart rate is regular. Apex beat is not displaced. On auscultation, S1 S2 are heard. There are no additional heart sounds. On auscultation of the lungs, there are absent breath sounds in the right lower zone, associated with stony dullness on percussion. The abdomen is markedly distended and the umbilicus is everted. It is nontender to palpation. A soft liver is felt 2 cm below the right costal margin. Shifting dullness is elicited. Bowel sounds are normal. His scrotum is enlarged, and it illuminates with light. There is mild tenderness and skin erythema over the bottom of the scrotum. No skin rashes or joint involvement is noted. Musculoskeletal examination is normal. Neurological examination is normal.

A urine dipstick is done. The results are as follows: leukocytes 1+, nitrite negative, urobilinogen negative, protein 4+, blood 1+, ketone negative, bilirubin negative, and glucose negative.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Generalized pitting edema

- What is your diagnosis?

- Hypoalbuminemia, due to primary nephrotic syndrome (most likely minimal change disease)

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Evidence for generalized pitting edema = bilateral periorbital edema, bilateral pedal pitting edema, weight gain of 4 kg over 2 weeks
- Evidence of fluid in body cavities/third spaces, namely ascites (shifting dullness) and pleural effusion (localized absent breath sounds with stony dullness)
- Absence of signs suggesting raised intravascular fluid: normal jugular venous pressure, nondisplaced apex beat, no gallop heart rhythm, no lung crepitations, nonturgid liver
- Presence of massive proteinuria on urine dipstick suggestive of nephrotic syndrome
- No evidence for secondary nephrotic syndrome: no rash or joint pains or mouth ulcers (lupus nephritis or other vasculitis); no history of chronic hepatitis B infection (secondary membranous nephropathy)
- Typical age for onset of minimal change disease (2–10 years old)
- Absence of clinical features suggestive of malnutrition, liver, or cardiac disease

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Urine dipstick showed leukocytes 1+ and blood 1+. But note that false-positive results are common for urine dipstick. This should be further evaluated by urine microscopy.
- Liver 2 cm (but liver may be pushed down if he has a pleural effusion).

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information provided on urine amount and color (expected to be decreased and more concentrated, but not brown or red)
- No information about pitting edema in sacral region

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Nephritic syndrome

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Has generalized swelling that is due to pitting edema.
- Liver is enlarged.
- Has pleural effusion and ascites.
- Has blood 1+ in the urine as well as proteinuria.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Not hypertensive.
- Intravascular volume is not increased (blood pressure not high, jugular venous pressure not increased, liver is soft and not turgid).

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Gross hematuria

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Hypoalbuminemia, due to primary nephrotic syndrome (most likely minimal change disease)

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Symptoms and signs of pleural effusion (mild cough, absent breath sounds, and stony dullness in right lower zone) but not in overt respiratory distress (normal respiratory rate and oxygen saturation)

• Signs of scrotal edema (enlarged, transillumination with light) and possible associated cellulitis (mild tenderness and skin erythema)

# • What is your plan for this patient?

• Investigations

- Urine protein/creatinine ratio to confirm and to quantify the proteinuria. In children who are continent in both day and night, a 24-hour urine total protein should also be performed. In the case of incomplete urine collection, it is a good practice to include 24-hour urine creatinine as well, so that the protein/creatinine ratio can also be calculated from the 24-hour urine sample.
- Urine microscopy to confirm the absence of pyuria and red cell casts. In nephrotic syndrome, a few red blood cells in urine may be present.
- Serum electrolytes, urea, and creatinine to determine the renal function and look for complications such as uremia and electrolyte abnormalities. Dilutional hyponatremia may be present. In cases where intravascular fluid is severely depleted, urea and creatinine may be raised.
- Serum albumin and liver enzymes to confirm hypoalbuminemia and the absence of transaminitis (liver disease).
- Tests to determine the absence of secondary nephrotic syndrome: serum complement levels, anti-double-stranded DNA, and hepatitis B surface antigen.

• Management

- Indications for albumin: scrotal edema and pleural effusion. Intravenous (IV) 20% albumin 1 g/kg over 4–6 hours, with IV furosemide 0.5–1 mg/kg mid-way and end of albumin infusion.
- Start oral prednisolone 60 mg/m² every morning, up to maximum of 60–80 mg in adults.
- Salt should be restricted when there is edema, but fluid may be restricted only if negative fluid balance cannot be achieved persistently despite albumin and furosemide. Severe fluid restriction may increase thrombotic risks in the frank nephrotic state.
- Antibiotics may be started if there is cellulitis.
- Referral to nephrologist.
- Take a good family history looking for kidney diseases or hearing loss

- Some forms of primary nephrotic syndrome may be hereditary and occur due to gene mutations in the podocytes or glomerular basement membranes.
- Some of these conditions are associated with hearing loss.
- In nephrotic syndrome due to genetic mutations, the use of immunosuppression should be minimal and judicious.

- Monitoring

- Put on strict input/output charting and daily weight and urine dipstick.
- Monitor blood pressure and heart rate regularly.
- Look out for other complications of nephrotic syndrome: spontaneous bacterial peritonitis and venous thrombosis.

- Education

- Educate parents on nephrotic syndrome and home monitoring of urine dipstick.

☐ Refer to the dietician. The diet should comprise of calories and protein within daily recommended intake, and low in salt, refined sugars, and saturated fat.
☐ Review vaccination records and educate parents on contraindications to live vaccines while on prednisolone.
☐ Live vaccines are contraindicated when immunosuppressive treatment is started. If he is not immune to varicella, then precautions need to be taken to avoid contact with people with active varicella infection. And if exposed to varicella infection, prophylaxis treatment with VZIg may be indicated.

# Enlarged Liver and/or Spleen

Quak Seng Hock • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF ENLARGED LIVER AND/OR SPLEEN

- Hepatomegaly is present when the size of the liver is bigger than expected. In normal circumstances, the liver is situated at the right hypochondrium, with the upper border at the right fifth intercostal space at the mid-clavicular line. In infants and young children, a palpable liver of 0.5–1 cm below the subcostal margin is acceptable. The lower border should not be palpable in older children.
- Splenomegaly refers to when the size of the spleen is larger than normal. A normal spleen is not palpable or percussible. It is at least three times its normal size when it is just palpable below the left costal margin.
- Hepatosplenomegaly refers to when both the liver and the spleen are noted to be enlarged simultaneously.

# SYMPTOMS OF ENLARGED LIVER AND/OR SPLEEN

- Most patients with enlargement of the liver and spleen do not have any symptom from the enlargement. This finding is detected only after examination of the abdomen.
- Abdominal distension may be the presenting complaint if the enlargement is significant.
- Patient may present with symptoms related to the etiology, e.g., jaundice, fever.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Pseudohepatomegaly and Pseudohepatosplenomegaly

- Not all palpable livers and spleens in children are due to true organomegaly.
- The most common cause of a "hepatomegaly" or "hepatosplenomegaly" is the ptosis of the organs due to hyperinflation of the lungs. These are also known as pseudohepatomegaly and pseudohepatosplenomegaly, respectively.
- To differentiate between apparent and true organ enlargement, locate the upper border of the liver by percussion. The upper border of the liver is at the normal position when there is true hepatomegaly. In pseudohepatomegaly, the upper border of the liver is displaced downwards/inferiorly.

# 2. Isolated Hepatomegaly

- In normal healthy infants, it is normal to find a small palpable liver of <1 cm below the right costal margin.

253

• In the older child, the liver is usually not palpable or just palpable below the right costal margin.

# • Causes:

# ○ Infection

- Intrauterine infection
- Hepatitis A, B, C, D, etc. (acute infection, chronic infection)
- Other viral infections, e.g., Cytomegalovirus (CMV), Epstein–Barr virus (EBV)
- Bacterial infections: cholangitis, septicemia, tuberculosis, typhoid
- Parasitic infections: malaria, hydatid disease, schistosomiasis, etc.
- Liver abscess: bacterial or parasitic (usually amoebiasis)

# ○ Inflammatory

- Autoimmune hepatitis
- Chronic active hepatitis
- Inflammatory bowel disease-associated liver disease
- Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)
- Systemic juvenile idiopathic arthritis (SJIA)

# ○ Drug-related hepatitis

○ Cirrhosis (see the splenomegaly section for causes of cirrhosis)

# ○ Structural

- Biliary atresia
- Choledochal cyst
- Intrahepatic biliary hypoplasia
- Congenital hepatic fibrosis
- Polycystic disease
- Tumors/lymph node obstructing biliary tree

# ○ Hematologic

- Chronic hemolytic anemias: thalassemia, sickle cell disease
- Oncologic: leukemia, lymphoma

# ○ Tumor/hamartoma

- Benign, e.g., hemangioma, liver cyst
- Malignant, e.g., hepatoblastoma

# ○ Infiltrative

- Histiocytosis X, sarcoidosis

# ○ Storage and or metabolic

- Fatty liver/nonalcoholic liver disease; may be associated with obesity, diabetes mellitus, Cushing syndrome, hypopituitarism
- Inborn errors of metabolism, e.g., glycogen storage disease, mucopolysaccharidoses, Wilson disease, alpha-1-antitrypsin disease, etc.

# ○ Congestion

- Congestive heart failure, especially right heart failure
- Budd–Chiari syndrome
- Constrictive pericarditis

# ○ Normal variant (Riedel lobe)

### 3. Hepatosplenomegaly

Causes:

- Infection

- Intrauterine infection
- Viral, e.g., CMV, EBV
- Bacterial, e.g., sepsis, typhoid, infective endocarditis
- Parasite, e.g., kala azar, malaria
- Fungal

- Malignancy

- Leukemia
- Lymphoma

- Chronic active hepatitis (viral, autoimmune)

- Liver cirrhosis with portal hypertension (see the splenomegaly section for causes of cirrhosis)

- Chronic hemolytic anemia, e.g., thalassemia intermedia/major, sickle cell disease

- Inborn errors of metabolism

### 4. Isolated Splenomegaly

- Isolated splenomegaly occurs in two big groups of conditions: in those with evidence of chronic liver disease and in those with no apparent evidence of chronic liver disease.
- In those with chronic liver diseases, the liver may not be palpable due to an advance state of cirrhosis where the liver has contracted in size and is no longer palpable. The enlarged spleen is due to portal hypertension. In such cases, features of chronic liver diseases such as jaundice, ascites, palmar erythema, and finger clubbing are usually obvious.

Causes of cirrhosis include

- Prolonged biliary obstruction

- Biliary atresia
- Choledochal cyst
- Gallstones
- Bile duct stenosis
- Familial intrahepatic cholestasis
- Alagille syndrome

- Hepatotropic viral infections

- Hepatitis B
- Hepatitis D
- Hepatitis C
- Hepatitis E

- Inherited genetic–metabolic diseases
- Drugs and toxins
- Total parenteral nutrition
- Autoimmune hepatitis
- Congestion

- Budd-Chiari syndrome
- Veno-occlusive disease

- Others: fatty liver disease/nonalcoholic liver disease

- In those without chronic liver disease, splenic enlargement can be due to

- Viral infection, e.g., CMV, EBV
- Parasite infections, e.g., malaria, kala azar
- Portal vein thrombosis
- Lymphoma, leukemia, myelodysplasia
- Chronic hemolytic anemia, e.g., thalassemia major/intermedia, hereditary spherocytosis
- Inflammatory/autoimmune, e.g., SLE, SJIA
- Splenic cyst or hamartoma
- Some inborn errors of metabolism

# CLINICAL SCENARIO

A 10-year-old previously well girl is brought to see you. She has been having fever and sore throat for the last 5 days. This is associated with tiredness. There is no contact or travel history. She has been taking paracetamol for her fever. On examination, her temperature is 39.1°C, heart rate 108/min, respiratory rate 27/min, blood pressure 105/66 mmHg, and SpO₂ 98% on room air. She appears lethargic. There is no jaundice or pallor. Her pharynx is inflamed. Her tonsils are enlarged. There are several enlarged, tender cervical lymph nodes felt. Her liver is 3 cm below the right costal margin, and her spleen is palpable 2 cm below the left costal margin.

# Failure to Thrive

Michelle L.N. Tan • Denise L.M. Goh • Marion M. Aw

# DEFINITION OF FAILURE TO THRIVE

- The term failure to thrive (FTT) is often used synonymously with “faltering weight,” “weight faltering,” or “growth failure.” In this book, our preferred term is FTT.
- There is no consensus as to what defines FTT.
- Most commonly, FTT is used when there is failure to gain weight appropriately.
- This may be quantified in the following ways:

- Weight is less than 3rd percentile on the appropriate growth chart.
- Serial weight measurements show a decrease of two or more major percentile lines (90th, 75th, 50th, 25th, 10th, and 5th) on the appropriate growth chart, e.g., 75th to 25th.
- Velocity of weight gain is decreased and is disproportionate to growth in length.
- Rate of daily weight gain is less than expected for age (more useful for infants).

- There are two major patterns of FTT:

- One where the weight parameter is faltering but the height gain is maintained.
- One where the weight and height growth are faltering.

- Please note:

- Children growing along a curve with a normal serial growth rate, even if their weight is below the 3rd percentile, are not considered to have FTT.
- “Catch-down” growth is used to describe a phenomenon where the growth of normal children decreases, usually between 6 and 18 months of age, to lower growth percentile curves so as to match their genetic programming. Their subsequent growth then follows the new, lower percentile curve. The decrease of growth is usually ≤2 major percentile lines and the children have normal development, and behavioral and physical examinations.
- FTT is not used to describe children with genetic short stature, constitutional growth delay, and prematurity or intrauterine growth restriction who have appropriate weight-for-length and normal growth velocity.
- Children born preterm need to be plotted according to their corrected age for the first two years of their life.
- There are specific growth charts for children with specific genetic conditions, e.g., Down syndrome, Turner syndrome, Prader–Willi syndrome, achondroplasia. Use those growth charts to assess growth.

262

- For children whose height is faltering but weight is not, these children are usually regarded as having short stature (see Chapter 2 Abnormal Stature).
- For children whose head size is faltering but not weight and height, these children are usually regarded as having microcephaly or a neurological insult. This is not covered in this book.

# SYMPTOMS OF FAILURE TO THRIVE

- Lack of appropriate weight gain (e.g., weight gain has slowed, weight gain has stopped, weight is less than 3rd percentile on growth chart).
- Losing weight unintentionally.
- Younger siblings are growing faster than the patient.
- Patient is not growing as fast as the older siblings did.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# Major causes:

- Inadequate calories absorbed compared to metabolic needs

- Not enough food offered
- Condition that causes loss of appetite or food aversion
- Condition that affects the ability to swallow
- Condition that causes recurrent vomiting
- Condition that causes abnormal food transit/digestion/absorption
- Condition that causes increased metabolic demands

- Other conditions

- Other illnesses
- Familial

# 1. Inadequate Calories Absorbed Compared to Metabolic Demands

- Not enough food offered

- Difficulties with breastfeeding.
- Inappropriate milk formula dilution.
- Poor knowledge of a child's nutritional needs, e.g., intentional avoidance of high-calorie foods, early introduction (before 4 months), or delayed introduction of solids.
- Restricted diet (e.g., low fat, vegan).
- Parents are not able to adequately manage the nutritional intake of a child with small /limited appetite.
- Caregiver neglect (a form of non-accidental injury/child abuse).

- Condition that causes loss of appetite or food aversion

- Behavioral causes:

- "Picky eater" – child with small/limited appetite, selective food choices
- Sensory food aversions/ anticipatory gagging
- Post-traumatic feeding disorder (following NG tube feeding or force feeding)

• Organic causes:

■ Hypothyroidism
■ Malignancy
■ Autoimmune, inflammatory condition
■ Renal failure
■ Some types of inborn errors of metabolism (IEMs) (e.g., protein aversion in urea cycle defect, organic acidemias)
■ Many chronic illnesses
■ Conditions where eating results in abdominal pain

• Condition that affects ability to swallow

○ Abnormal craniofacial structures or esophagus, e.g., cleft lip/palate, Pierre–Robin sequence, choanal atresia
○ Swallowing dysfunction, e.g., cerebral palsy, muscular dystrophy, myopathy
○ Causes of respiratory distress

• Condition that causes recurrent vomiting

○ Gastroesophageal reflux disease
○ Hiatal hernia
○ Pyloric stenosis
○ Malrotation with intermittent volvulus
○ Raised intracranial pressure
○ Some IEMs

• Condition that causes abnormal food transit, abnormal digestion/absorption (abnormality in gastrointestinal structure, motility, or function)

○ Gastro-intestinal (GI) tract malformation, e.g., tracheo-esophageal fistula, hiatal hernia
○ Cow's milk protein allergy (CMPA)
○ Inflammatory bowel disease
○ Coeliac disease

• Condition that causes increased metabolic demand

○ Congestive cardiac failure
○ Chronic respiratory failure/distress
○ Hyperthyroidism
○ Recurrent/chronic infections
○ Many chronic illness
○ Increased activities/exercise

# 2. Other Conditions

• Other illness

○ Anemia
○ Malignancy
○ Other metabolic disorder, e.g., diabetes mellitus, diabetes insipidus, IEMs
○ Placental insufficiency, asymmetrical IUGR, SGA
○ Teratogens, e.g., intrauterine infections, alcohol

• Genetic conditions, e.g., chromosomal abnormalities, skeletal dysplasias

• Familial (genetically programmed to be small)

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

- Is there really FTT? When was the onset? What is the chronological pattern?
- What is the cause? Any complication(s)?
- What has been tried before and what was the outcome?
- Physical examination.
- Look for red flags.

# 1. Is There Really Failure to Thrive? When Was the Onset? What Is the Chronological Pattern?

• Obtain the child's growth parameters and plot them

• Accurately measure the child's current growth parameters (height, weight, head circumference).
• Obtain the child's previous growth parameters, preferably from birth onwards. (Look at the health booklet, previous medical records.)
• Plot the growth parameters on the appropriate growth chart (age, sex, corrected for prematurity, appropriate for disease condition, etc.).

• Determine and plot mid-parental height and target height range (see Chapter 2).
• Determine if there really is FTT (see if any of these features are met).

• Weight is less than the 3rd percentile on the appropriate growth chart.
• Serial weight measurements show a decrease of two or more major percentile lines (90th, 75th, 50th, 25th, 10th, and 5th) on the appropriate growth chart, e.g., 75th to 25th.
• Velocity of weight gain is decreased and is disproportionate to growth in length.
• Rate of daily weight gain is less than expected for age (more useful for infants).
• Height is less than target height range.

• Determine the onset
• Determine the pattern of FTT

• If it is weight only, the most common cause is inadequate caloric intake (but all the causes listed above are possible).
• If it affects both weight and height, it can all be the causes listed above (inadequate nutrition will lead to faltering in weight first, but in the long run can lead to faltering growth in height too).

# 2. What Is the Cause? Any Complications?

• Determine the caloric requirement of the child

• Determine the age-appropriate basal caloric requirements and if any additional calories are needed to meet metabolic needs or to enable catching up growth.

• Basal caloric requirements can be estimated by using dietary reference intake (DRI) for age per kg of body weight

|  Age (months) | Energy (kcal/kg/day)  |
| --- | --- |
|  0–3 | 102  |
|  3–6 | 82  |
|  6–12 | 80  |

|  Age (years) | Energy (kcal/kg/day)  |
| --- | --- |
|  1–3 | 82  |
|  3–5 | 77  |
|  5–7 | 72  |
|  7–9 | 67  |

• Take a history of the child's diet and determine the child's nutritional intake

• Take a detailed dietary history to determine the nutritional intake (calories, macronutrients, and micronutrients).
• Caloric intake can also be calculated from a 3- to 5-day food diary.
• Compare the caloric intake with the calories required.
• If the caloric intake is less than the calories required, the cause is inadequate caloric intake. Look for the cause (see the above table).

• Take a history of what the caregiver is actually doing with regard to meal/food/feed preparation.

• Take a history of the child's feeding and behavior during meals

• Any symptom(s) of feeding difficulties?

■ Small appetite
■ Refusal to feed/food aversion
■ Taking a long time to feed
■ Narrow food spectrum eaten
■ Swallowing difficulties/choking/gagging
■ Discomfort or pain with feeding
Vomiting
■ Abdominal pain
■ Noisy breathing

• See Chapters 26 and 27 if there are symptoms of feeding difficulties.

• Take a history to look for an underlying medical cause and complications

• Antenatal history, including in-utero growth, intrauterine infection, teratogenic exposures such as alcohol/illicit drugs, maternal food intake, maternal health, maternal disease.
• Gestational age at birth, APGAR scores.
• Growth parameters at birth.

• Ask for associated symptoms. Examples of possible symptoms include

- A significant illness that preceded or coincided with the onset of poor growth.
- Abnormal stool frequency and consistency, e.g., watery stool, loose stool, oily looking stool, bloody stools, mucoid stools.
- Association with the type of food eaten.
- Loss of consciousness, altered mental state.
- Recurrent infections, frequent infections.
- Features to suggest neglect (see Chapter 37 on injuries).
- Family history of respiratory and gastrointestinal disease, including cystic fibrosis, coeliac disease.
- Family history of cow's milk protein allergy/intolerance.
- Developmental delay, regression.

• Look for symptoms of nutritional deficiencies, dehydration.

• Do a systemic review.

### 3. Take a History about What Has Been Done and What the Outcome Was

- Because most of the causes are related to inadequate calorie absorbed to meet metabolic needs, figure out what has been done before. For example, was a trial of increased calories given and how did the child respond to it?
- Any investigation done before.

### 4. Physical Examination

• A head-to-toe examination is needed

• Nutritional and health status

  • Does the child appear sick, scrawny, irritable, or lethargic?
  • Any evidence of loss of muscle mass or subcutaneous fat stores? Look especially the upper arm, buttocks, and thighs.

- Dysmorphic features suggestive of a genetic/chromosomal disorder.
- Physical signs that may indicate risks for/presence of suck–swallow dysfunction: cleft lip/palate, drooling.
- Look for dehydration, pallor, and edema.
- Any feature of chronic illness.
- Look for signs of child abuse and neglect.
- Observe the child–parent interaction and a feed/meal.

LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Failure to gain weight despite adequate caloric intake
- Clinical features suggestive of the underlying medical condition
- Signs of child abuse, including psychological neglect
- Signs of dehydration
- Signs of malnutrition

# CLINICAL SCENARIO

Mrs Tan, a homemaker, took her only child, 18-month-old Sophie, to the family clinic for fever and running nose. You diagnosed an upper respiratory tract infection and were about to prescribe paracetamol when Mrs Tan mentioned her concern that Sophie was not gaining weight and looked skinny compared to other children. She asked you if there was anything seriously wrong with Sophie and requested vitamin supplements to boost her appetite.

Sophie was born full-term, with a birth weight of 2.8 kg. She has always been small sized but is an active child and hardly falls sick. Sophie is a fussy eater. She is not keen to try new foods, and eats only a limited variety, with rice, chicken, and cabbage being the foods she would eat most consistently. Mrs Tan and her husband are of average height with no family history of significant medical conditions and no financial difficulties.

Physical examination showed Sophie to be an alert and cheerful child who looked thin but there were no other features suggestive of any chronic disease or nutritional deficiency. She appeared to be interacting appropriately with her mother, and there was no suggestion of any neglect. Her weight was at the 25th percentile 6 months ago and is now at the 10th percentile. Her head circumference and height have otherwise been tracking at the 25th percentile.
