What is the clinical problem?
What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

What is the clinical problem?

- Failure to thrive (FTT)

What is your diagnosis?

- Selective feeding behavior resulting in inadequate caloric intake to match her energy expenditure.

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

Weight is crossing percentiles downwards.
Weight percentile is lower than the height and head circumference percentiles.
History suggestive of fussy eating.
No other signs or symptoms of an underlying medical/organic cause.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

Nil

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

Accurate parental heights not provided; target height range not calculated
Developmental assessment not provided
No information about what has been tried, e.g., dietary intervention
No information about parent-child interaction during meal times
No caloric count; selective intake of varieties of food may not mean inadequate calories
No information about newborn screening test results

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

Maybe genetically programmed to be small

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

Otherwise healthy child.
Weight pattern is consistent with catch-down growth pattern.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

None

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

Parental heights should have been obtained. Mid-parental height and target height range should have been calculated. Child's height should be within the target height range.
Developmental assessment should have been done. If the caloric deprivation is not too severe and not for too long, the child should have normal development.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

FTT due to selective feeding behavior resulting in inadequate caloric intake to match her energy expenditure.

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

None

- What is your plan for this patient?

Trial of increased caloric intake.
May need to see a dietitian for meal advice.
The child should then be reviewed to assess for weight gain. If her weight does not improve despite adequate caloric intake, investigations may be needed to look for a cause. If adequate caloric intake cannot be achieved, behavioral causes and medical causes should be looked for. Treatment is dependent on the cause.
She is at risk for iron and vitamin D deficiencies. She may need to be tested for that and supplemented accordingly.

# Feeding Difficulties or Eating Problems in the Older Child

Sajini Mary Varughese • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF FEEDING DIFFICULTY AND EATING PROBLEM

- Feeding difficulty refers to

- Any problem that negatively affects the process through which parents/caregivers provide food to their child.
- Any problem that negatively affects the process through which an individual accesses or consumes food.

- Eating problem refers to the problem where an individual is perceived to be not consuming a normal diet.

# SYMPTOMS OF FEEDING DIFFICULTY AND EATING PROBLEM

The symptoms can include

- Loss of appetite
- Refusal to feed
- Taking a long time to feed
- Becoming very secretive and irritable, especially about food or meals
- Not wanting to eat in public
- Dieting/skipping meals
- Consuming inadequate amount of food
- Taking inadequate calories
- Having a narrow food spectrum/food aversion
- Eating only a few certain "safe" foods, usually those low in fat and calories
- Ingestion of nonnutritive substances (eating cotton balls to fill up the stomach)
- Drinking a lot of water/fluids
- Adopting rigid meal or eating rituals, such as cutting food into tiny pieces or spitting food out after chewing
- Cooking elaborate meals for others but refusing to eat
- Binge eating
- Choking/gagging/swallowing difficulty
- Vomiting, regurgitation
- Feeling discomfort or pain with feeding
- Feeling abdominal pain with feeding
- Noisy breathing, stridor with feeding
- Recurrent pneumonias
- Failure to thrive
- Low body mass index (BMI)

270

- Loss of weight (>5% of body weight)
- Nutritional deficiencies
- Fear of weight gain
- Overweight

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Causes of feeding difficulties that affect the infant and young child can also affect the older child and the adolescent. These are discussed in Chapter 27 and are also listed below.
- Causes of eating problems include organic causes, behavioral causes as well as psychological causes.

# 1. Organic Causes of Feeding Difficulties/Eating Problems

- Condition that causes loss of appetite or food aversion

- Hypothyroidism
- Malignancy
- Autoimmune, inflammatory condition
- Renal failure
- Some types of inborn errors of metabolism (IEMs) (e.g., protein aversion in urea cycle defect, organic acidemias)
- Many chronic illnesses

- Condition that affects ability to swallow

- Abnormal craniofacial structures or esophagus, e.g., cleft lip/palate, Pierre-Robin sequence, choanal atresia
- Swallowing dysfunction, e.g., cerebral palsy, muscular dystrophy, myopathy

- Condition that causes of respiratory distress, e.g., heart failure, respiratory distress, laryngomalacia, glottic/subglottic stenosis

- Condition that causes recurrent vomiting

- Gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD)
- Hiatal hernia
- Malrotation with intermittent volvulus
- Raised intracranial pressure
- Some inborn errors of metabolism

- Condition that causes abdominal pain, abnormal transit, abnormal digestion/absorption (abnormality in gastrointestinal structure, motility, or function)

- Gastrointestinal tract malformation, e.g., tracheoesophageal fistula, hiatal hernia
- Gastroesophageal reflux (GER), GERD
- Gastritis
- Pancreatitis
- Gall stone disease
- Inflammatory bowel disease
- Coeliac disease

- Condition that affects normal dietary principles, e.g., restricting certain nutrients

- Liver failure diet, renal failure diet
- Some IEM diets
- Fluid restricted diet

## 2. Behavioral and Psychological Causes of Feeding Difficulties/Eating Problems

- Anorexia nervosa
- Bulimia nervosa
- Binge eating disorder
- Pica
- Rumination disorder
- Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder

- Food fixation (selective, extreme dietary limitation)

- Sensory food aversions/anticipatory gagging

- Post-traumatic feeding disorder (following nasogastric tube feeding, or force feeding)

### 3. More Information about Eating Disorders

Anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa, binge-eating disorder, pica, rumination disorder, and avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder are some of the examples of eating disorders. The exact etiology of eating disorders is unknown but may have multifactorial components:

- Biologic factors: genetic inheritance, increased estrogen during puberty, chronic illnesses such as Type 1 diabetes mellitus (DM).
- Psychological factors: personality traits such as perfectionism, sensitivity to rejection or teasing, low self-esteem, comorbid anxiety, depression, or obsessive-compulsive disorder.
- Environmental/social factors: family and societal demands for performance or appearance, stigmatization of obesity, sports that promote weight loss, or thinness such as ballet or cheerleading.

Although the prevalence of eating disorders is not very high (<5%), the mortality and suicide rates from this disorder are significant enough to warrant early recognition and referral to an appropriate health center for treatment.

- Anorexia nervosa (AN)

- There is an intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat; the individual is unwilling to maintain a normal or healthy weight due to the individual's distorted body image, despite being grossly undernourished.

- There are two types of AN:

- In the restrictive type, adolescents achieve weight loss by dieting and exercising excessively.
- In the binge-eating/purging type, weight loss is achieved by inducing vomiting or using laxatives or diuretics.

• Bulimia nervosa (BN)

o BN is characterized by recurrent binges (intake of large amounts of food in a short period of time in a way that feels out of control) along with compensatory mechanisms (such as self-induced vomiting, purging, food restriction, and exercise) to prevent weight gain.
o The individual is often at or above normal weight but still has undue concerns about his/her weight and shape.

• Binge-eating disorder (BED)

o BED involves recurrent episodes of binge eating without the compensatory behaviors seen in BN.
o The individual usually eats large amounts of food until he/she feels uncomfortably full and subsequently feels disgusted, guilty, or depressed.

• Pica

o Pica disorder is the recurring ingestion of nonnutritive substances, including mud, paper, cloth, hair, and numerous other substances, inappropriate to the child's developmental level.
o Pica can be a manifestation of an organic disease, e.g., iron deficiency.
o Pica may be comorbid with a number of mental disorders such as intellectual development disorder, autism spectrum disorder, schizophrenia, and obsessive-compulsive disorder.

• Rumination disorder

o The affected individual regurgitates partly digested food voluntarily with minimal effort, at times seeming to derive pleasure from the activity, and then either spit out, rechew, or swallow the regurgitated food.

• Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder (ARFID)

o ARFID comprises a variety of restrictive eating behaviors that do not involve a fear of weight gain or distorted cognitions but does lead to significant physical and emotional impairment.
o Examples include swallowing phobias and textural aversions.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

• Is there really a feeding difficulty or eating problem?
• What is the cause?
• What is the consequence?
• Is there any red flag?

# 1. History Taking: Is There Really a Feeding Difficulty or Eating Problem?

• Determine what the parents/patients mean by feeding difficulty or eating problem.
• See if any signs and symptoms listed in the earlier sections are present.

# 2. History Taking: What Is the Cause? What is the Consequence? Is there any red flag?

• Information about serial height and head circumference measurements/growth.

- Information about serial weight, weight loss/gain, BMI – time course, minimum and maximum.
- Take more history about the signs and symptoms of feeding difficulty or eating problem.

- Chronology of symptoms
- Associated symptoms (see Table 26-1 for possible causes based on symptoms)
- Other illness
- Any perceived trigger or preceding event

- Patient's view on their current feeding issue, eating issue, weight, and body image.

- Dietary history

- Current diet: quantity and type of food eaten, and food aversions.
- Meal times: length of meal times, mealtime routines, strategies used at mealtimes, environment and behavior around mealtimes.
- Patient's attitude and behavior around food.

- Determine if the current nutritional intake is adequate.

- Assess for consequences of malnutrition – e.g., pallor, fatigue, mood changes, syncope, hair loss, cold intolerance, sleep disturbances, and amenorrhea.
- Assess hydration status.
- Systems review looking for organic causes of feeding difficulty or eating problem
- Exercise history
- Psychosocial history – recent stressors, risk behaviors, comorbid mental illness such as major depression, anxiety disorder, obsessive compulsive disorder, cutting, family dynamics, other relevant relationships
- Family history – eating disorder in parents or siblings, other psychiatric illnesses
- See Table 26-1 for the possible causes of the symptoms.

### 3. Physical Examination: What Is the Cause? What is the Consequence? Is There Any Red Flag?

- A very thorough head-to-toe examination is required to answer these questions: What is the cause? What is the consequence? Is there any red flag?
- Anthropometry – height, weight, and BMI on age-appropriate charts. Try to obtain serial data points and review the trends.
- Vital signs – heart rate, respiratory rate, blood pressure and any orthostatic change, body temperature, oxygen saturation.
- Hydration status; if dehydrated, quantify the severity of the dehydration.
- Pallor
- Jaundice
- Sallow
- Skin/hair – alopecia, lanugo hair, acrocyanosis, calluses over the proximal interphalangeal joints from induced vomiting (Russell sign), carotenemia, petechiae, bruises.
- Orofacial/dentition – parotid enlargement, enamel erosion, inflamed throat.
- Cardiac – arrhythmia, mitral valve prolapse, heart failure.
- Pulmonary – pneumothorax, pneumomediastinum from forceful retching and vomiting, aspiration.
- Abdominal – palpable stool, mass, hepatomegaly, splenomegaly.
- Endocrine, bone – Tanner staging, rickets, fractures.
- Lymph node enlargement
- Central nervous system – muscle bulk and strength, reflexes.
- Psychiatry – mood, anxiety, depressed.

### 4. Observe of a Meal if Possible

- Observe for swallowing problems, choking, feeding behavior, behavior suggestive of eating disorder, etc.

![img-36.jpeg](img-36.jpeg)

5. Algorithm

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- <75% ideal body weight
- Refusal to eat
- Body fat <10% using skin fold callipers
- Heart rate <50/min daytime; <45/min night time
- Systolic blood pressure <90 mmHg
- Orthostatic changes in pulse (increase >35/min) or blood pressure (decrease >10 mmHg)
- Arrhythmia
- Syncope
- Hypothermia (<35°C)
- Suicide risk
- Intractable vomiting
- Hematemesis
- Electrolyte abnormalities

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 14-year-old girl was referred to the adolescent clinic for the evaluation of drastic weight loss over the past 4 months. This was picked up when she presented to the Children's Emergency 2 weeks prior after she had experienced several near-syncopal episodes in school. Her weight then was 43 kg, with a BMI of 18. Her highest weight was 58 kg about 8 months ago. At that point in time, the patient and her family embarked on changes in their lifestyle to achieve a healthier weight. Initially, her weight loss was gradual. In recent months, the weight loss was more drastic and accompanied by hair loss, fatigue, and cold intolerance. Menstruation had also become irregular, skipping 2 months in a row. On asking about her diet and exercise, her family noted that at times they would have great difficulty in ensuring that she ate her meals, at other times they would see her eating large amounts of food at one go. She had also enrolled in Zumba classes four times a week and would engage in daily runs of 4 km. When questioned, she denied any compensatory purging behavior. She had concerns that her abdomen was flabby and would like to lose more weight.

Physical examination showed that her height was 155 cm, weight 43 kg, and BMI 18. Her heart rate was 56/min and blood pressure 92/55 mmHg. She was a teary teen with cuts on both her wrists. There was bilateral parotid enlargement and the presence of dental caries as well. The rest of the physical examination was unremarkable. Blood investigations revealed a serum potassium level of 2.7 mmol/L, bicarbonate (tCO₂) of 35 mmol/L, and chloride of 110 mmol/L.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- A teenager with intentional weight loss and body image issues

- What is your diagnosis?

- Bulimia nervosa

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- There is significant weight loss.
- The teenager has alternating bingeing and restrictive eating patterns.
- She has ongoing body image issues.
- She has parotid enlargement and dental caries, which may indicate induced vomiting as a compensatory mechanism.
- There are cuts on her to suggest a possible psychosocial stressor that may be triggering the eating problem

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- If the weight loss continues to markedly low levels, this may be anorexia nervosa (bingeing/purging type) instead.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- A history of laxative use or prolonged periods of time spent in the toilet following meals.
- Russell sign, lanugo hair.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Anorexia nervosa (bingeing/purging type)

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Weight loss, bingeing and restrictive eating patterns, body image issues, enlargement of parotid gland

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Weight loss not sufficient to meet this diagnosis.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

○ Nil

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Bulimia nervosa

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ The electrolyte disturbances are worrying.

- She may be depressed and at risk of serious self-harm.

• What is your plan for this patient?

- Admit her to the hospital because of the electrolyte imbalances.

- Perform an ECG to look for rhythm changes as well as prolonged QTc.

- Correct the electrolyte abnormalities.

- Refer her to the mental health team and eating disorder team for evaluation and management.
